# Rules Primer

> **Rule set:** Written to NHL rules. Where IIHF, USA Hockey or typical rec-league rules
> differ in a way that changes how you play, it is flagged inline. **Playing in Britain? The
> IIHF flags are yours** — every level of UK hockey runs the IIHF book, amended locally; see
> [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md), and read **"If you play in Britain"** in the Overview
> before you take any NHL statement below as describing your game.
>
> **Related:** [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md) · [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md) · [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) · [Faceoffs](../systems/faceoffs.md) · [Special Teams](../systems/special_teams.md) · [Game Management](../systems/game_management.md) · [How to Watch Hockey](../off-the-ice/how_to_watch_hockey.md)

## Overview

Find out which rulebook your league plays under before your first game, because the books disagree, and one of the disagreements can cost you the rest of the match. **And a penalty can cost you games beyond tonight in every one of the four, each on its own trigger** — a first game misconduct under USA Hockey 404(b), a second in the same category within 41 regular-season games under NHL 23.6 for a physical foul or 23.5 for a stick foul, a third major in a season under USA Hockey 411, a **game misconduct** in the last ten minutes, in overtime or after the final whistle under Hockey Canada 4.8(c), and the IIHF's supplementary discipline at the Proper Authorities' discretion (Rule 28.1).

**A hit to the head is the rule to learn from the harshest book rather than the most watched one.** The NHL is the outlier and it is the lenient one: Rule 48.3 says *"There is no provision for a major penalty for this rule."* Everywhere else it can end your game. **IIHF** Rule 48 covers the head *or neck*, opens *"There is no clean check to the head or neck"*, and reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct — you are gone for the rest of the match — for reckless endangerment (48.3). **USA Hockey** Rule 620 allows a minor and a misconduct *or* a major and a game misconduct, and makes the harsher one compulsory for reckless endangerment — **and for contact that is merely careless** (620(b)). **Hockey Canada** Rule 7.6 opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck."* If you play in Britain, the lenient version is not yours. **That protection is not unconditional, though**, and the exception is the one that should change how you skate: IIHF 48.1 also says a player who bodychecks an opponent *"who is skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player and does not use an upward motion or drive their body up into the opponent, shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'."* The checker is still obliged not to hit your head, and the IIHF book attaches no penalty of its own to the player whose head was down. **Hockey Canada writes a wider exception, in junior and senior divisions only, and on a different trigger — ducking to avoid a check rather than carrying the puck head down.** Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) provides that a player who *"ducks to avoid a check"* and is hit in the head area with *"an otherwise legal check"* draws no Head Contact penalty against the checker, and that the referee *"may assess a penalty to the player who ducks to avoid a check"* under Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 — Clipping. **A minor under 8.7(a) is the floor there, not the ceiling:** 8.7(b) makes a major plus a game misconduct **mandatory** if the duck injures the checker and allows one at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence even if nobody is hurt, and 8.7(c) **makes a match penalty mandatory** for attempting to injure as well as injuring deliberately — and 8.7's preamble states the prohibition flatly: *"Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked"*. That Hockey Canada interpretation *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, and no equivalent exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules or in Hockey Canada's rules for minor and female hockey. ⚠️ **And the crouching prohibition is not only Hockey Canada's — the IIHF writes one too, in the British reader's own book.** IIHF Rule 44.1 says a player *"may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them"* — narrower than Hockey Canada's flat prohibition, because it wants the boards **and** the checker going over you, but real: IIHF 44.2 is a minor on the degree of violence and IIHF 44.3 reaches a major plus an **automatic game misconduct** for reckless endangerment. Neither the NHL nor USA Hockey writes any crouching clause at all. Keep yours up anyway — and **under Hockey Canada, ducking out of a check is the version that can put *you* in the box in any division**, because Rule 8.7's prohibition on crouching to avoid a bodycheck carries no division scope; near the boards it can under the IIHF book too. Head up — **chin off your chest** — is not the same as standing tall into the hit. Take it on a wide base with the knees bent, and **near the wall get your skates parallel to it** and take the contact along your side — **your forearm and hip meet the wall, your shoulder and hip turn toward the checker** — never the point of **your** shoulder, never your back to the boards, and never ducking. Neither of those first two is penalised in any book; avoiding them is how you avoid a spinal injury. Ducking is both penalised and dangerous — penalised under Hockey Canada, whose Rule 8.7 prohibition on crouching to avoid a check **carries no division scope at all**, and under the IIHF near the boards. (What *is* confined to junior and senior is the **checker's** amnesty, Interpretation 3 to 7.7(a).) See [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) for how to take one.

**Checking from behind is the one hit with no small version. Three of the four books eject you at every tier they write, and the fourth — USA Hockey — ejects you too for a forceful check along the boards or a reckless one anywhere.** A check on a player who cannot see it coming and cannot protect himself, with contact on the back of the body, has **no minor option at all** under the **NHL** (Rule 43.2: *"There is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"*) or the **IIHF** (43.2: *"This infraction is considered serious and dangerous, therefore there is no option to award a minor penalty"*) — it is a major plus an automatic game misconduct. **Hockey Canada** ejects you from its smallest version upward: Rule 7.5(a) is *"a Minor penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty"*, and 7.5(e) makes that game misconduct compulsory for a minor and a major alike. ⚠️ **That is a *game* misconduct, and Hockey Canada writes no plain misconduct here at all** — 7.5(d) says *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for a checking from behind infraction."* Where the books do pair a minor with a plain misconduct it is a different rule and a different tier: Hockey Canada 7.7(a) does it for head contact **in junior and senior divisions only**, and below that 7.6(d) forbids it — *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for a Head Contact infraction."* **How often either pair is assessed in play is not something the rulebooks state.** **USA Hockey is the only one of the four that lets you finish the game** — Rule 608(a) permits a minor plus a misconduct — and even there 608(b) makes the major and game misconduct compulsory where the check *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame, as a result of checking from behind."* ⚠️ **And USA Hockey's own Casebook takes that lighter option off the hit this section is about.** Its Rule 608, Situation 1 confines the minor plus misconduct to an open-ice push on an opponent *"skating ahead of them and not near the boards"* and to *"minimal body contact"* making a *"'pinch' against the boards"*, and requires the major plus a game misconduct or the match penalty — *"In every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"*, and *"In every instance where the player recklessly endangers the opponent from a check from behind, regardless of whether or not board contact is made."* **So what separates the four books here is the tier they start at, not what happens when you finish a check into somebody's back.** **Two of the four books put the onus on the checker in terms — USA Hockey Rule 608 and Hockey Canada's Rule 7.5; the NHL's Rule 43 and the IIHF's state no onus at all.** But "he turned at the last second" is not a defence under any of them, because the waiver the NHL and the IIHF do write (Rule 43.1 in each) is for a *deliberate* turn to draw the contact, which is a different thing.

**In recreational hockey almost every penalty is a stick foul, and almost every stick foul is a conditioning failure.** Hooking, slashing and tripping dominate, and they are the product of being beaten and reaching. Stick on the puck, never on the player. *(That ordering is a reading of the rulebooks against how adult recreational hockey is officiated, not a published count of calls.)*

**A first major can end your game, and that is true in both books.** IIHF Rule 20.4 attaches an automatic game misconduct to a major for any infraction on its Reference Table 6 — twenty of them, and it requires no injury for any. **That table number is the 2025/26 book's**: the 2026/27 edition merges its Tables 5, 6 and 7 into a single Table 5 that still carries all twenty. The NHL's Table 5 is neither the same list nor unconditional, and its residual trigger is a **third** major in a game where the IIHF's is a **second** — or a second misconduct, which the NHL book does not have at all.

**Offside and icing are worth learning as constraints rather than as definitions**, because between them they decide where you are allowed to be and where you can safely send the puck. Under NHL and IIHF rules an icing costs the offending team three things at once: the restart happens in their own end, they may not substitute tired players, and they may not take a timeout. **USA Hockey imposes neither of the last two.** The mechanics differ book to book in ways that change the act itself — what works in all four is getting the puck itself past the centre red line, on your stick, before you send it away.

Every rule below is quoted by rule number from the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and IHUK In-House rulebooks listed in the sources. Where they disagree, that is said rather than smoothed over.

### If you play in Britain, the IIHF column is yours

This document is written to NHL rules, and the rule-set flags in it are mostly written for a North American reader. **If you play anywhere in Britain, the flag to follow is the IIHF one.**

Every level of the game here runs on the **IIHF Official Rule Book**: the Elite Ice Hockey League, the NIHL, the recreational and junior game across the Home Countries, university hockey, and the GB national teams. The In-House Rules say so in terms. Their **2025-26** edition had England Ice Hockey, the Scottish Ice Hockey Association and the British Universities Ice Hockey Association *"adopt the IIHF Official Rule Book 2025–2026 (Version 1.1, published July 2025) as the foundation for the rules of the game to be applied in England and Scotland."* Their **2026-27** edition, published 25 July 2026, restates that for a reorganised governing body: **Ice Hockey UK (IHUK)** and the BUIHA *"adopt the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025) as the foundation for the rules of the game to be applied in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"* — the coverage is wider, and EIH and SIH policies are now IHUK's.

⚠️ **Do not read that sentence as telling you which IIHF edition you are on, because the same document contradicts it.** Its cover page reads *"to be read and applied in conjunction with the **Current** IIHF Rule Book"*, where the 2025-26 cover named *"the 2025–26 IIHF Rule Book"* — but the Introduction quoted above still names Version 1.1 of July 2025, which **is** the 2025/26 book; the IIHF's 2026/27 book gives its own version as 1.0, June 2026. **So the In-House Rules are evidence of what they amend, not evidence of which IIHF edition sits underneath them.** Where an edition difference matters in this document it is flagged at the point of use. The two IIHF editions diverge sharply in three places that touch these pages — the **face-off** rules, the **high-sticking** definition, and the numbering of **Rule 46** and of the Appendix tables — and only the first of the three is settled for Britain by the In-House Rules' own text rather than by resolving this question. If your league's answer matters to you, ask it.

On top of that base sits one thin layer of local amendment: the **In-House Rules**, which the 2026-27 edition applies across all four Home Countries and university hockey, or the **EIHL Casebook** in the Elite League. The In-House Rules are not a rulebook and say so — *"This document is to be read alongside the IIHF Rule Book and is not intended to serve as a standalone rule book"* — but they are the *"overriding interpretation of how the IIHF Rule Book is to be applied domestically"*, so where they amend the IIHF book, they win.

**What that changes about reading this document.** Wherever the text below says *"under IIHF rules"*, it is describing your game, not a footnote to somebody else's. Wherever it flags a **USA Hockey** or **Hockey Canada** divergence, that flag is not yours. The comparison table in [section 10](#10-rule-set-differences-that-actually-matter) carries a **Home Countries** column for exactly this reason, and it is yours wherever in Britain you play: the In-House Rules govern *"England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"* and university hockey, not England alone. Where that column reads *"As IIHF"*, that is the answer and not an omission: it means the In-House Rules carry no amendment to that rule, so the IIHF cell beside it is your rule.

**[UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md) has the full list of England Ice Hockey amendments.** Read it before your first game — several of the amendments (neck protection, facial protection, rink size, junior age categories) change things this document does not cover. ⚠️ **Read the junior age categories out of the current season's In-House Rules and nowhere else.** Every band shifted by a full year between the 2025-26 and 2026-27 editions — Under 19 went from birth years 2007–2009 to 2008–2010, and each band below it moved with it — so a table copied from a previous season puts a player in the wrong category.

---

## 1. How a Game Is Structured

### The teams on the ice

Each team plays **five skaters and one goaltender** at even strength. A skater is any outfield player — the three forwards (a **centre** and two **wingers**) and the two **defencemen**, sometimes called defenders or D. The goaltender, or goalie, is the player permitted to use special equipment and to catch and hold the puck.

An NHL roster for a game is **20 players — 18 skaters and 2 goalkeepers** (NHL Rule 5.1). Rec teams are usually smaller; two lines of forwards, two pairs of defence and a goalie is a common minimum, and it makes for a very long night.

### When a rule says "a player", does that include the goaltender?

Most of the rules below say *"a player"* and never mention the goalie, and you will sooner or later need to know whether one of them binds yours. **Three of the four books answer that in their own text; USA Hockey does not answer it at all**, and the difference is worth carrying into everything that follows.

**The NHL and the IIHF give the same answer under the same rule number.** NHL Rule 5.1 and IIHF Rule 5.1 carry the same sentence, the IIHF capitalising the term it is defining and the NHL not: *"For the purposes of these playing rules, any reference to 'Player' shall refer to both skaters and goalkeepers. Any reference to 'goalkeeper' shall mean that the section of the rule is specific to goalkeepers."* So a bare *"player"* takes in your goaltender, and a rule that names the goalkeeper is telling you it is theirs alone. **That is also why the IIHF book says "skater" in places where the NHL says "player":** Rule 5.1 splits *"Player"* into *"skaters and goalkeepers"*, which leaves the IIHF a word for everybody who is not the goalie, and it uses it. Rule 67.5 is the two books side by side — the NHL's *"If a player, when the goalkeeper has been replaced for an extra attacker, falls on the puck…"* is the IIHF's *"If a skater…"*, the rest of the sentence differing only in a pronoun, a comma and an article.

**Hockey Canada arrives at the same place from its Glossary rather than from a rule.** The entry reads *"PLAYERS: The members of the team physically participating in the game. The goaltender is considered a player, except where explicitly stated because special rules or privileges apply to them."* Silence covers your goaltender there too, and an exclusion has to be written down for it to exist.

**USA Hockey is the book that publishes no provision of the kind, and that changes what its silences mean.** Its definitions apparatus is Appendix V, the Glossary, and that has no entry for *"player"* and none for *"skater"* — it defines *Goalkeeper*, *Substitute Goalkeeper*, *Temporary Goalkeeper*, *Possession of the Puck* and *Vulnerable or Defenseless*, and runs straight from *Physical Engagement* to *Possession of the Puck*. What the book does instead is settle the question one rule at a time: *"a player or goalkeeper"* where it means both, *"All players, including goalkeepers"* through the equipment rules at 304(c), and *"a player (except the goalkeeper)"* at Rule 618(a) or *"a player other than the goalkeeper"* at Rule 614(a) where the goalie is out. So a bare *"player"* in that book is genuinely undetermined by the wording. *(The Glossary was read here in full, and no equivalent interpretive provision was found anywhere else in the playing rules or in the Casebook, both of which were searched for one; the same search finds Rule 5.1 immediately in the NHL and IIHF books. That is a search result rather than a proof that none exists.)*

**How to use this.** Under the NHL, the IIHF and Hockey Canada, read a bare *"player"* as covering your goaltender unless that rule carves them out. Under USA Hockey the wording will not settle it, so take the cautious reading of each: a **prohibition** binds your goalie until someone tells you otherwise, and a **permission** does not extend to them until someone tells you it does. The reading that costs you is always the one that assumes an exemption the book never granted.

### Periods and the clock

A game is **three periods of twenty minutes of actual play** (NHL Rule 77.1; IIHF Rule 77.1; USA Hockey Rule 636(a) gives 20 minutes as the *maximum* period length). The clock is **stop time** — it runs only while the puck is in play, so a 20-minute period takes roughly 35–40 minutes of real time.

Intermissions between periods are **18 minutes** in the NHL (Rule 77.2), **15 minutes** at IIHF (Rule 77.2) and **15 minutes** under USA Hockey (Rule 636(a)).

Teams **change ends after every period** (NHL Rule 86.3). This matters more than it sounds: in the second period your bench is at the far end from your defensive zone, which means the trip to the bench from a defensive-zone shift is the length of the ice. That is "the long change", and it is why being caught tired in your own end costs you more in the second period than in either of the others. See [Game Management](../systems/game_management.md).

**Rec-league variation:** many recreational and beer leagues use shorter periods, **running clock** (the clock does not stop for whistles) except perhaps in the final minutes, and a hard curfew that ends the game whatever the clock says. USA Hockey formally accommodates short periods by **pro-rating penalty times** (Rule 402): a minor is 1:00 in periods of 12 minutes or less, 1:30 in periods of more than 12 but under 17 minutes, and 2:00 in periods of 17–20 minutes. Adult classifications always use 2:00. **Majors, misconducts and match penalties scale too, but not on the same terms**: Rule 402 says minors *"shall be pro-rated"*, while the Notes to Rules 403, 404 and 405 say only that affiliates or governing bodies *"are authorized to reduce"* them, and all three exclude Adults. Where an affiliate does, a major is 3:00 in periods of 12 minutes or less, 4:00 in periods of more than 12 but under 17 minutes, and 5:00 in periods of 17–20 minutes; a match penalty's **served** time scales on the same three figures — the ejection does not, since 405(a) removes the player for the balance of the game either way; a misconduct is 6:00, then 8:00, then 10:00 across those same period lengths. **Even the minor schedule is not fixed**, though — Rule 402's own Note lets affiliates *"grant exceptions to the pro-rated schedule for minor penalties for the 14 & under age classification and older."* Check your own league's sheet — this is the single most common thing players get wrong when they move between leagues.

### Stoppages

Play stops for: a goal, a penalty, offside, icing, the puck leaving the playing surface, the goalie freezing the puck, a hand pass, a puck played above shoulder height, an injury, a displaced net, or an official's judgement that the puck is unplayable or out of sight. Every stoppage restarts with a **faceoff** at one of nine marked spots (see section 8).

**Practically:** the whistle is not the end of your shift's responsibility. Two things carry on after it — the scrum around the net, and the faceoff. Both are places where undisciplined players hand over penalties and possession. ⚠️ **And a third thing carries on after it: the puck stops being yours to send anywhere.** Under the **NHL and the IIHF**, firing it out of the rink once the whistle has gone is a two-minute minor and nothing worse — Rule 63.2(ii) reaches a player who *"deliberately shoots or bats … the puck outside the playing area (from anywhere on the ice surface) during the play **or after a stoppage of play**"*, and NHL Rule 63.9 scopes every delay-of-game infraction to *"a penalty (minor, bench minor, penalty shot or awarded goal)"* — there is no misconduct rung in either book. **In the two amateur books the minor is the bottom of a ladder.** **USA Hockey** puts a **misconduct** on a player who *"[s]hoots or bats the puck outside of the playing area during a stoppage of play"* (Rule 601(c)(10)), a **game misconduct** on that same act *"when done in protest of an official's decision"* (601(d)(7)) or on a puck shot *"in the general direction of an on-ice official, or in the direction of a spectator during a stoppage of play"* (601(d)(9)), and a **match penalty** where a player *"shoots the puck directly at an official or spectator in a manner that recklessly endangers the official or spectator"* (the Note to 601(d)). **Hockey Canada names the act in its own words:** *"Shooting the puck out of the playing area, after the whistle"* is unsportsmanlike conduct under **Rule 11.1, preamble item (v)** — a Minor or Bench Minor under 11.1(a), and a **Misconduct** under 11.1(d)(i) for a player who persists after one. ⚠️ **Do not read that as one free minor:** Note 1 to 11.1(d) provides that *"A Referee is not required to assess a Minor penalty under this rule before assessing a Misconduct penalty, but may assess either penalty initially."* So let the linesperson pick it up, and never send it toward an official or the crowd. The tiers and the one question USA Hockey leaves open are set out under §Other infractions.

### Changing on the fly

You may change players **at any time during play** (NHL Rule 74.1). The condition is that the player leaving the ice must be **within five feet of the bench and out of the play** before the substitute steps on.

**⚠️ That five feet is two books' number, not four.** **IIHF Rule 74.1** writes the identical provision in metric — *"within 1.50m of their Players' bench and out of the play before the change is made"* — which is five feet to within an inch, so it is the same rule. **Hockey Canada Rule 6.1(a) gives you twice the room**: the player leaving *"will be at the Players' Bench (within 3.05m (10 ft.)) and out of play before any change is made"*, penalised under Rule 10.7(a). **CARHA Rule 19(b) writes the same 10 ft.** **And USA Hockey Rule 204(b) writes no distance at all** — the retiring player *"shall always be at the players' bench and out of the play before any change is made"*, and there is nothing to measure. If you play under an American or Canadian book, the five-foot figure is not yours.

The exact mechanics matter because they define the penalty:

- A substitute is considered **on the ice once both of his skates are on the ice**.
- If either player — the one coming on or the one going off — **plays the puck, checks an opponent, or makes physical contact with an opponent** while both are on the ice, that is **too many men**.
- If the retiring player is accidentally *struck* by the puck during the change, **no penalty** and no stoppage.
- If a player on the bench plays the puck or interferes with an opponent while still on the bench, that is **interference** (Rule 56), not too many men.
- You must leave via the **bench**, not any other exit. Leaving via the gate to the dressing room and being replaced is a bench minor.

**⚠️ USA Hockey adds a third way to commit this offence, and none of the other four books compared here writes it.** The two triggers above — playing the puck, or contacting an opponent — are the NHL's whole list. **Rule 204(b)** adds *"or causes their team to gain a competitive advantage"*, and the USA Hockey **Casebook** applies it at Rule 204 Situation 8 to a change where the retiring player is *"in the vicinity of the players' bench"*, neither player touches the puck or an opponent, and the substitute turns a 2-on-1 against into a 2-on-2: *"Yes… Team A gained a territorial or positional advantage as a result of the change… and should be penalized."* Under that book, winning the odd-man rush **is** the infraction.

**What this means for you:** the change is a defensive act, not an administrative one. Change when your team has the puck in the offensive zone or in the neutral zone going forward — never when the puck is coming at you. If the puck turns over while you are halfway to the bench, **finish the trip and get off**; do not turn around and play the puck, because that is the actual infraction. Your teammate stepping on is the one who plays.

**Long shifts are a rules problem as well as a fitness problem.** A 90-second shift ends with you on the wrong side of the puck, and the temptation to reach with your stick instead of moving your feet is what produces hooking and tripping calls. Most rec-league penalties are conditioning penalties in disguise — a reading of the rulebooks against how adult recreational hockey is officiated, not a published count of calls. See [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md).

### Too many men on the ice

**Too many men is a bench minor** — two minutes, served by any player the coach designates through the captain (NHL Rules 74.2, 17.1). It can be called by **either the referees or the linespersons**.

Two useful details:

- If the offending team **scores** before the whistle goes for too many men, **the goal is disallowed** and the penalty is still assessed (Rule 74.2).
- A player coming out of the penalty box who is being changed for must **skate to within five feet of his own bench** before the change happens (NHL Rule 74.3; IIHF Rule 74.3 is the same at 1.50 m). Sending a fresh body over the boards while the penalised player is still crossing the ice is too many men.
  ⚠️ **The other two books word this one without a distance, and one of them prices it against you personally.** USA Hockey Rule 204(c) requires the player to *"proceed by way of the ice and be at their players' bench before any substitution can be made"*, and Hockey Canada Rule 6.1(b) the same — but Hockey Canada assesses *"a Minor penalty under Rule 10.4 (a) – Leaving the Players' or Penalty Bench"* rather than a bench minor, so it is your two minutes, not the bench's.
  ⚠️ **And USA Hockey carves out adults.** Rule 204(c)'s own exception covers *"when a major or match penalty is imposed in Adult Classifications under Rule 403(d) or 405(a)"* — adult teams are not required to put a substitute on the penalty bench for a major or match, so its Casebook, Rule 204 Situation 11, permits them to send a player straight from the players' bench when the penalty expires. **Youth is the opposite**: Situation 10 keeps the team short-handed until the penalised player has actually left the penalty bench by way of the ice, on pain of a bench minor.

**Practically:** count. The centre or the last defenceman back is usually the one who notices. If you step on and see six blue jerseys, get straight back off — the officials will usually allow the correction if nobody has played the puck.

### Line changes at a stoppage

At a whistle, the **visiting team puts its line-up on the ice first** (up to five seconds), then the **home team changes** (up to eight seconds), and the referee lowers his hand to close the window (NHL Rule 82.2 sets out that four-step clock). That is the concrete value of home ice: the home coach gets **last change** and can therefore match his lines against yours.

**But last change is not unconditional, and the exception lives in a different rule.** Rule 82.1 — not 82.2 — grants the right in the first place, and grants it with a carve-out: *"The home team may then make any desired substitution, **except in cases following an icing, which does not result in the delay of the game**."* Later in the same rule, that exception is spelled out: *"A team that is in violation of Rule 63.8 – Delaying the Game or Rule 81 – Icing shall not be permitted to make any player substitutions prior to the ensuing face-off."*

**Quoted in full, the clause is not ambiguous about whose icing.** The two passages are the same bar — the second one names the team it falls on, *"a team that is in violation of… Rule 81 – Icing"* — and it pairs Rule 81 with Rule 63.8 (Delaying the Game), which is what the first paragraph's trailing "delay of the game" wording is pointing at. **NHL Rule 81.4** then repeats the bar in the same words, with the same three exceptions (replacing a goalkeeper who had come off for an extra attacker, replacing an injured player, or a penalty that changes on-ice strength) — exceptions that only make sense for the team that iced it. If the carve-out froze both benches after any icing, the later paragraph and Rule 81.4 would have nothing left to do. So: **ice the puck and you cannot change, home or away; after the *visitors* ice it, the home team changes normally and keeps last change.** That is exactly why icing is expensive — you hand the other side fresh legs *and* the matchup. See [Faceoffs](../systems/faceoffs.md) and [Game Management](../systems/game_management.md).

**After an icing, the offending team may not change at all** (Rule 82.1, Rule 81.4). More on that below.

**And icing is not the only way to lose the change — Rule 63.8 is the other half of the clause quoted above, and it is the half nobody repeats.** Under the **NHL**: *"In the event that the goal post is displaced accidentally by a defending skater or goalkeeper causing a stoppage in play, the ensuing face-off shall be conducted at one of the end zone face-off spots in the defending zone. The offending team shall not be permitted to make any player substitutions prior to the face-off."* The exceptions are the icing three — replacing a goalkeeper who had come off for an extra attacker, replacing an injured player, or a penalty that changes on-ice strength. **IIHF Rule 63.8 is the same rule in the same words**, writing *"a defending Player"* where the NHL writes *"a defending skater or goalkeeper"* — **which is the same set of people, not a narrower one**, because IIHF Rule 5.1 provides that *"any reference to 'Player' shall refer to both skaters and goalkeepers"*, as set out at the top of this section. It reads identically in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions — so it is the British rule too. In both books the attacking team then chooses which of the two end-zone dots the draw is taken at. **Rule 63.8 has a second limb as well** — for a goalkeeper freezing a puck shot in from beyond the centre red line — **and its exception list is one shorter than this one's.** Section 7 covers that one.

⚠️ **So knocking your own net off does not buy your tired line a change. You get the whistle and you stay on the ice** — with a defensive-zone draw at the dot the other team picked, **no timeout available to you either** (Rule 87.1, in the Timeouts list below), and a **minor for delay of game** on top if the referee judges you did it deliberately — that penalty is in section 4. **And it is the defender's act that does this:** where an *attacking* player displaces the net, NHL and IIHF Rule 63.4 let play run on while the defending side still has the puck, and put the draw where play was stopped, with no substitution bar at all. **And the IIHF's *Situation Handbook* shuts the last hatch.** Asked whether a commercial break can be taken at that stoppage and whether the defending team may change after it, **Situation 82.6** answers: *"The commercial break shall not be initiated according to Rule 87.2. The defending team is not allowed to change any players."* (It is Situation 82.7 in the 2026/27 edition, in the same words.) **Situation 63.32** restates the exception list as those three and no others.

**The other two books part here.** **Hockey Canada writes the same consequence, but only in some categories.** Rule 6.1(g) applies *"In categories of U18 AAA, Junior and at the option of the Member in Senior"*, and there, *"when the goal frame has been accidentally dislodged by any defending player, including the goaltender, causing a stoppage of play, this will result in a 'no-change' situation"*, with the defending team *"subject to the same restrictions as outlined in Rule 6.7 (d)(i)"* — the no-change-on-icing clause, whose three exceptions are the same three. **That is exactly the scope of its icing bar**, so below U18 AAA, and in Senior unless your Member has adopted it, there is no bar at all. **No counterpart to any of this was found in USA Hockey.** Its Rule 610(d) stops play for a displaced goal frame and penalises a deliberate one, Rule 612 writes no faceoff location for it, and neither Rule 204 nor the *Casebook* situations on Rules 610 and 617 tie a substitution consequence to it — searched by rule number and by concept across both volumes, which is a limit of that search rather than proof none exists. Under USA Hockey the ordinary change window is the only thing stopping you, and that closes at every stoppage anyway.

### Overtime

**NHL regular season** (Rule 84.1, 84.4): a **five-minute, sudden-death, 3-on-3 overtime**, then a **shootout** if still tied. The shootout is three shooters a side, then sudden death; no player may shoot twice until everyone eligible has shot. A team that loses in overtime or the shootout still keeps the single point it earned by being tied after 60 minutes.

Three-on-three is a different sport. There is enormous open ice, the value of possession is extreme, and the single worst thing you can do is force a low-percentage play that gives the puck away — a turnover in 3-on-3 is very often a goal against within seconds. Note also that pulling the goalie in regular-season overtime **forfeits the guaranteed point** if you lose while he is off (Rule 84.2), and once he is off he **cannot return on the fly** — he must wait for a stoppage, or it is a bench minor.

**NHL playoffs** (Rule 84.5): **full 20-minute sudden-death periods at even strength**, with a 15-minute intermission before the first and ends changed at the start of each overtime period, repeated until somebody scores. No shootout, no 3-on-3. *Note: 84.5 states those periods, that one intermission and the change of ends, and carries no on-ice-strength provision at all — so the even strength is an inference from the rule's silence rather than something it states. Rule 34.3 confirms that the later intermissions happen — it says each one "will be completed in a normal manner" — but states no length for them, and "normal" read against Rules 34.2 and 77.2 points at eighteen minutes rather than the fifteen 84.5 sets for the first. No figure is given here for the later ones.*

**Overtime with penalties** (Rule 84.3) is fiddly but worth knowing the shape of: teams never drop below three skaters, so a power play in 3-on-3 overtime is played **4-on-3**, and a two-man advantage is played **5-on-3** with the penalised team at three. Once strength evens out, the count is corrected to 3-on-3 at the next stoppage.

**IIHF** (Rule 84.1, 84.4–84.6): preliminary-round games get a **five-minute 3-on-3** overtime then a **five-shooter shootout**. Playoff, relegation-round, classification-round and bronze-medal games get **up to ten minutes of 3-on-3** then a shootout (84.5 names all four). The **gold-medal game of a top-category championship** goes to unlimited sudden-death 3-on-3 periods with 15-minute intermissions until somebody scores. *Note: the IIHF rulebook text for the gold-medal game (Rule 84.6) does not state the length of each overtime period, so no figure is given here rather than a guess. It does state the rest: three skaters and a goaltender a side, a 15-minute intermission with an ice resurfacing before each, and — easily misread — teams do **not** change ends for the first overtime, but **do** for the second and for every one after it.*

**The IHUK In-House Rules amend the IIHF rule, and the amendment is that there often is no overtime at all.** The In-House Rules state against Rule 84 that overtime *"is only played in NIHL fixtures and in SNL fixtures where specifically provided for in competition regulations"* — so a British league game that is level at the buzzer normally stays level, and none of the IIHF formats above applies to it. Where NIHL fixtures do go to overtime the ordinary format — National League games and National group-stage playoff games alike — is **five minutes of 3-on-3** followed by a three-shooter game-winning-shots competition. **Playoff Final Weekend is played to two different provisions, and they are different in kind from each other**: a **semi-final** level after regulation goes to **one 20-minute 5-on-5 period** and then, if still tied, the same three-shooter GWS; the **final** goes to successive **20-minute 5-on-5 sudden-death periods** — the "golden goal" — with **no** GWS at all (In-House Rules, Appendix 1). So the longer period does not by itself mean the shootout is gone: in the semi-final it is still there. See [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md).

**USA Hockey** (Rule 638): overtime is **recommended**, not mandated — a 5-minute rest, then a **10-minute sudden-victory period**, on-ice strength never below three skaters plus a goalie, and a shootout may be used in addition to or instead of overtime. Crucially, the rule ends with a note that **affiliates and local governing bodies may make their own rules** on overtime length, number of periods and shootout format.

**Rec and beer leagues:** ⚠️ **there is no general practice here to carry into a game, and the one standalone recreational rulebook this document cites declines to set one.** CARHA Hockey's *Official Rule Book* defines a game as regular playing time *"and overtime, if such is required, in accordance with league or tournament regulations"* — it hands the question to the league rather than answering it. Ties may simply stand; where there is overtime it may be short (three to five minutes) and 4-on-4 or 3-on-3, sometimes followed by a three-shooter shootout. **This document has no count of how leagues divide between those.** Read your league's rules.

### Timeouts

Each NHL team gets **one 30-second timeout per game** (Rule 87.1). **IIHF Rule 87.1 also gives one 30-second timeout.** USA Hockey gives **one 60-second timeout** (Rule 636(f)) — **except in a curfew game, where neither team has one at all.** **Hockey Canada** gives one of **30 seconds** (Rule 6.17(e)). **In minor and female hockey** that timeout exists only in **stop-time** games and only *"wherever the local Member gives approval"*, though Note 3 makes it mandatory at Hockey Canada minor and female regional and national championships. Rule 6.17's preamble lets Members and leagues *"make their own rules regarding time of game for games under their jurisdiction"* — so the 30 seconds is a local default, **except that the preamble then requires this rule in inter-Member or national competition**, where it is fixed.

There are specific situations where the NHL will **not** grant a timeout (Rule 87.1):

- Following a **faceoff violation**.
- To the **defensive team following an icing**.
- To the defensive team when the **goalie froze a puck shot in from beyond the centre red line**.
- When a **defending player accidentally dislodged the net**.
- Once instructions have been given for a **penalty shot**, or at any point during a **shootout**.

The pattern is deliberate: all of those are situations where a team has just done something that is supposed to leave them stuck on the ice, tired. The rulebook closes the escape hatch.

**IIHF Rule 87.1 lists the same exclusions in the same words**, so a player under IIHF rules — which is what British league hockey runs on — gets no relief anywhere on that list. **USA Hockey Rule 636(f) lists none of them.** It carries a different restriction that has nothing to do with icing: *"For time curfew games, no time-outs shall be permitted"* — so in a game that must end by a fixed clock time, neither team has one at all. Its other restrictions are that the timeout be requested during a stoppage before the line-change procedure ends, and that a team wanting one at the same stoppage as its opponent must ask before the first timeout finishes. So **outside a curfew game** the escape hatch the NHL and IIHF close is open under USA Hockey: a team there really can ice the puck and then buy its tired players a minute. **In a curfew game there is no minute to buy** — and you are entitled to know in advance whether you are in one, because USA Hockey's own definition requires that *"Both teams must be notified of the curfew time prior to the start of the game."* **Hockey Canada writes no list of situations in which a time-out is refused** — nothing answering to the NHL's and the IIHF's icing, frozen-shoot-in and dislodged-net exclusions was found anywhere in its book, and where it does reach the question it goes the other way: Rule 6.7(d)(iii) expressly permits *either* team the time-out after an icing, in the three categories where its no-change rule runs, while still barring the offending team from making substitutions during it. So the clock stops for you and the same five skaters take the draw. What conditions a Hockey Canada timeout is whether your category has one at all — the approval question above — and not what has just happened on the ice.

---

## 2. Offside

### Offside: The rule

**Players of the attacking team must not precede the puck into the attacking zone** (NHL Rule 83.1).

The mechanics, precisely:

- **Skates decide, not sticks.** Your stick can be as far into the zone as you like.
- You are offside when **both skates are completely over the leading edge of the blue line**.
- You are **onside if either skate is in contact with the blue line, or on your own side of it**, at the instant the puck completely crosses the leading edge.
- "Your own side of the line" is defined by a **vertical plane** rising from the leading edge of the blue line. **If your skate has not yet broken that plane, you are onside — even if it is in the air.** An airborne trail skate over the neutral zone is onside. **This is NHL and IIHF only — under USA Hockey Rule 630(a) and Hockey Canada Rule 6.11 an airborne skate is offside**, because both of those books require actual skate *contact*: with the neutral zone or the blue line in USA Hockey's words, with *"the ice surface"* in Hockey Canada's. That is two books of the four. See **Rule-set differences** below.
- New for 2025-26: **"a 'skate' is to be considered the blade of the skate only."** Your boot and your ankle are irrelevant; the blade is the reference.
- The puck must **completely cross** the leading edge of the blue line. A puck sitting on the line is not yet in the zone.
- The blue line is **12 inches wide** (Rule 1.5). That is a full foot of legal real estate.
- A player **carrying the puck** may cross the line ahead of it, provided he had **possession and control of the puck with his stick** before both skates crossed (2025-26 wording; the change is reported by *Scouting The Refs*, comparing the 2024-25 and 2025-26 books, which suggests it *may* close the case of a player kicking the puck ahead of themselves — the rulebook itself does not say so).

### The technique consequence: drag a trail skate

Because **one skate on or behind the plane keeps you onside**, and because the line is a foot wide, you do not have to slow down at the blue line: you can attack it at full speed with your body and lead skate already in the zone, holding the trail blade on or behind the line until the puck crosses. **Which book you are under changes how you do it** — dragging the blade *in contact* with the line is legal under all four rule sets, but holding the trail skate *in the air* behind the plane is **NHL and IIHF only** and is **offside under USA Hockey Rule 630(a) and under Hockey Canada Rule 6.11**, both of which require skate contact. **How much North American rec and youth hockey runs on USA Hockey's book or Hockey Canada's is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it, so find out which book your league runs.** Keep the blade in contact with the line and you are onside in all four.

**The technique itself belongs to [Zone Entries](../systems/zone_entries.md)** — the four steps, the timing, and what it does to a defender. The same logic applies on a **regroup**: see [Neutral Zone Systems](../systems/neutral_zone_systems.md), and [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) for the defender's side of it, which is that you cannot rely on the blue line to save you.

### Delayed offside and tagging up

If attacking players have preceded the puck into the zone but the defending team is **in a position to bring the puck out without delay or contact**, the linesperson raises an arm and signals a **delayed offside** rather than blowing the whistle (NHL Rule 83.3, signal 29.7).

The delayed offside is **nullified — arm drops, play carries on** — if either:

1. **All players of the offending team clear the zone** (skate contact with the blue line). This is **tagging up**. **All four books require the same thing and three of them use the same words:** the NHL (83.3(i)), the IIHF (83.3(I)) and USA Hockey (630(d)(2)) say *"at the same instant"*; **Hockey Canada 6.11(b)(ii)** (the tag-up clause) **states it as a result instead** — *"so that the attacking zone is completely clear of attacking players"* — and its Interpretation 11 resolves that to *"the instant the zone is completely free of attacking players"*. **Do not read the wording difference as a permission.** Neither version requires the exits to coincide, and neither lets you re-enter while a teammate is still deep: IIHF Situation 83.34 says it in the book that uses the phrase — a player has cleared *"the instant the skate makes contact with the blue line"*, and *"should another Team A player enter the attacking zone while A31 is airborne, the delayed off-side shall remain in effect"* — and Hockey Canada Interpretation 11 rules the identical scenario the same way. Tag as one and the zone is clear. ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey 630(d) there is no delayed offside and no tag-up at all below Youth 15-Only, Girls 16U, High School and Adult** — below those levels the whistle has already gone. Once the arm is down, everybody may re-enter and pursue the puck.
2. The **defending team passes or carries the puck into the neutral zone**.

**Clearing the zone requires actual skate contact — the vertical plane does not apply here.** This is the one place the NHL's own wording is easy to misread, and the IIHF text spells it out: where the NHL parenthesis reads *"(skate contact with the blue line)"*, **IIHF Rule 83.3(I)** reads *"(skate contact with the blue line, '3D' judgement is not used in this situation)"*. So the airborne trail skate that keeps you **onside on the entry** does **not** clear you on a delayed offside. Coasting over the line in the air leaves the arm up. Touch the paint.

**Under the NHL and the IIHF**, the delayed offside becomes a **whistle** if any attacking player, while the arm is up:

- **Touches the puck**, or
- **Attempts to gain possession of a loose puck**, or
- **Forces the defending puck carrier further back into his own zone**, or
- **Is about to make physical contact with the defending puck carrier**.

Those four are NHL Rule 83.3's, and **IIHF Rule 83.3 lists the same four in the same order**, differing only in pronoun — so they are the British reader's four as well. Note the second and third of them: under those two books you do not have to touch anything, and **merely chasing the defenceman deeper into his own zone kills the play.** That is the most commonly misunderstood part of the rule and it costs teams offensive-zone possession every game.

⚠️ **The other two books are shorter, and the fourth trigger — the whistle that arrives *before* contact — is written in neither of them.** **USA Hockey Rule 630(d)** stops play only where *"(1) An attacking player touches the puck. (2) An attacking player attempts to gain possession of the puck or continues to apply pressure to the defending puck carrier."* Its pressure limb is actually **wider** than the NHL's — pressure that never drives the carrier deeper still brings the whistle there — but it is a judgement about pressure, not about an imminent hit, and there is no about-to-make-contact condition at all. **Hockey Canada Rule 6.11(b)(ii)** (the stop-play clause) is shorter again: *"If any attacking player touches the puck or attempts to gain possession of a loose puck while the puck is still in the attacking zone, the Linesperson will stop play."* No pressure limb, no contact limb, and a locative qualifier none of the other three carries.

⚠️ **Read that citation carefully if you go to look it up, because Hockey Canada's Rule 6.11(b) carries two separate i./ii. lists and "6.11(b)(ii)" therefore names two different rules.** The **tag-up clause** — the one that nullifies the delayed offside, requiring skate contact from every attacker *"so that the attacking zone is completely clear of attacking players"* — sits under the heading *"The Linesperson will drop their arm to nullify the off-side violation and allow play to continue if"*. The **stop-play clause** — touching the puck or going for a loose puck *"while the puck is still in the attacking zone"* — sits under *"During a delayed offside, if"*. The labels *tag-up clause* and *stop-play clause* are used throughout this page to say which of the two is meant; they are not the rulebook's own names for them.

⚠️ **So under either North American book, do not close on the defending puck carrier expecting the whistle to arrive first.** Nothing in Hockey Canada's rule stops play before you get there, and under USA Hockey the only thing that might is a linesperson's reading of *pressure*. The arm being up is not a stoppage, he is still playing the puck, and **he is not braced for you.** Hockey Canada answers the player who hits anyway only after the fact: Rule 6.11(d)(i) reaches an attacker who *"deliberately plays or attempts to make a play on the puck **or against an opposing player** in a delayed off-side situation, knowing that they are off-side"*, and calls it an intentional offside, which costs a face-off in your own end (6.11(d)) rather than a penalty — and that is no protection whatever for the man you hit. [Zone Entries](../systems/zone_entries.md) works this divergence through from the forechecker's side, at the line.

A nice piece of trivia with real use: a player in the zone during a delayed offside may **clear it by going to the bench**. **NHL Rule 83.3** writes the whole route: an attacker who *"elects to proceed to his players' bench (which extends into the attacking zone) to be replaced by a teammate… shall be considered to have cleared the zone when both skates are off the ice and the Linesperson judges him to have left the playing surface"* — and *"[i]f his replacement comes onto the ice in the attacking zone, while the delayed off-side is still in effect, he too must clear the attacking zone."* **IIHF Rule 83.3 carries the same two sentences**, differing only in pronoun and in capitalising *"Players' bench"*.

⚠️ **Read the words "his players' bench" as a condition, because in one book getting the door wrong is a bench minor.** USA Hockey's *Casebook*, at **Rule 630 Situation 12**, allows it *"[p]rovided the player jumped into their own players' bench and no substitution entered the ice prior to the puck crossing the blue line"* — and then: *"However, if the player jumped off the ice surface into any other part of the rink, and was substituted for, their team must be assessed a bench minor penalty for illegal substitution."* So going over the boards at the penalty bench, or anywhere else, with someone coming on for you, has left your own team short-handed in the act of trying to nullify an offside. **Hockey Canada names the same door**: its **Interpretation 11 to Rule 6.11(b)** has the player leave *"into their Players' Bench"* and nullifies the delayed offside *"provided that the substitute for player A10 does not enter the attacking zone"* until it has been — **so this is not a USA Hockey peculiarity**, although Hockey Canada's interpretation attaches no penalty to the wrong door, and neither the NHL's nor the IIHF's Rule 83.3 does either. *(Situation 12 gives its rule reference as 630(a) Note 2, and the printed Rule 630(a) carries one Note. The number is the book's, not a misquotation of it.)*

**What to do as a player on a delayed offside:**

- If you are the one in the zone: **get out, and get out cleanly to the line**, do not just drift. **The zone has to be empty before anyone re-enters, so the last man decides** — that is true in all four books, whether they word it as *"at the same instant"* or as *"completely clear of attacking players"*. **Do not re-enter on your own tag while a teammate is still deep:** Hockey Canada Interpretation 10(i) is explicit that *"the attacking zone must be completely clear of all attacking players before the off-side is nullified"*, so your own tag has not nullified anything — and playing the puck **or making a play against an opposing player** while knowingly offside is an intentional offside under 6.11(d)(i), with the faceoff in **your own** end. (And below USA Hockey's Youth 15-Only, Girls 16U, High School and Adult levels there is no tag-up to use.)
- **Do not touch the puck. Do not chase the D.** Retreat to the line, tag up, then attack again.
- If you are the defending team: **keep your head up and expect to be hit — in all four books.** You have a moment to make a clean breakout pass, and how free that moment is does depend on your book, but **in none of the four does the rule still protect you once the puck has left your stick.** Under the **NHL and the IIHF** (Rule 83.3) an attacker who forces you deeper, or who is about to make contact with you, ends the play — **but both of those triggers name the defending *puck carrier*, so the protection lapses the instant you pass**, and a whistle for an imminent hit stops the play rather than the body already committed at speed. ⚠️ Under Hockey Canada Rule 6.11(b)(ii) **nothing in the stop-play clause stops a forechecker closing on you at all**, and under **USA Hockey Rule 630(d)** the only thing that might is *"continues to apply pressure to the defending puck carrier"* — which names the carrier too, and lapses on the same pass. Take the extra look; do not take it with your head down. See [Breakouts](../systems/breakouts.md).

### Disallowed goals on a delayed offside

If the puck is **shot into the zone** creating a delayed offside and that same shot goes in the net — directly, or off the goalie, a player, the boards, the glass, equipment or an official — **the goal is disallowed**, even if the attacking team had cleared the zone in the meantime (Rule 83.4). The only way the attacking team scores during a delayed offside is if the **defending team puts it in their own net** with no contact or action by the attacking team.

**That second sentence is NHL and IIHF only, and this is where the four books part.** IIHF Rule 83.4 carries both halves in the NHL's words, so it is the British rule too. **Hockey Canada** carries only the first: Rule 6.11(b) has the goal *"not be allowed as the original shot was off-side"*, and Note 1 extends that to a shot that goes in after the attacking team has cleared — but no own-goal exception appears in Rule 6.11 or in any of its thirteen interpretations. **USA Hockey** carries neither in its *Playing Rules*. Rule 630 has no disallowed-goal provision, and the general scoring rule pulls both ways. Rule 617(c) lists what is not allowed, including *"The puck enters the attacking zone illegally"* (617(c)(8)); Rule 617(b) lists what is, including *"The puck enters the goal as a result of any action by the defending team"* (617(b)(2)). Nothing in the Playing Rules settles the delayed offside between the two — but the separate Casebook does, and settles it against the exception. Casebook Rule 630, Situation 41 asks *"May a goal ever be allowed during the course of a delayed offside?"* and answers *"No"*, holding that this is so *"[e]ven if the defending team shoots, passes or deflects the puck into its own goal"* and even where the attacking team cleared the zone first. (Situation 41 gives its rule reference as 630(d.3); the printed Rule 630(d) stops at (2).) So of the four books USA Hockey is the one that writes the own-goal exception out, and Hockey Canada the one that leaves it unsettled. Under neither North American book count on it.

### Where the faceoff goes

This is worth learning, because it is a real territorial consequence (Rule 83.6):

- Offside where the puck **entered the zone in an attacker's possession** — the rule's own words are *"as a result of the attacking team carrying the puck over the attacking blue line"* → faceoff at the **neutral-zone spot nearest that attacking zone**. You lose the zone but keep the neutral-zone draw.

  **The carrier is not the offender.** A player with possession and control may cross the line ahead of the puck, as quoted above (Rule 83.1), so nobody is ever offside for carrying it in. The offence is a *teammate* who had already preceded the puck over the line. **What Rule 83.6 sorts on is how the puck entered the zone, not who took it in.**
- Offside where the puck **entered on a shot or a pass** → faceoff at the *"face-off spot in the zone closest to the point of origin of the shot or pass (even if deflected off an attacking or defending player or an official)"*, which Rule 83.6's delayed-offside list adds is the one *"that gives the offending team the least amount of territorial advantage."* **That is a faceoff spot, not the patch of ice the pass left from** — if your defenceman fired it in from behind his own net, the draw goes to an **end-zone dot in your own end**, not to the goal line where he was standing.

So: a carried offside costs you a little; a pass-created offside can cost you the length of the neutral zone and most of your own end on top of it. At the extreme the two draws are **89 ft apart up the ice** — from the neutral-zone spot 5 ft outside the attacking blue line, back to an end-zone dot 20 ft out from your own goal line. (Those two spot positions, and the 64 ft from blue line to goal line that sits between them, are owned by [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md); the 89 ft is the distance between those two spots, measured in opposite directions from centre ice: the neutral-zone spot sits 20 ft out (the blue line is 25 ft from centre, and Rule 1.9 puts the spot 5 ft outside it), and an end-zone dot sits 69 ft out (the goal line is 89 ft from centre, and Rule 1.9 puts the dot 20 ft in front of it). 20 plus 69. Those are the rulebook's dimensions added up, not a distance the rulebook states. **Those are NHL-sheet distances**, and USA Hockey Rule 630(c) words the location differently again — "the nearest last play face-off location".) That is a real argument for carrying the puck over the line under pressure rather than firing a hopeful pass into the zone.

![](diagram:offside-faceoff-location)

⚠️ **One more thing decides where the draw goes, and it is not in the offside rule at all: which infraction gets called when two happen on the same play.** A defenceman fires the puck the length of the ice while a teammate is already over the far blue line, and that is a **delayed offside and a potential icing at once** — two calls that send the faceoff to opposite ends of the rink. **Three of the four books answer it, and all three lean the same way: the icing. None of them answers it in its playing rules** — the answers live in the case and interpretation layers, so they are rulings rather than rule text. **USA Hockey**'s *Casebook*, at Rule 630 Situation 33, rules that *"[t]he icing infraction takes precedence"* and that *"the face-off shall take place in the end zone of the offending team because the icing infraction was completed"* — printed under the heading for the classifications where tag-up applies, so it does not speak for the ones below them. Its Situation 21 gives the general principle, on a different collision: *"As a general rule, the more 'serious' infraction is penalized."* **Hockey Canada** states it in the book, at **Interpretation 8 to Rule 6.11(b)**: *"Where icing occurs with an attacking player in an off-side position, the infraction that is called is icing and the ensuing face-off will take place in the offending team's end zone"* — and its **Interpretation 10(iii)** adds a limb the others do not, for the case where the officials go the other way: *"If the play is stopped because of the off-side, the ensuing face-off would go to the face-off spot nearest to where the shot originated."*

**The IIHF answers it as a judgement rather than a rule**, and only in its *Situation Handbook*. Situation 81.20 (Situation 81.21 in the 2026/27 edition) reads: *"The Linesperson shall use discretion to determine whether to stop play for off-side or intentional off-side; however, whenever possible with player safety at the forefront, the linesperson will allow the icing to be completed."* Situation 81.1 turns it the other way where the offside attacker is the danger — there the linesperson lets the icing run because *"there will be no physical contact by the attacking player on the delayed off-side"*, and the Handbook's note adds that if the linesperson judges the offside attacker *"will get to the puck first or may make contact with an opponent, the linesperson must stop play and call the off-side first."* **⚠️ No precedence provision was found in the NHL's rulebook text or in the IIHF's** — searched by rule number at 81 and 83 and by concept across both books — and the NHL publishes no case book, so for the NHL this is unanswered here rather than settled.

**Under the NHL and the IIHF that call decides more than the end you draw in — it decides whether you can change**, because the icing carries the substitution bar and the offside carries none. The IIHF Handbook says so in the same breath: *"It is imperative the Linesperson inform the nearest Referee and the teams of what the call is immediately after the whistle as it will affect the offending team's ability to make a player substitution."* Under USA Hockey there is nothing to decide, because its icing carries no substitution bar either.

### Video review

An offside that leads to a goal is reviewable by **Coach's Challenge** (NHL Rule 38.2(a)). An **unsuccessful** challenge costs the challenging team a **minor penalty (2:00)** for delaying the game, and a **double minor (4:00)** for any subsequent unsuccessful challenge in the same game (Rule 38.8). Other than a challenge or the delayed-offside case above, **no goal can be disallowed after the fact for offside** (Rule 83.4).

**The IIHF has the same machinery** — Rule 83.5 routes an offside challenge to Rule 38, and IIHF Rule 38.7 imposes the same escalating bench minor: *"a bench minor penalty (2 minutes) for Delaying the Game"*, then *"a double-bench minor penalty (4 minutes)"* for a further unsuccessful challenge.

> **⚠️ The IHUK In-House Rules remove it entirely, and this is one of the sharpest British departures from everything else in this document.** There is no review during the game. That is not the same as nothing being reviewed — In-House Rule 28 opens supplementary-discipline cases through *"the defined video review process"*, and that can reach an incident nobody penalised at the time. The In-House Rules state against IIHF Rule 37 that *"On-ice video review is not available in EIH or SIHA competition and may not be used by referees, official scorers, timekeepers, or any other game official"*, and against Rule 38 that *"As on-ice video review is not available (see Rule 37), a Coach's Challenge is not permitted in EIH or SIHA competition."* The same bar is repeated against double minor penalties (18.4), high-sticking (60.3, in Section 8), goaltender interference (69.1), goals (78.5, 78.6) and puck out of bounds (85.7). **In EIH and SIHA competition the referee's call on the ice is the call, on every one of those.** Wherever this document describes a decision being reviewed, challenged or overturned, that is the NHL and international tournament game, not yours. See [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md).

### Rule-set differences

- **IIHF Rule 83** reaches the same place as the NHL rule on everything that matters, including the plane concept, but it is not word-for-word. ⚠️ **That comparison is edition-dependent, and the newer IIHF book has closed the gap.** The **IIHF 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** book carries **neither** of the NHL's 2025-26 clarifications — no *"blade of the skate only"*, and no *"with his stick"* in the puck-carrier clause. The **IIHF 2026/27** book carries **both**, in the NHL's own words: at 83.1, *"For the purposes of this rule, a 'skate' is to be considered the blade of the skate only"*, and, in the puck-carrier clause, *"provided they have 'possession and control' of the puck **with their stick** prior to **both skates completely** crossing the leading edge of the blue line"* — where the 2025/26 clause reads only *"prior to their skates crossing the leading edge"*. ⚠️ **Both changes alter what you do.** Under the newer book only the **blade** counts, so a blade that has broken the plane is offside however far back the boot is; and the puck-carrier's carve-out now requires the **stick**, so pushing the puck across with a skate no longer earns it. 🇬🇧 **Whether that has reached Britain is unresolved, and this document does not resolve it.** The IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 adopt *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"* by name and version in their Introduction, while their cover names *"the **Current** IIHF Rule Book"* — and the token "83" appears nowhere in that document, so it amends Rule 83 not at all. **If you play IIHF-sanctioned hockey the 2026/27 wording is yours now; in a British league, ask which edition your competition is on.** See [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md). It adds two things the NHL text does not have: the sentence that *"the question of an off-side never arises until the puck has completely crossed the leading edge of the blue line, at which time the decision is to be made"*; and, in the delayed-offside clearing test, the explicit *"'3D' judgement is not used in this situation"* quoted above.
- **USA Hockey Rule 630(a) requires contact, not a plane — and that reverses the airborne trail skate.** The rule reads: *"The position of the player's stick shall not be considered in all instances deciding offside. A player is considered 'offside' when the player does not have skate contact with any part of the Neutral Zone or the blue line when the puck crosses the determining edge of the blue line."* The Note adds that *"a player only needs to have skate contact with one skate with the blue line to be considered 'onside.'"* So for a skate **on** the line the outcome matches the NHL — but a skate held **in the air** over the neutral zone is **onside under the NHL and IIHF and offside under USA Hockey**. If you play USA Hockey, drag the blade in contact; do not lift it — and read the next bullet before you assume that is the only book it applies to.
- **Hockey Canada Rule 6.11 reaches the same result by different words, so the split is two books against two — not one against three.** It does not borrow USA Hockey's test; it counts only skates touching the ice. The rule reads: *"Once the puck has completely crossed the blue-line, it is the position of the player's skates, and not that of their stick or any other part of their body, will be the determining factor in all instances in deciding an off-side. **Only the player's skate(s) that are in physical contact with the ice surface will be used in determining an off-side.**"* Its **Interpretation 3 to Rule 6.11(a)** settles the case in one line: *"A player has one skate above the blue-line (not touching the ice) and one skate over the blue-line at the instant the puck completely crosses the blue-line. OFF-SIDE."*
- **USA Hockey delayed offside only exists at Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult** (Rule 630(d)). Below those levels **there is no tagging up** — offside is immediate. If you coach or play youth hockey, this is a genuine difference in how you attack the line. **Hockey Canada writes no such limit — 6.11 is unscoped.** The rule carries no division or category clause at all, where the same book scopes its icing rule in terms (6.7(e), *"In categories of U18AAA, Junior, and at the option of the Member in Senior"*) and its body-checking rule too (7.3, *"This rule applies only in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada."*). So a delayed offside tags up the same way in every Hockey Canada division.
- **⚠️ The acts that turn a delayed offside into a whistle also differ, and the one that matters for your safety is written in only two of the four books.** *Being about to make physical contact with the defending puck carrier* is a whistle under **NHL and IIHF Rule 83.3** and appears in **neither USA Hockey Rule 630(d) nor Hockey Canada Rule 6.11(b)(ii)** (the stop-play clause) — so in neither North American **rule** does an imminent hit itself bring a whistle. ⚠️ **Do not close on the defending puck carrier expecting one to arrive before you do.** Hockey Canada writes no pressure limb either; USA Hockey's — *"continues to apply pressure to the defending puck carrier"* (630(d)(2)) — **can** stop play before contact, but it is a linesperson's judgement about pressure rather than about an imminent hit, and it names the *carrier*, so it lapses the moment he passes. ⚠️ **That negative is about the two rules, though, and not about the two books — USA Hockey's Casebook does give body contact as a reason to blow the whistle.** At Rule 630, Situation 39 it directs that where offside players *"decline to turn immediately and go back to the blue line to 'tag up' and instead continue in pursuit of the puck, play should be stopped immediately"*, reasoning that *"[t]he potential for unnecessary body contact should not be allowed in a situation where an infraction (offside) has already occurred."* **Read it no wider than its own facts: it is guidance to the linesperson on a delayed offside already in progress, not a whistle for an imminent hit anywhere on the ice**, and it carries an exception of its own where a defender will plainly reach a loose puck first and have a reasonable chance to advance it out of the zone. The four books are set against each other in full under §Delayed offside and tagging up above.

---

## 3. Icing

### Icing: The rule

If a team **equal or superior in numerical strength** to their opponent shoots, bats or deflects the puck from **their own half of the ice** across the opposition **goal line**, play is stopped (NHL Rule 81.1). The **centre red line divides the ice into halves** for this purpose, and the reference point is the **point of last contact by the team in possession**.

### "Gaining the line"

To nullify a potential icing, your team must **"gain the line"**: the puck, **while on a player's stick — not a skate — must make contact with the centre red line** (NHL Rule 81.1; IIHF Rule 81.1 carries the sentence word for word).

This is the detail almost nobody knows. If you are carrying the puck through the neutral zone and you fire it down the ice, the question is not where *you* were, it is where *the puck on your stick* was. Carry it that extra half-stride so the puck touches the red line and you can dump it in freely. Under those two books, kicking it forward over the line with your skate does **not** count.

![](diagram:icing-gaining-the-line)

> **⚠️ "Gaining the line" is an NHL and IIHF phrase, and it is in neither of the other two books.** It appears nowhere in the USA Hockey Official Playing Rules 2025-29 or the Hockey Canada Playing Rules 2026-2028, and neither of those books draws the stick-versus-skate distinction anywhere in its icing rule. Both word the test instead as the **point of last contact with the puck**: USA Hockey Rule 624 (Note) says *"the point of last contact with the puck shall be used to determine whether a potential icing exists"*, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 5 to Rule 6.7(c)(ii) says *"The key point is where the puck was last contacted, relative to the centre red line, prior to crossing the goal line."* The two also differ on the skate as an instrument: **Hockey Canada Rule 6.7 lists "kicking" among the actions that ice the puck**, while **USA Hockey Rule 624(a) lists only** *"shoots, bats with the hand or stick or deflects"*. So do not carry the NHL sentence into a USA Hockey or Hockey Canada game as though it were the rule there. **What is safe in all four books is the same act: get the puck itself past the red line, on your stick.** What differs is what happens if you use a skate instead, and only the NHL and IIHF answer that in terms.

### Hybrid, no-touch and touch icing

There are three systems:

- **Touch icing** (historical): a defending skater must physically touch the puck for icing to be called. Produces high-speed races into the end boards; largely abandoned for injury reasons.
- **No-touch / automatic icing:** play stops the instant the puck completely crosses the goal line, whoever is chasing.
- **Hybrid icing:** the race is to **the puck**, but the linesperson must decide it by the time the first player reaches the **end-zone faceoff dot** — the dot is a deadline, not a finish line.

**The NHL and IIHF both use hybrid icing** (NHL Rule 81.1; IIHF Rule 81.1 — near-identical wording, but not identical: see the note below). The mechanism is:

1. The linesperson first judges that **the puck will cross the goal line**.
2. He then judges **which player would touch the puck first**.
3. That decision is made **no later than the instant the first player reaches the end-zone faceoff dots**, with the **skate** as the determining factor.
4. If the race is **too close to call** by that point, **icing is called**.

Note the clarification the rulebook makes explicitly: the determining factor is **who would touch the puck first, not who reaches the dot first**. The dot is a deadline for the decision, not the finish line of the race.

> **One IIHF difference.** IIHF Rule 81.1 tracks the NHL wording closely but adds a paragraph the NHL rule does not contain: *"If there is no 'race' for the puck, icing should not be called until a defending skater crosses its defending blue line and the puck crosses the goal line (not between the goal posts)."* In practice this means that under IIHF rules, an uncontested icing is not whistled until a defender has actually come back for it — so a lazily uncontested dump can stay live fractionally longer than it would in the NHL.

The NHL adopted hybrid icing in **2013-14** and the IIHF in **2014**. **USA Hockey uses automatic (no-touch) icing** — Rule 624 states that "icing the puck shall have been completed the instant the puck has completely crossed the goal line", with no hybrid provision in the standing game. (Precisely: the one place "hybrid icing" does appear in the 2025-29 book is the **Disabled Hockey / sled** section, which reads *"Automatic icing will be used at youth levels. Hybrid icing will be used in adult classification games."* That does not apply to standing hockey, but the phrase is in the book.) Some rec and youth leagues use no-touch; a few still use touch icing. **Ask before your first game** — it changes whether you chase a dump-in at all.

### The full wave-off list

Icing is **not** called if (NHL Rules 81.1, 81.3, 81.5, 81.6):

1. The team that shot it is **shorthanded**.
2. The puck goes **directly off a faceoff** into the far end.
3. The puck **touches any part of an opposing player** — stick, skate or body — before or after crossing the goal line.
4. The puck **touches the opposing goalkeeper** in any way.
5. The **opposing goalkeeper feigns playing the puck, attempts to play it, or skates in the direction of it** at any time.
6. A **linesperson judges that an opposing skater (not the goalie) could have played the puck but did not**. This explicitly includes a team declining to play it during a line change to avoid a too-many-men call.
7. The puck **rebounds off an opponent** in their own half so as to cross the shooter's own goal line — that is not icing on the shooter.
8. The puck goes **into the net** — that is a goal, not an icing. None of the four NHL clauses cited above says so; it is the practical effect of the icing call requiring a stoppage the goal has already produced. USA Hockey states it expressly, in Rule 624(a): *"if the puck has entered the goal as a result of a legal action by the team shooting the puck, the goal shall be allowed."*
9. The **goalkeeper takes any action to dislodge the puck from the back of the net**.
10. The attacking team **gained the line** as defined above — an NHL and IIHF wave-off; neither the USA Hockey nor the Hockey Canada book contains the concept.

The puck **deflecting off an official does not automatically nullify** a potential icing.
