Two exceptions worth knowing on the shorthanded clause: when a penalty is **about to expire**, icing is judged **at the instant the penalty expires** — if the puck left the stick before expiry, no icing (Rule 81.6). And a team serving a **major** that fails to put a substitute on the penalty bench in time keeps playing a man down but is **no longer "shorthanded" for icing purposes** — icing will be called against them (Rule 81.6; the underlying substitution requirement is Rule 20.3).

**USA Hockey differs on the goalie:** Rule 624(b) lists five wave-offs and **does not include the goalkeeper moving toward or feigning at the puck**. Only actual contact by an opposing player, including the goalie, waves it off. USA Hockey also restricts the shorthanded exemption to **Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult** classifications (Rule 624(b)(1)) — below those levels, **a shorthanded team cannot ice the puck**.

### The consequences

Under NHL rules, an icing costs the offending team three things:

1. **A defensive-zone faceoff.** And the **attacking team chooses which of the two end-zone dots** the draw is taken at (Rule 81.2).
2. **No line change.** The offending team may not substitute before the faceoff (Rules 81.4, 82.1). The exceptions are narrow: replacing a goalie who had been pulled for an extra attacker, replacing an injured player, or when a penalty has been assessed that changes on-ice strength. Players on the ice are determined **at the moment the puck leaves the offending player's stick**.
3. **No timeout.** The defensive team may not take a timeout after an icing (Rule 87.1). **Nor is a commercial timeout permitted** — the same rule states that *"No commercial time-out is permitted after the calling of an icing infraction"* — **except where a penalty is assessed that affects the on-ice strength of either team.**

If a **linesperson erred** in calling icing, the faceoff is at **centre ice** (Rule 81.2) — the same remedy appears in IIHF Rule 81.2 and USA Hockey Rule 624(c).

If the **defending team deliberately abstains** from playing the puck to force the stoppage, the referee moves the draw to the **corner spot nearest their own goal** (Rule 81.2).

**IIHF imposes all three consequences, in the NHL's own words.** IIHF Rule 81.2 gives the attacking team the choice of dot; IIHF Rule 81.4 bars substitution; and **IIHF Rule 87.1 reads *"No 'time-out' shall be granted to the defensive team following an icing, following the goalkeeper causing a stoppage of play from a shoot-in from beyond the center red line, or when a defending Player accidentally dislodges the net causing a stoppage of play"*** — the NHL sentence with the punctuation changed, third limb and all. If you play under IIHF rules, which is what British league hockey runs on at every level, treat the NHL treatment above as your own. **England Ice Hockey does not amend any of it** — the In-House Rules contain nothing against Rules 81, 82 or 87.

**There is one genuine IIHF/NHL split inside the substitution bar — but read the caveat under it before you use it.** Both books allow a team that has iced the puck to substitute for a pulled goalkeeper, an injured player, or a penalty that changes on-ice strength. **The IIHF adds a fourth**: Rule 81.4 permits a substitution *"to replace a goalkeeper who had been substituted for an extra attacker, to replace an injured Player, **a Player with a broken skate**, or when a penalty has been assessed which affects the on-ice strength of either team."* NHL Rule 81.4 is the same sentence with those four words absent — set side by side, that clause is the **only** difference between the two rules, and IIHF 81.4 is word-for-word identical across the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions.

**⚠️ But the IIHF book states the icing substitution bar twice, and the two statements differ — so do not plan a change around the fourth exception.** Rule 82.1's later paragraph restates the bar for a team in violation of *"Rule 63.8 – Delaying the Game or Rule 81 – Icing"*, and its list is *"to replace a goalkeeper who had been substituted for an extra attacker, to replace an injured Player, or when a penalty has been assessed which affects the 'on-ice strength' of either team"* — **the other three, and not the broken skate.** The NHL has no such mismatch: its 81.4 and 82.1 carry the same three. **Which of the IIHF's two lists controls an icing has not been published.** 81.4 is the icing-specific rule, triggered by icing alone, where 82.1 covers delay of game as well — but the book does not rank them, and the IIHF's own Situation Handbook reads the two **together** rather than choosing between them: Situation 81.8 answers a substitution question *"Both teams must replace their penalized players but may not make any other substitutions. Rule 81.4 and Rule 82.1"*, citing both as one authority. **No situation in either Handbook edition names a broken skate**, so a search by phrase finds nothing here — but that is not the same as silence, and one answer cuts against the exception without naming it. ⚠️ **Situation 81.7** puts a lengthy broken-glass delay after an icing and answers: *"No. **Only** time a line change is permitted is when a penalty is assessed which affects the on-ice strength of either team. Rule 81.4."* **That is adverse authority on the size of 81.4's list, and it turns on the word the case for the exception turns on** — *only*, which appears in neither rule. **Read it as over-broad rather than as a ruling:** the same sentence also excludes the injured player and the returning pulled goalkeeper, both of which 81.4 expressly permits, so it is a sentence shortened for a question about a delay rather than a considered narrowing of the list. Situation 81.8, quoted above, shortens the same way — *"may not make any other substitutions"* — and so do Situations 81.6 and 82.1, which say a team that iced the puck is *"only permitted to make a substitution for the penalized player."* **It reads identically in the 2025/26 v1.0 and v1.1 Handbooks and in the 2026/27 one.** None of it settles which of the two rules governs; all of it is one more reason not to plan on the fourth exception. Both **rulebook** editions read the same way, and **the In-House Rules amend neither rule** — checked in the 2025-26 and 2026-27 editions, neither of which contains any Rule 81 or Rule 82 substitution material — so nothing changes it for a British reader. So treat the exception as something to ask for rather than something to rely on: **tell the official the blade has gone, and expect to be kept on the ice.** **And do not send a replacement over the boards on it.** Neither the IIHF nor the NHL writes a penalty into the icing substitution bar itself — **Hockey Canada does, and it is set out in the paragraph below** — but under those two a change attempted after the referee has closed the change window is caught by **IIHF Rule 82.2(V)**: the referee *"will send the Players who have attempted to change back to their Players' bench"* and then warns the coach that *"any subsequent violations during the rest of the game (including overtime), shall result in a bench minor penalty for 'delaying the game'"* — announced as *"Delay of Game – Improper Line Change."* **That warning is one per team per game and it is not reserved for this**: it covers every violation of 82.2(V), so a bench warned earlier for anything else has already spent it and the next attempt is the bench minor. It does not lapse late in the game either — **82.2(VIII) suspends only points (vi) and (vii)** in the last two minutes and in overtime, and the Situation Handbook confirms as much at Situation 82.5, which has the officials enforce *"points 82.2 (I) through (V)"* there. ⚠️ **Under the NHL it may be the bench minor with no warning at all, and the NHL book disagrees with itself about it.** NHL 82.2(v) is the IIHF's warning-first sentence word for word, but **NHL Rule 82.3's opening paragraph** reads *"Any attempt by the visiting team to make a change after the Referee's signal shall result in the assessment of a bench minor penalty for delay of game"* — and the book does not say which of its two provisions governs a visiting team. **The IIHF has no counterpart to that sentence**; its own 82.3 opens on last change instead, and puts a warning first.

**USA Hockey allows a line change after an icing, and a timeout.** Nothing in Rule 624 restricts substitution, Rule 204 permits a change at every stoppage, and Rule 636(f) attaches no icing condition to the one 60-second timeout each team gets — **though 636(f) gives no timeout at all in a curfew game**.

⚠️ **Do not read that as a free change, though. USA Hockey ties no substitution bar to icing, and it still closes the change window at every stoppage — with a warning and then a penalty behind it.** Rule 204(a) gives the visiting team five seconds to substitute and then the home team five, and then shuts the window: *"Once the line change procedure has been completed, no additional player substitutions shall be permitted until the face-off has been completed legally and play has resumed, except when a penalty or penalties are imposed that affect the on-ice strength of either or both teams."* **Two separate provisions enforce that, at two different penalty tiers, and both give a warning first.** The second paragraph of **Rule 204(a)** reads *"If there is any undue delay by either team in placing their lineup on the ice or changing players, the Referee shall warn the offending team. Any subsequent delay by the same team will result in a minor penalty for delay of game."* **Rule 610(g)**, inside *Delaying the Game*, reads *"A bench minor penalty shall be assessed to any team, after warning by the Referee, that fails to place the correct number of players on the ice and commences play or that causes any delay by making additional substitutions or attempts to delay the game in any manner."* So a minor under one and a bench minor under the other, and neither before a warning has been given. ⚠️ **204(a) does not say who serves its minor** — the offender it names is *"the offending team"* — so do not assume it lands on the player who went over the boards. **The book names the offence, too**: the *Summary of Penalties* in both the Playing Rules and the *Casebook* lists it as *"Continued improper line change during stoppages"* and *"Continued incorrect players on ice"*. And USA Hockey's *Casebook* puts the same on-ice remedy on an attempted late change as the IIHF does: at **Rule 613, Situation 15**, a false face-off occurs and either team then tries to change — *"No. … The Referee must order all players attempting to change back to the players' bench. If there is any delay involved in getting the original line-up back on the ice, the Referee may assess a minor penalty for delaying the game, in which case substitutions shall be permitted."* ⚠️ **One caution on the citation, because the book's own apparatus is out by a letter.** The *Casebook* cites this provision as *"610(h)"* and so does each Summary of Penalties, but the printed Rule 610 runs **(a) to (g)** and there is no (h) — the bench minor is **610(g)**, and that is the number to quote to an official. **The practical position: you may change after your own icing under USA Hockey, and you may not change late. Late is the part that costs you.**

**Hockey Canada sits between the two**: 6.7(d) bars the change in U18AAA, Junior and Senior-at-the-Member's-option, and nowhere else, with carve-outs for replacing a goaltender pulled for an extra attacker, replacing an injured player, and any stoppage where a penalty was assessed. In those same three categories 6.7(d)(iii) expressly allows the timeout, with the icing team still barred from changing during it; below them Hockey Canada's 30-second timeout exists in minor and female hockey only in **stop-time** games and only *"wherever the local Member gives approval"*, though Note 3 to Rule 6.17(e) makes it mandatory at Hockey Canada minor and female regional and national championships. **Senior is at the Member's option, so if you play adult Hockey Canada hockey, assume you may not change until somebody tells you otherwise.** Rule 6.7(d) attaches no penalty of its own, but the book states one elsewhere: **Rule 10.1 – Delay of Game names this situation expressly**, listing *"a team which, after a warning by the Referee, fails to place the correct number of players on the ice and commence play"*, and saying in terms that this *"includes 'no change' situations"* where *"the offending team attempts to make a change"* — cross-referring to Rule 6.1(f), which is the no-change-on-icing rule by name. 10.1(a) makes deliberate delay by a team a **bench minor**. So there is a warning first, by the rule's own words. Do not plan on spending it: a second change in a single stoppage is a bench minor under 6.1(c) with no warning at all. This is a real difference in how tired you are allowed to be.

### What this means for how you play

**Icing is the tax on a bad clear.** Getting the puck out of your zone matters, but getting it out *legally* matters more when you are tired, because the punishment for an illegal clear is a defensive-zone draw with your worst five players stuck on the ice and no way to rest.

- **From your own end under pressure**, the safe clear is **off the glass and out** along the boards or **high off the glass** — the puck leaves the zone, no icing, and it is very hard to intercept. A rim around the boards that carries all the way down the ice is what gets iced.
- **Get the puck to the red line before you dump it.** One extra stride is the whole difference between a legal dump-in and an icing.
- **On the penalty kill you may ice it freely** (Rule 81.6) — subject to the two exceptions immediately above, the NHL's empty-penalty-bench major and USA Hockey's classification restriction. Where it applies, shorthanded teams should use it ruthlessly: it is the single biggest structural advantage the kill has. See [Special Teams](../systems/special_teams.md).
- **As the defending team chasing a dump-in under hybrid icing**, the race is to **the puck** — but the linesperson's judgement is locked in by the time the first skate reaches the dot, so the first half of the race is the half that counts. Beating your opponent to the dot does not by itself decide it; being clearly on course to reach the puck first does. If you are clearly beaten, do not run yourself into the end boards for nothing; if you are clearly winning it, commit to retrieving it. And a race still too close to call at the dot is icing.
- **As the goalie, be careful behind your net on a potential icing.** Under NHL and IIHF rules, merely **skating toward the puck waves the icing off** and gives the other team a live puck in your corner (Rule 81.3). If you want the icing, stay put. The exception is when you are legitimately heading to the bench for an extra attacker and make no attempt on the puck — that does not wave it off.
- **Do not casually poke a dumped puck as it goes by.** Any touch by the defending team, including the goalie, kills the icing. On the other hand, that is exactly the trick to use when the *other* team is stuck and you would rather keep playing than take the faceoff.
- **Boarding note:** unnecessary contact with a player playing the puck on an obvious icing or offside play that drives him into the boards is explicitly **boarding** (Rule 41.1), and the clause is verbatim in three books (NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1, Hockey Canada 7.2). **USA Hockey reaches only the icing half**, at 624(d), which penalises *"unnecessary contact with an opponent who is attempting to play the puck in an obvious icing situation"* — no offside limb, and it does not name boarding. Icing races are not a licence to hit.

---

## 4. Penalties

### The types, and what each does to your team

| Type | Time | Does your team play short? | Substitute allowed? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Minor** | 2:00 | Yes | No |
| **Bench minor** | 2:00 | Yes — any player serves it | No |
| **Double minor** | 4:00 | Yes | No |
| **Major** | 5:00 | Yes | No |
| **Misconduct** | 10:00 | **No** | Yes, immediately |
| **Game misconduct** | Rest of game | **No** | Yes |
| **Match penalty** | Rest of game + 5:00 served | Yes, for 5:00 | Yes, after 5 minutes |
| **Penalty shot** | — | No | — |

**Minor penalty** (Rule 16.1) — two minutes, no substitute. Your team plays 5-on-4.

**Bench minor** (Rule 17.1) — two minutes served by **any player except the goalkeeper**, designated by the coach through the captain. Used for team infractions with no single culprit: too many men, delay of game by the bench, and a **second** faceoff violation by the same team on the same draw.

**Double minor** (Rule 18.1) — four minutes. Used for high-sticking that draws blood (Rule 60.3) — **that is the NHL's tier; the other three books reach a major and a game misconduct, and the NHL itself has a match penalty at 60.4 — see the high-sticking bullet below**; and butt-ending or spearing attempts (58.2, 62.2) — **NHL tiers, and the IIHF and Hockey Canada give a double minor for those attempts too. USA Hockey is the one book of four that does not: see the stick-foul bullets below.** It behaves as two separate two-minute penalties: if the other team scores in the first two minutes, only that half is washed out and the player serves the remaining two (Rule 18.2).

**Major** (Rule 20.1) — five minutes, no substitute, and — this is the key difference from a minor — **the penalty does not end when the other team scores** (Rule 20.2). The full five minutes are served no matter how many goals go in. A **third major in one game is an automatic game misconduct** (NHL Rule 20.4), and a major that injures an opponent's face or head brings a game misconduct with it. 🇬🇧 **Both of those are NHL wording, and the IIHF ejects you sooner.** IIHF Rule 20.4 sets the trigger at the **second**: *"An 'Automatic Game Misconduct' shall be applied to any Player who has been assessed a second major penalty, **or a second misconduct penalty in the same game**."* Two ten-minute misconducts are a trigger the NHL book does not have at all — IIHF Rule 22.1 repeats it in the misconduct rule, and NHL Rule 22 has no equivalent sentence — and the IIHF rule carries no face-or-head-injury clause. **One major can be enough, and that is true in both books**: NHL 20.4 and IIHF 20.4 each add an automatic game misconduct *"whenever a player is assessed a major penalty for any of the infractions listed in the Reference Tables"* — the NHL's Table 5, the IIHF's Table 6 — **that IIHF number is the 2025/26 book's, and the 2026/27 edition merges its Tables 5, 6 and 7 into one Table 5 carrying the same entries**. **The IIHF's is unconditional — meaning no injury is required, though whether to call the major at all is still the referee's judgement — and Table 6 lists twenty** — three of them fighting entries, so against the NHL's nineteen rather than its thirteen non-fighting rows — checking from behind, cross-checking, slashing, hooking, high-sticking, slew-footing, clipping and an illegal check to the head among them. **The NHL's is neither the same list nor unconditional**, and the book flags the tension itself: 20.4's second sentence attaches no condition, while six of its thirteen non-fighting rows require an injury, three of those to the face or head. Table 5 carries a footnote — keyed to two asterisked cells rather than printed under the whole table, and which two rows they sit on does not survive the text extraction — *"The individual rules state that the automatic game misconduct only applies if major is assessed due to an injury. However, a major penalty cannot be assessed on its own."* It writes no major at all for high-sticking, slew-footing or an illegal check to the head. **All three reach a *match penalty* there instead** — 60.4, 52.2 and 48.5 — and **52.2's match penalty for slew-footing is mandatory and unconditional**. ⚠️ **60.4 requires that a player *"attempts to or deliberately injures"*; 48.5 is discretionary and narrower still. A merely reckless illegal check to the head reaches no tier above the minor on the ice.** §10 sets the two books' triggers side by side.

  **So under the book British hockey runs on, your second major of the night ends your game, and so do two misconducts — but the twenty are not your only first-major risk.** IIHF **Table 7** — the 2025/26 book's number, merged with Tables 5 and 6 into the 2026/27 edition's single Table 5, where these five are the rows ticked in **both** its *"MAJOR ONLY"* and *"MAJOR + GMP"* columns — is headed *"Summary of major penalties that result in a game misconduct based on the Referee's judgement"*, and it lists **boarding (41.4), charging (42.4), elbowing (45.4), interference (56.5) and tripping (57.4)**. On any of those the referee may end your game on the **first** major. Below adult level in Britain it is stricter again, and may bite on the *first* major: [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md) covers the In-House Rules' junior and women's application of IIHF Rule 201.1, and the hedge that goes with it. Each of the Table 7 five is written as a single major-plus-game-misconduct act — 41.4 reads *"may assess a **major and a game misconduct penalty**."* Table 7's heading rests it on the referee's judgement, so this is not beyond doubt; **play as though the ejection follows the major.** Under the IIHF book all majors other than a Rule 46 fighting situation are reviewed on-ice by video (Rule 20.6) — 46.5 sucker-punching being the one fighting call that *can* be reviewed. ⚠️ **Not in Britain: In-House Rule 37 removes on-ice video review from EIH and SIHA competition entirely** — *"and may not be used by referees, official scorers, timekeepers, or any other game official"* — and the In-House Rules repeat the bar in nine further provisions across eight other rules, 18.4 among them. There is no second look during the game; the call on the ice is the one that ejects you.

**Misconduct** (Rule 22.1) — ten minutes for the player, but a **substitute goes straight on**, so **the team does not play shorthanded** (Rule 22.3). ⚠️ **The two books word 22.3 differently, and the difference is a tier:** the IIHF's reads *"unless they also receive a minor or major penalty"*, the NHL's *"a minor, major or match penalty"* — the match penalty being the tier the IIHF book does not have at all. This is the most commonly misunderstood penalty in hockey: a ten-minute misconduct costs your team nothing on the scoreboard directly, it costs *you* a third of the game. A player whose misconduct expires stays in the box until the next stoppage. 🇬🇧 **A second misconduct in the same game is not another ten minutes — it ends your game**, under the IIHF (Rule 22.1: *"An 'Automatic Game Misconduct' shall be applied to any Player who has been assessed a second (2nd) misconduct penalty in the same game"*) and under USA Hockey (Rule 404(a): *"For the second misconduct penalty assessed to the same player during the same game, a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to that player in lieu of the second misconduct penalty"*). **The NHL book contains no such trigger.**

**Game misconduct** (Rule 23.1) — the player is gone for the balance of the game, a substitute replaces him, and **the team is not shorthanded**. Ten minutes go on his record and the incident is reported for possible supplementary discipline. 🇬🇧 **The IIHF's figure is not ten but twenty** — its 23.1 reads *"Twenty (20) minutes are applied in the records to the Player incurring a game misconduct penalty"*, against the NHL's *"Ten minutes are applied in the league records."*

**Match penalty** (Rule 21.1) — the most severe on-ice sanction. Imposed on **any player who deliberately attempts to injure, or deliberately injures, an opponent**. The player is sent to the dressing room immediately, **five minutes are served by a designated teammate**, and the player is automatically suspended until the league has ruled. Like majors, match penalties are reviewed on-ice (Rule 21.5). ⚠️ **If you play in Britain, this penalty does not exist for you.** The IIHF book writes no match penalty at all. So where a bullet below names a match penalty as the ceiling, your ceiling is **the major plus automatic game misconduct in that same rule** — 60.4 for high-sticking, 48.3 for a check to the head — and *not* IIHF Rule 21, which is *Dangerous Actions* and reaches only *"an action that is not covered by the playing rules"*. The effect on the ice is the same either way: off for the balance of the game, five minutes served by a teammate. What you do not inherit is the NHL's automatic suspension until the league has ruled — the IIHF refers the case onward instead. **The onward route runs to a separate book, reproduced nowhere in the Rule Book and not consulted here.** IIHF Rules 23.3 to 23.6 name four disciplinary-measure categories — General, Abuse of Officials, Stick Infractions, Physical Infractions — and each says only that *"The respective process is outlined in the IIHF Disciplinary Code."* Rules 40.2 to 40.4 do the same for abuse of officials, under the headings *"Supplementary Discipline – Suspension – Category I, II and III"*: they define the **conduct** in each category and set **no suspension length for any of them**, and 40.5 adds only the process, by which the referees decide the category after the game and the case goes to IIHF Disciplinary. The headings are in the book; the tariff is not. **Do not read that as leniency.** IIHF Rule 20.7 requires the referee to report **every** major penalty, and the In-House Rules give **the EIH Department of Player Safety or the SIHA Disciplinary Committee, whichever is your home association's**, power to impose further suspensions *"regardless of whether such offences were penalised by the Referee at the time."* Not automatic; also not off the table.

**Penalty shot** (Rule 24) — not a time penalty at all but a restored scoring chance. The puck is placed at centre ice and the designated player skates in alone. Rules that catch people out: **no rebounds** (except off post or crossbar and then off the goalie straight in); the puck must be **kept in motion toward the goal**; the **spin-o-rama is banned**; a lacrosse-style "Michigan" **is** permitted provided the puck is never raised above shoulder height and is not released above the crossbar; the goalie must **stay in his crease until the shooter touches the puck**. If the shot is awarded for what would have been a minor, **no minor is served regardless of the outcome** (Rule 24.6).

**Coincidental penalties** (Rule 19) — when equal penalties are given to both teams, they cancel for on-ice strength. But there is a subtlety worth knowing: **one minor to each team at the same stoppage, with nothing else on the clock, is played 4-on-4** (Rule 19.1). If a team is already shorthanded, the coincidental minors are served with immediate substitution and the strength stays as it was. 🇬🇧 **That is the NHL rule.** IIHF Rule 19.1 requires immediate substitution for an equal number of minors and the teams stay five a side — so in Britain this particular situation is 5-on-5. Britain still reaches 4-on-4 by other routes at the same stoppage.

### Stick fouls

These are the calls that come from using the stick as a tool against a player instead of against the puck. As a group they are the most-called infractions in adult recreational hockey, for one reason: **they are what a tired or beaten player does instead of skating.**

- **Hooking** — **NHL Rule 55**, IIHF Rule 55, USA Hockey Rule 623, Hockey Canada Rule 8.2; the numbers do not travel. Using the stick to **restrain** an opponent. ⚠️ **The two things you have probably been told are both unsafe.** *"Only stick-on-stick, so it cannot be hooking"* — the NHL and IIHF do exempt stick-to-stick contact, but only *"when a player is checking another"*, and **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada both name the opponent's stick as a hookable surface.** And *"no tugging motion, so it cannot be hooking"* — the tug is in USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's definitions and **appears nowhere in NHL Rule 55**, while Rule 56.1 in the NHL and IIHF reaches a player *"who does not have body position on his opponent"* and uses the stick *"to impede or prevent his opponent from moving freely on the ice"* — the NHL's wording, the IIHF's the same but for its pronouns — with no tug at all. ⚠️ **A stick on the hands is hooking in three books of four — but only the IIHF's is flat about it.** USA Hockey 623 needs the contact to take *"away the ability for the opponent to pass or shoot the puck with a normal amount of force"*, and **Hockey Canada expressly permits the stick lift itself**: Interpretation 1 to 8.2(a) allows using your stick to lift an opponent's *"for the purpose of making a play on the puck"* and penalises it only where it contacts the hands *"and impedes their ability to pass, shoot, receive, or otherwise propel the puck."* **USA Hockey permits it too, and on a broader footing** — asked whether a player may lift an opponent's stick without being penalised under the Standard of Play guidelines, its Casebook answers *"Yes. A player may lift the opponent's stick at any time provided they do not impede their progress"*, and calls it *"a good defensive play"* (Appendix VI, Standard of Play Casebook, Situation 2). ⚠️ **The two permissions are conditioned on different things:** Hockey Canada's turns on your *purpose* — a play on the puck — and USA Hockey's on the *effect*, that you do not impede their progress. **And neither is permission to take it at the hands.** The same Casebook, at Rule 623, Situation 1, is asked whether a player who *"hooks the stick of another player causing them to lose possession of the puck"* has done something legal and answers *"No"* — *"the fact they hooked the stick indicates that they impeded the opponent's progress"* — before allowing that *"a stick lift or stick check where the focus is on the puck and preventing the opponent from playing the puck are both considered good defensive plays."* ⚠️ **So take the lift lower:** blade under their shaft **below their bottom hand**, lifted up rather than sideways — that is what keeps it off the hands. **And lift; do not chop.** USA Hockey's Standard of Play singles out *"hard slashes to the upper portion of the stick (just below the hands) of an opponent, with no attempt to legally play the puck"* for strict enforcement — the same few inches of shaft you are aiming at. ⚠️ **And lift under their shaft, not through it.** "Up rather than sideways" is the right cue and it is also the direction the danger lies in, because the stick you are lifting ends near their face. **Hockey Canada writes two escalations for this exact technique**, in the list of interpretations printed under Rule 9.5: an *"attempted lifting of the opponent's stick, which results in accidental contact to the head or neck area"* and **causes injury** is a **double Minor**. And the second covers *"any aggressive, reckless, negligent or forceful action of the stick (e.g. pitchfork action in lifting the stick or hooking an opponent) that results in contact to the neck or head area, causing injury, will result in a Major penalty and Game Misconduct being assessed."* **Injury is the trigger on both limbs**, and the second names the pitchfork lift by name. ⚠️ **Be careful about the scope, because the book is less explicit than it looks.** That list states no division of its own. What surrounds it does: it is printed inside **Rule 9.5, titled *High-Sticking – Junior/Senior***, whose (a) opens *"In Junior and Senior divisions"* and whose two numbered Interpretations both confine their rulings to those divisions in terms. The major limb assesses its penalty under *"Rule 7.7 – Head Contact – Junior/Senior"*. **Reading the list as Junior/Senior is therefore an inference from its setting, not a scope the list itself states** — defensible, and worth knowing it is an inference. ⚠️ **And it is not relief if you play minor or female hockey**, where **Rule 7.6 – Head Contact** governs instead and is no softer. It opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"*, and says it *"supersedes any other rules"* except fighting. At 7.6(b) it reaches a major plus a game misconduct both at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of the impact"* — **with nobody hurt** — and **mandatorily** where a head-contact infraction that would otherwise be a minor injures the opponent. A third paragraph in the same clause makes that pair mandatory for a cross-check above the shoulders *"whether or not injury results"*. And 7.6(a) puts a **double Minor** on intentional contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body or equipment, with a **Game Ejection** on a third penalty under 7.6(a). **And a minor is not the ceiling.** The NHL needs an injury for its major; **the IIHF does not**, reaching a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment with nobody hurt, which is the British reader's exposure. **USA Hockey 623(b) makes that mandatory on the same no-injury trigger** and 623(c) reaches a match penalty; **Hockey Canada 8.2(b)'s mandatory major turns on injury instead**, and 8.2(c) bars a match penalty outright. *Read your own book's tiers before you rely on any of this.* See [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md), which covers the stick-lift technique and its cues.
- **Slashing** (Rule 61) — swinging the stick at an opponent, **contact or not**. Any **forceful chop on the body, on the opponent's stick, or on or near the hands** that is not an attempt to play the puck is slashing — that enumeration is the NHL's and the IIHF's, with USA Hockey carrying a shortened form of it. **Hockey Canada's 9.3 defines slashing only as *"hitting an opponent with a stick while holding the stick with one or both hands"*** and enumerates nothing. ⚠️ **The shin-pad carve-out is not.** *"Non-aggressive stick contact to the pant or front of the shin pads should not be penalized as slashing"* is NHL 61.1 and IIHF 61.1. **USA Hockey writes no such exemption** and instructs referees to penalise any player who *"swings their stick at any opposing player (whether or not contact is made)"*. And **Hockey Canada's substitute is narrower** — it exempts *"tapping the stick of the **puck-carrier**"* for the sole purpose of gaining the puck — not a tap on the body, and not a tap on a player who does not have it. ⚠️ **The ceilings diverge sharply.** The NHL **must** assess a major where injury occurs (61.3), with a game misconduct mandatory alongside any slashing major (61.5) and a match penalty at 61.4. **The IIHF has no mandatory-major-on-injury clause at all**, reaching a major plus an automatic game misconduct only for reckless endangerment (61.3), and no match penalty. USA Hockey 634(b) is mandatory for reckless endangerment with a match at 634(c). **Hockey Canada 9.3(b) runs on two limbs, and the first of them needs nobody hurt either** — a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, and that same pair again where a slash that would otherwise be a minor injures. A match penalty sits at 9.3(c), and 9.3(d) and 9.3(e) mean **no misconduct may be assessed for slashing but a game misconduct must accompany any major.** Two USA Hockey limbs worth knowing: 634(d) reaches a slash *"in the course of an altercation"* on top of the fighting rule. And **634(e) is a minor for stick contact with a goalkeeper who has *"covered or caught"* the puck in their crease, *"regardless of whether or not the Referee has stopped play"*** — the whack at a smothered puck. ⚠️ **Hockey Canada adds the case in which a slash most often becomes serious, in junior and senior hockey.** Its interpretations to Rule 9.5 — its *High-Sticking – Junior/Senior* rule — make *"a slashing action [that] glances off the upper body and deflects upwards to make contact with the head or neck area, causing injury"* a **major plus a game misconduct** under Rule 7.7. In minor and female hockey the route is 7.6.
- **Tripping** — **NHL Rule 57**, IIHF Rule 57, USA Hockey Rule 639, Hockey Canada Rule 8.6; the numbers do not travel, and the rule sets differ more here than almost anywhere. Placing your stick or your body so as to trip an opponent. **Five things a reader should carry, and one to look up; the rest belongs in your own book.** ⚠️ **A stumble that never becomes a fall is still a penalty.** The NHL and the IIHF each define tripping as *"trip **or** fall"* and then write their minor for *"trip **and** fall"* — an unresolved conflict inside those two books, and no interpretation available here settles it. The IIHF Situation Handbook's four Rule 57 situations, 57.1 to 57.4, are all penalty-shot questions, and none of them reaches the point. The NHL's minor *"shall be imposed"*, where the IIHF's is *"at their discretion… based on the severity of the infraction."* **USA Hockey's own Casebook settles for that book what the other two leave open**, at Rule 639, Situation 1: *"For a tripping penalty to be assessed, must the fouled player fall to the ice? No. … it must be clearly obvious their progress was impeded and a competitive advantage was gained as a result of the tripping action."* *(That situation describes its rule as using "trip or fall". USA Hockey Rule 639, Note 1 in fact reads "lose balance or fall" — which does not change the answer.)* And Hockey Canada penalises *"any player who trips an opposing player"*. **Treat the stumble as a penalty.** ⚠️ **Touching the puck first does not save you the minor in the NHL or the IIHF.** It saves you the penalty shot; the minor still stands. ⚠️ **But those two books write one relief the other two do not, and it runs the opposite way from the rest of this bullet.** NHL 57.1 and IIHF 57.1 carry the same two sentences word for word — *"Accidental trips which occur simultaneously with a completed play will not be penalized. Accidental trips occurring simultaneously with or after a stoppage of play will not be penalized."* **Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes any equivalent.** USA Hockey and Hockey Canada do give the puck-first relief, on separate terms set out under [Common Mistakes](#common-mistakes) — but **in neither book does it reach slew-footing**, which is the mechanism that puts the other player's head on the ice, and the two books draw the boundary in different places. **Hockey Canada's relief stops at its own rule:** 8.6 says in terms that it *"does not apply to actions defined under Rule 8.7 – Clipping or Rule 8.8 – Slew-Footing"*, so a clip is outside it. **USA Hockey's Rule 639 is titled *Tripping/Clipping/Leg Checking/Slew Footing* and covers all four together**, so its relief — withheld *"under this rule"* — can reach a clip. What it cannot reach is slew footing, which 639(a) excepts outright and whose **minimum** under 639(b) is a major plus a game misconduct. Its Casebook is blunt about that floor, at Rule 639, Situation 5: *"A minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing."* ⚠️ **Hockey Canada tiers the same act at the opposite end, and its double minor for slew-footing is a floor rather than a ceiling. A reader who takes it as a ceiling will misjudge both books.** Rule 8.8(a) assesses the double minor and 8.8(b), (d) and (e) then forbid the major, the misconduct and the game misconduct — *"No Major penalty may be assessed for slew-footing"* — **but 8.8(c) goes straight past the forbidden major to a match penalty**, at the referee's discretion on the *"degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice, goal, or boards)"*, and **mandatory** for *"any player who injures an opponent by a slew-footing infraction"*. So slew-footing is a major plus a game misconduct at its lowest under USA Hockey, and under Hockey Canada either four minutes or a match penalty. ⚠️ **Back to the plain trip, and here only the NHL has no ejection tier.** Rule 57 stops at a minor. The IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada all reach a major plus a game misconduct, so **a British reader can be thrown out for a trip in a way an NHL viewer never sees** — and even the NHL gets there through Rule 21.1 for a player who *"deliberately attempts to injure… in any manner."* The reason those tiers run so high is not the trip itself: **a player tripped at speed may be unable to protect himself and can go into the ice or the boards head first**, which is why Hockey Canada grades a clip on the degree of violence *"including the impact with the ice or boards"* and USA Hockey 639(b) turns on a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of tripping, clipping or leg checking."* *And the thing to look up is the one exemption most players assume they have: only the NHL and IIHF write an **accidental**-trip exemption at all, and only for a trip simultaneous with a completed play, or *"simultaneously with or after"* a stoppage. USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no accident exemption — which is a different question from the puck-first relief above, and they do not answer it the same way.* **Get the puck, not the man.**
- **High-sticking** (Rule 60) — contacting an opponent **above the shoulders** with your stick. ⚠️ **Some books add a floor to that — that the opponent's shoulders be at waist level or higher — and which IIHF book you are under decides whether yours does.** The NHL has it twice: 60.1 defines a high stick as one which *"contacts an opponent above the shoulders, provided their shoulders are at waist level or higher"*, and 60.3 assesses the double minor for contact with the *"neck, face or head (above the height of the opponent's waist)"*. **The IIHF Official Rule Book 2026/27 carries both of those sentences in the same words.** The **IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** carries neither. Its 60.1 tests a stick *"carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"*, its 60.3 a Player who *"carries or holds any part of their stick above the shoulders and makes contact"*, and the word "waist" appears **once in that entire book**, about goalkeeper pant padding. It appears **four** times in the 2026/27 book, at 60.1, at 60.3, in the same padding rule, and in that book's own list of rule changes. ⚠️ **And 60.3 changed in the opposite direction to 60.1 between the editions, so do not carry one direction across to the other.** The 2025/26 double minor has two limbs and needs both: the Player must *"carr[y] or hold[] any part of their stick above the shoulders"* **and** make the contact with the neck, face or head. The 2026/27 clause deletes the carry limb and asks only where the contact landed. **So under the newer book a stick you never raised above shoulder height can still cost you four minutes, where under the older one that clause does not reach it at all.** The 60.1 change narrows what counts as a high stick; the 60.3 change widens what the double minor catches — and a reader who has just learned the first will carry the wrong direction into the second. (Neither 60.3 says whose shoulders its carry limb is measured against; 60.1 measures a high stick against the opponent's.) 🇬🇧 **Which of the two is yours in Britain is worth asking rather than assuming**, because the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 name both. The cover says *"the **Current** IIHF Rule Book"*, the Introduction *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"* — and Version 1.1 of July 2025 **is** the 2025/26 book. See [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md). **USA Hockey Rule 621 and Hockey Canada Rule 9.5 write no *waist* floor in either case — but neither writes a bare "above the shoulders" test either.** Both measure against the *"normal height of the shoulders"*: USA Hockey's Rule 621 Note against *"the opponent's"*, Hockey Canada at 9.5 and again at 9.5(a). ⚠️ **Whether "normal" means the shoulders where they actually are or the shoulders of a player standing upright is a question neither book answers for these rules, so do not read it either way.** Hockey Canada defines the phrase exactly once in the whole book — Rule 6.9(a) Note 1, *"The 'normal' height of the shoulders refers to when a player is standing fully upright"* — but 6.9 is high-sticking **the puck**, and the book nowhere says that definition travels to 9.5; USA Hockey defines *"normal"* nowhere at all. **And the floor is in no book's 60.2** — the clause that actually imposes the minor, word-for-word identical in the NHL book and in both IIHF editions: *"Any contact made by a stick on an opponent above the shoulders is prohibited and a minor penalty shall be imposed."* So **do not plan a stick on a crouching opponent on the strength of the floor**. ⚠️ **What reaches a crouching opponent in the two North American books is head-contact law rather than the high-sticking rule, and that law does not turn on stick height at all.** USA Hockey Rule 620 makes head contact *"the action of a player contacting an opponent in the head, face or neck with any part of the player's body, equipment or stick"*, puts the onus *"on the player delivering the check"*, and starts at a minor **plus a misconduct** (620(a)); Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"* and requires that *"[r]egardless of the circumstances, players must be penalized for contact with an opponent's head, face, or neck at all times."* **And no IIHF edition protects a bent-over opponent outright either:** 60.1 permits *"accidental contact"* on a centre bent over at a face-off in both editions. ⚠️ **Where the floor does apply, the waist that decides it is the struck player's, not yours.** Both of the 60.3 clauses that carry it read *"above the height of the opponent's waist"*. The **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** puts the case at **Situation 60.13** — a stick-lift attempt that misses a player bent over digging the puck out of the corner and catches them in the face, the striker's own stick *"only slightly above the height of their waist"*. The answer is *"Yes, this is a minor (or double-minor) penalty for high-sticking. The key here is that the opposing player was hit in the neck/face/head with the opponent's stick, at or above the height of the waist of the player being struck."* That Situation is in the 2026/27 Handbook and not in the 2025/26 one, and the Handbook ranks itself below the rule book on its own Foreword. **A stick below your own waist is a defence in no book.** ⚠️ **But do not read Hockey Canada Rule 9.5 as its general high-sticking rule — it is titled "High-Sticking – Junior/Senior" and 9.5(a) opens "In Junior and Senior divisions".** Hockey Canada has no general high-sticking rule at all. In **minor and female hockey** contact above the shoulders falls to **Rule 7.6 – Head Contact**, which the book says *"supersedes any other rules"* bar fighting, and which is considerably harsher than a minor. Its 7.6(a) gives a minor for accidental contact, a **double minor** for intentional contact, and a **game ejection on a third** head-contact penalty **in the same game** (4.8(b)). A **normal windup or follow-through** on a shot is permitted, and so is accidental contact on a centre bent over at a faceoff — **but only under the NHL and IIHF books, which carry both carve-outs in the same words (IIHF 60.1). Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes either one, and neither does CARHA Hockey**, and Hockey Canada expressly reverses the follow-through: Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.5(a) assesses a minor where a follow-through hits an opponent above the shoulder with no injury. **A wild swing at a bouncing puck is never permitted** — the carve-out covers a normal shooting motion and nothing wilder than one. **Injury (drawing blood or otherwise) makes it a double minor** (Rule 60.3). ⚠️ **A double minor is not the ceiling in any book, including the NHL's.** What the NHL lacks is a *major* tier, not an ejection: **NHL Rule 60.4** assesses a **match penalty** where a player *"attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent with a high-stick"*. **Note that 60.4 is a different rule in each book** — the NHL's is that match penalty, the IIHF's is a major plus an automatic game misconduct. **IIHF Rule 60.4 (the British reader's book)** lets the referee assess *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"* where a player *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'high-sticking'"*. USA Hockey Rule 621(b) makes that major-plus-game-misconduct mandatory for reckless endangerment and 621(c) allows a match penalty. **Hockey Canada bars both tiers inside its high-sticking rule** — 9.5(b) *"No Major penalty may be assessed for high-sticking"*, and 9.5(c) the same for a match penalty — **and routes the case to its head-contact rules instead**: 7.7 in junior and senior, 7.6 in minor and female. **The two destinations differ, but they are mandatory on different triggers — neither is simply the harsher rule.** 7.6(b) is discretionary on violence (the referee *"may assess"* a major) and **mandatory on injury**: a major plus game misconduct *must* follow where head contact that would otherwise be a minor injures. 7.7(b) inverts that — **mandatory on violence**, since a major plus game misconduct *"will be assessed for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"* whether or not anyone is hurt — and it has no injury clause of 7.6(b)'s shape. Its injury limb requires a *head* injury caused by the glass, ice or boards — narrower in what it requires, but wider in what it reaches, since it covers **any** infraction where *"a player is fouled or hit illegally"* and not only head contact. One further difference is real: 7.7(a) with 7.7(d) makes the floor a minor *plus a misconduct*, where 7.6(a) starts at a bare minor and **7.6(d) bars the ten-minute *Misconduct* — not the ejection**, since 7.6(e) makes a game misconduct mandatory with any 7.6(b) major. But 7.7's preamble lets minimal-violence contact be penalised *"with a minor penalty for roughing, charging, or another appropriate penalty"* — an open list. **7.6 forecloses that leniency in terms**, requiring that *"[r]egardless of the circumstances"* head contact be penalised *"at all times"*. It requires too that *"any foul that results in contact to the head must be penalized as Head Contact."* **And two limbs distinguish nothing at all:** the cross-check clause is word-for-word the same in both. So is the match tier's first limb — **7.6(c) and 7.7(c) both reach a match penalty** for hitting a player *"in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves."* If anything **7.6(c) is the wider**, reaching a player who *"attempts to or deliberately injures"* where 7.7(c) reads *"deliberately attempts to injure"*. Note also what 9.5(b) and 9.5(c) do *not* say: they name 7.7 and nothing else, so the route to 7.6 is read from 7.6's own preamble, which *"supersedes any other rules"* bar fighting. The tier is relocated, not removed, and §10 has the row. ⚠️ **Whether carrying the stick high is punishable on its own is the second thing the two IIHF editions split on.** The NHL's 60.1 makes the test *contact* above the shoulders, and the **IIHF 2026/27** 60.1 makes it contact too, in the NHL's words. The **IIHF 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** 60.1 instead defines a high stick as one *"carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"*, with no contact element at all. And Hockey Canada's 9.5 preamble says a penalty *"may be assessed regardless of whether or not contact occurs."* Hockey Canada reads furthest on its face — **though 9.5(a), the only clause that actually assesses a penalty, requires contact, so the text does not settle it.** ⚠️ **And USA Hockey answers the question twice, in opposite directions.** Its playing rule requires contact: 621's Note defines high sticking as where a player *"carries the stick above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders **and** makes contact with the opponent"*. Its Casebook then asks *"Must contact with an opponent occur in order for the Referee to assess a penalty for high sticking?"*, answers *"No."*, and adds both that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"* and that the call must be made *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions"* (Rule 621, Situation 2). The two texts are printed in one volume, the *Official Rules and Casebook of Ice Hockey 2025-29*, which carries the playing rule word for word and then reads it wider in its situations. **This document records that rather than picking a side.** **Play it as though the carry alone can be penalised**, which is the safe reading under every book and the only reading under some — and under USA Hockey add that a stick catching an opponent *below* their shoulders can still be high-sticking. **And no IIHF edition requires contact throughout:** 60.2 and 60.3 each turn on contact in both — the 2025/26 60.3 on a carry as well, as above, while **60.4 does not in either** — its trigger is *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'high-sticking'"*. So the IIHF's minor and double minor need contact; its major may not.
- **Cross-checking** (Rule 59.1) — *"the action of using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent."* That is the complete NHL definition; **there is no blade-on-ice element in it.** You will often hear that a blade touching the ice means it cannot be a cross-check — that phrasing **is** part of the playing rule in USA Hockey. Its Rule 609 (Note) defines cross-checking as *"using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent **with no portion of the stick on the ice**"* — but it is absent from NHL Rule 59.1 and IIHF Rule 59.1. **The floor is a minor, and whether it is discretionary depends on your book.** NHL Rule 59.2 imposes one *"at the discretion of the Referee based on the severity of the contact"* — and **59.3 hands the referee the major on word-for-word that same test**, so under the NHL book the severity of the contact is the whole of what separates two minutes from five. **The IIHF writes its minor at the same number on a differently worded test:** its 59.2 has the referee assess one *"at their discretion"*, *"based on the degree of force and violence"*. **USA Hockey's is mandatory on its face** — 609(a) reads *"A minor penalty shall be assessed for cross-checking an opponent"*, with no severity element in the sentence — and **Hockey Canada's 9.2(a) is written the same way**, its threshold arriving only in the interpretation quoted below. A major for cross-checking carries an **automatic game misconduct** — and that much is true in all four books (NHL 59.5, IIHF 59.3, USA Hockey 609(b), Hockey Canada 9.2(e)). ⚠️ **But it is the wrong tier to learn alone, because Hockey Canada gives you no minor floor at all for a cross-check to the head.** Its 7.6(b) and 7.7(b) carry the same sentence word for word, with **no division scope in either**. It reads: *"Any player who strikes an opponent above the normal height of their shoulders with a cross-check will be penalized with a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty, whether or not injury results."* Its Interpretation 2 to 9.2(b) says that penalty **must** be assessed. The narrowing matters as much as the tier, and it is narrower than it sounds. Interpretation 2 to 7.6(b) says the rule *"is not intended to penalize players who make minimal, accidental contact with an opponent's head with their stick"* but those who *"make a deliberate cross-checking action and strike an opponent in the head or neck"* — so it takes away the **major**, not the penalty. Hockey Canada 7.6(a) still gives a **minor** for accidental head contact in minor and female hockey, and 4.8(b)(ii) ejects a player assessed *"three Minor or double Minor penalties"* for head contact in the same game. Note also that this narrowing is written to **7.6(b)**; Hockey Canada writes no equivalent interpretation for Junior or Senior. Below the shoulders, Hockey Canada 9.2 runs a bare minor at 9.2(a), a major plus game misconduct at 9.2(b) on two limbs — *"at the discretion of the referee, based on the degree of violence of impact"*, **with nobody hurt**, and mandatory where it injures — and a **match penalty at 9.2(c)**; its definition is the widest of the four, dropping *"forcefully"* altogether, with Interpretation 1 to 9.2(a) supplying the test instead: *"If a pushing motion is used, a penalty may not be necessary. However, a striking motion must be penalized."* The NHL and USA Hockey both reach a match penalty too (59.4, 609(c)); **the IIHF does not, because it writes none.** One further Hockey Canada limb, from the interpretations to its *High-Sticking – Junior/Senior* rule and so scoped to those divisions: *"a cross-checking action [that] glances off the upper body and deflects upwards to make contact with the head or neck area, causing injury"* is a **major plus a game misconduct** under Rule 7.7 — so a cross-check that rides up reaches that tier without being aimed at the head. In minor and female hockey, 7.6 governs.
- **Spearing** (Rule 62) — stabbing with the **point of the blade**. **Double minor if you miss, major plus game misconduct if you connect, match penalty if you injure** — that is the NHL's ladder, and **of the four books only the IIHF has no match tier for it at all.** ⚠️ **The IIHF stops two short:** 62.2 and 62.3 match the NHL, but **62.4 is *Disciplinary Measures*, not a match penalty**, because the IIHF book writes none — so a British reader's ceiling here is the major plus automatic game misconduct. ⚠️ **And the other two books remove a rung at each end.** **USA Hockey 635(a) has no double minor at all**: *"a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed for spearing an opponent"*, with 635(b) adding a match penalty for reckless endangerment. **Hockey Canada 9.4(b) removes the middle instead** — *"No Major penalty may be assessed for a spearing infraction"* — so above its double minor there is only a match penalty.
- **Butt-ending** (Rule 58) — using the **shaft above your upper hand**, or the butt of the stick, to jab or check. Same first two rungs in the NHL and IIHF books — **double minor for the attempt, major plus game misconduct for contact** — and the NHL adds a **match penalty for injury** at 58.4. ⚠️ **The IIHF does not: its 58.4 is *Disciplinary Measures*, so your ceiling is the major plus automatic game misconduct.** ⚠️ **And the same two divergences as spearing.** **USA Hockey 606(a) puts the *attempt* straight at a major plus a game misconduct**, and says so in terms: the attempt *"shall include all cases where a 'butt-end' gesture is made regardless of whether body contact is made or not."* A gesture with nobody touched is an ejection there. **Hockey Canada 9.1(b)** again bars the middle rung — *"No Major penalty may be assessed for butt-ending"* — with **9.1(c)** supplying a match penalty at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence, and **mandatory** where the foul injures. USA Hockey has its own match tier too, at **606(b)**, *"for reckless endangerment"*. And note Hockey Canada narrows its own offence: 9.1's definition does reach a player who uses the shaft above the upper hand *"to check or jab"*, but its Interpretation 1 to 9.1(a) then says *"in order for a butt-ending penalty to be assessed, a jabbing motion must occur"* — so the check limb is written in the rule and taken away by the interpretation.
- **Holding the stick** (Rule 54.2) — a specific minor, announced as such. ⚠️ **The grab-and-release grace is a permission, and it is written in two books of four.** NHL 54.2 and IIHF 54.2 both say a player is permitted to protect *"himself"* — the IIHF reads *"themself"* — *"by defending against an opponent's stick"*, and *"must immediately release the stick"*. **Hockey Canada permits only *blocking*** — 8.1 lets you block an opponent's stick *"with their hands, arms, or body"* and writes no grab-and-release grace at all. **And USA Hockey runs the other way**: Rule 622's Note names *"using a free hand to clutch, grab or hold the stick"* as holding outright, with no self-defence permission anywhere in the rule. So "grab it, then let go" is a minor in a USA Hockey game — and do not read USA Hockey's Standard of Play, which states the same definition and adds *"in a manner that impedes their progress"*, as a grace period. The same two pages list *"grabbing the opponent's body, stick or sweater with one or both hands"* among its own examples, put the principle disjunctively as *"will not be allowed to grab **or** impede"*, and tell officials these are *"to be called with very strict enforcement."* The NHL and IIHF narrow their permission too: Rule 56.1's free-hand standard says a free hand *"may be used by a player to 'fend off' an opponent or his stick, but may not be used to hold an opponent's stick or body."* The ceiling is a minor in the NHL, the IIHF and USA Hockey — Rule 54 and Rule 622 have no major tier for ordinary holding — but **Hockey Canada 8.1(b) makes a major plus a game misconduct mandatory where *any* holding infraction injures an opponent — its words are "a holding infraction", not the stick limb alone** — the only book of four where holding a stick can end your night. (USA Hockey's major and match tiers in the same rule are for grabbing a **facemask**, which is a different offence.)

**Practically:** the whole stick-foul family collapses into one habit. **Stick on the puck, never stick on the player.** (Not *"stick on the ice"* — that is USA Hockey's cross-checking wording and it is not a general safe harbour: a stick on the ice can still be tripping, hooking or slashing.) If your stick is horizontal and touching an opponent, you are one referee's judgement away from a penalty. The defensive alternative is your feet and your body position, which is why gap control and angling are defensive *skills* and reaching is a defensive *failure*. See [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) and [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md).

### Body fouls

*These are the ones you are most likely to meet — a reading of the rulebooks against how the game is officiated, not a published count of calls, and not a complete list. **Two fouls are deliberately not covered here: head-butting and kicking.** All four books penalise both, and they grade them very differently from one another; this document does not compare those tiers across the books, so look them up in the book your league runs rather than inferring anything from their absence. Where a bullet below gives a penalty range, check that against your own book too.*

- **Boarding** (Rule 41) — checking or pushing a **defenceless** opponent so that he hits the boards violently or dangerously. Severity, and therefore whether it is a minor, major or match, is at the referee's discretion based on the impact — **but that ladder is the NHL's, and it is wrong in both directions elsewhere.** **USA Hockey has no bare minor for boarding**: 603(a) is *"a minor plus a misconduct or major plus game misconduct"*, with 603(b) making the harsher one mandatory for reckless endangerment and 603(c) reaching a match. **Hockey Canada** makes the game misconduct mandatory with any boarding major (7.2(e)), and **7.2(b) itself runs on two limbs**: a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* — **nobody has to be hurt for that one** — and the same pair **mandatory** where a board that would otherwise be a minor injures. **The IIHF book — the British one — reaches the ejection on a different trigger**: 41.4 reaches a major and a game misconduct for reckless endangerment, again with nobody hurt. The IIHF book carries **no match penalty for boarding at all**, so do not treat "match" as your worst case there; treat 41.4 as it. Even the NHL makes a game misconduct mandatory where a boarding major injures an opponent's face or head (41.5). The onus is explicitly on the **checker** to ensure his opponent is not defenceless — though the rulebook does allow the referee to consider whether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position at the last moment. **Unnecessary contact on an obvious icing or offside play that puts a man into the boards is boarding by definition** — and that clause is in **three** books verbatim (NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1, Hockey Canada 7.2), not the NHL's alone; **USA Hockey reaches it from the icing rule instead**, at 624(d), for icing only, without the offside half and without naming it boarding. ⚠️ **USA Hockey's trigger is wider than the definition above.** Rule 603 reaches a player who *"pushes, **trips** or body checks an opponent causing them to go dangerously into the boards"* — no defencelessness needed in the base sentence — and separately reaches *"any check delivered for the purpose of punishment or intimidation **that causes the opponent to go unnecessarily and excessively into the boards**"*, which catches a fully aware opponent. Its glossary makes a skater defenceless when *"unaware, unprepared or unsuspecting"*, and **640(b)** says of a player who has released a shot or pass that *"[t]hey are considered to be vulnerable or defenseless and are not eligible to be body checked"* — a **definition that carries no classification limb at all**, so while 640(b)'s *minor* is written *"except Adult Male Classifications"*, adult men lose that one named tier and keep everything else: the definition still feeds 603's trigger — an inference, on the same footing as the 640(g)/(h) question below, and not settled by the rulebook alone, so a check into the boards on someone who has just moved the puck *may* be boarding there rather than merely a late hit. Hockey Canada's preamble says *"unsuspecting"* rather than defenceless, which is wider again. ⚠️ **USA Hockey alone writes a permission here, and it is narrower than it sounds:** 603 says *"'Rolling' an opponent along the boards where they are attempting to go through too small an opening is not considered boarding"* — but that protects you from a *boarding* call only, only under that book, and **only if you are in a classification where body checking is legal at all**, because 604 defines an illegal body check as intentional contact *"using overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso action"* — rolling a man along the wall is torso action — **and 604 prohibits body checking in 12U and below, all girls' and women's classifications and all non-check adult classifications.** (604(c)'s Note pulls the other way for one case: *"Competitive contact also occurs when a player has established an angle (Angling - see Glossary) on the opponent and closes the gap to create an opening that is too small for the puck carrier to advance"*, and *"[l]egal competitive contact shall not be penalized under this rule."* But the sentence straight after it is the limit: *"However, deliberate physical contact with an opponent, with no effort to legally play the puck, shall be penalized."* USA Hockey's separate Casebook was searched for this and does not move that limit: its Rule 604, Situation 2 restates the test at length — legal competitive contact is where players are "focused on the puck and are simply maintaining legally established body position" — without qualifying the sentence above.) Import it into an IIHF or Hockey Canada game and you have nothing. ⚠️ **And the cost of a major can outlive the game — for most of the fouls in this list, not just this one.** NHL 23.6 collects boarding, charging, checking from behind, clipping, elbowing, head-butting, interference and kneeing into a *Physical Infractions Category*, where a second game misconduct before 41 clean games is an automatic one-game suspension. **USA Hockey Rule 411 goes further**, counting a third major across a seventeen-rule list — most of the fouls below — as three further games, a fourth as five, and a fifth as *"suspended until a hearing is conducted by the proper authorities (USA Hockey Affiliate)."* The count is cumulative across all of them, not per foul. **And a USA Hockey game misconduct is never only tonight:** Rule 404(b) suspends you for your team's next game automatically, on the first one. **Hockey Canada 4.8(c) does the same** for any game misconduct in the last ten minutes, in overtime, or after the game — *"a minimum of the next regular league/play-off game or seven days"* — and its 7.2(b), 8.1(b) and 8.3(b) each make that game misconduct mandatory where the foul injures. **Nor is boarding scoped by division.** Hockey Canada's body-checking rule, 7.3, is the rule that *bans* hitting, and it applies **only** in U13 and below, in female hockey and in any Member-approved division — **it does not scope 7.2**, and USA Hockey does not scope 603 either. So if you have been told the checking rules are not yours, the boarding rule still is.
- **Charging** (Rule 42) — violently checking an opponent **as a result of distance travelled, speed built up, or leaving your feet**. It can be into the boards, into the net, or in open ice. ⚠️ **Jumping is charging in its own right, however short the approach.** NHL 42.1 opens on a player who *"skates, **jumps into** or charges an opponent in any manner"* and IIHF 42.1 on one who *"either **jumps to check an opponent**"*; Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to 7.4(a) fixes the moment — feet off the ice **before** contact, and not charging if they leave it after — but only *"during an otherwise legal check"*. ⚠️ **The written stride threshold differs by one stride between the two books that give a number:** USA Hockey Rule 607 penalises *"more than two strides"*, Hockey Canada 7.4(ii) *"two or more"* — so the two strides that sit under the count in one book are already the offence in the other. IIHF 42.1 says *"multiple strides"* and the NHL gives no count. ⚠️ **Do not read any of those numbers as a ceiling you may skate to, because the count is one limb of the test and not the test.** USA Hockey's own sentence continues *"or travels an excessive distance to accelerate through a body check for the purpose of punishing the opponent"*, and its *Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis — Body Checking* lists *"[a]ccelerating through a check for the purpose of punishing the opponent"* as an example of charging **with no stride count attached to it at all**. Two strides is a safe harbour in no book. ⚠️ **A minor is the floor in three books of four — and in none of them is it the ceiling.** The NHL (42.2) and the IIHF (42.2) both allow a bare minor at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence, and **Hockey Canada is blunter still — 7.4(a) says a minor *"will be assessed to any player who charges an opponent"*, with no violence test and no discretion at all** — while 7.4(d) forbids adding a misconduct to it; **USA Hockey is the exception**, where 607(a) is a minor **plus a misconduct** or a major plus a game misconduct and no bare minor exists. Above that floor: USA Hockey 607(b) makes the major and game misconduct compulsory for reckless endangerment and 607(e) reaches a match penalty; IIHF 42.4 allows a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment **at the referee's discretion** — the IIHF's own summary tables put charging among the majors where the game misconduct is a judgement call, unlike clipping, where it is automatic; Hockey Canada 7.4(b) has the same two limbs its other physical fouls do — a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, **with nobody hurt**, and that same pair **mandatory** where a charge that would otherwise be a minor injures an opponent — and 7.4(c) reaches a match penalty where the opponent could not defend themselves; even the NHL has a match penalty at 42.4 and a mandatory game misconduct at 42.5 where a major injures the face or head. **Hockey Canada makes an open-ice check to an opponent's blind side charging outright** (7.4(v)) — and the IIHF defines a blind-sided hit in its own Definitions appendix as one delivered *"outside the latter's established peripheral vision"*, which *"is not considered to be a well-executed check, and may be penalized accordingly"* — a standard it leaves to judgement. **The only book that puts a number on it is Hockey Canada**, whose Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.4(a) defines a blindside hit as *"an open-ice body check that is delivered from an angle of approach outside a 90° peripheral point of view of a vulnerable opponent, whether or not the player has the puck."* See [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md), which covers this.
- **Checking from behind** (Rule 43) — a check on a player who is **unaware and unable to protect himself**, with contact on the back of the body. **Under NHL rules there is no minor penalty for this** — Rule 43.2 says so in terms (*"There is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"*): it is a **major plus an automatic game misconduct**, anywhere on the ice. **The IIHF book lands in the same place and is blunter about why** — IIHF 43.2 reads *"This infraction is considered serious and dangerous, therefore there is no option to award a minor penalty for 'Checking from Behind'"*, and 43.3 provides *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"* against a player who *"recklessly endangers the fouled Player."* Rule 43 is one where the NHL and the IIHF agree, which is what makes Rule 48 below an exception rather than a pattern. **USA Hockey does provide a minor option** — Rule 608(a) reads *"a minor plus a misconduct penalty, **or** a major plus a game misconduct penalty, shall be assessed for checking from behind"* — but 608(b) makes the major-plus-game-misconduct mandatory where the check *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame, as a result of checking from behind"*, and 608(c) escalates to a match penalty. Do not read the minor option as leniency, and do not read Rule 608 as the whole of USA Hockey's answer, because **its Casebook confines that tier.** *USA Hockey Official Rules and Casebook 2025-29*, Rule 608, Situation 1 says *"The minor plus misconduct penalty must be assessed in the following situations"* and names two: a player who, *"in an attempt to catch an opponent who is skating ahead of them and not near the boards, pushes the opponent from behind, causing them to fall to the ice"*, and a player who *"makes minimal body contact from behind to an opponent who is in close proximity to them, and board contact is made. This check should be called as a minor and a misconduct as the result of this 'pinch' against the boards from behind."* ⚠️ **The second of those does involve the boards** — so it is the force, not the presence of the wall, that separates the tiers. Against those two the Casebook sets three instances in which *"The major plus a game misconduct penalty, or match penalty, must be called"*: *"(1) In every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"*, *"(2) In every instance where a player is thrust head first into the boards or goal frame"*, and — the one that does not depend on the boards at all — *"(3) In every instance where the player recklessly endangers the opponent from a check from behind, regardless of whether or not board contact is made."* The same page tells officials to be sensitive to every check from behind inside the *"danger zone,"* which it puts at *"approximately ten feet out from the boards."* **Hockey Canada reaches the ejection by a different route again:** Rule 7.5(a) assesses *"a Minor penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty"* to a player who *"intentionally pushes, body-checks, cross-checks while in motion, or otherwise hits an opposing player from behind, anywhere on the ice"* — the in-motion element is what its Interpretation 1 uses to separate this from plain cross-checking, 7.5(b) has two limbs — a major plus game misconduct **at the referee's discretion** on the degree of violence of the impact, and a major plus game misconduct that **must** be assessed *"if a player is injured, as the result of a checking from behind infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty"* — 7.5(c) makes a match penalty mandatory where the player is driven into the boards or goal frame *"in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, and 7.5(e) requires the game misconduct *"any time a Minor or Major penalty is assessed for Checking From Behind"* — so under that book even the small version ends your night. Of the four books, **USA Hockey is the only one whose lowest tier stops short of an ejection** — its misconduct is a plain one, which returns you to the ice where a game misconduct would not (Rule 404(a)) — **but on its own Casebook that tier reaches only the push and the pinch above, so for a forceful check along the boards, and for any check from behind that recklessly endangers, all four books end your game.** **Treat the onus as the checker's** — **USA Hockey** Rule 608 states it in those words (*"the onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind"*) and **Hockey Canada** Rule 7.5's preamble says *"in all circumstances"*, while **NHL and IIHF Rule 43 state no onus at all**. Either way "he turned at the last second" is not a defence: **the NHL and the IIHF** waive the penalty only for a *deliberate* turn to draw the contact — the NHL frames it as a player who *"intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back"* — and neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes even that. Hockey Canada writes a different waiver instead, for marginal accidental contact while playing the puck and for low-speed pinning, and a late turn is neither. See [Common Mistakes](#common-mistakes) for why a late turn is not a deliberate one.
- **Slew-footing** (Rule 52) — sweeping an opponent's feet out from under them with your leg or foot, or **pushing their upper body backward with an arm or elbow while your leg takes their skates**. ⚠️ **It grows straight out of a legal battle position, and the four books grade it further apart than almost any other foul.** **NHL 52.2 has no minor tier, no major tier and no discretion at all**: *"Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty."* **IIHF 52.2** — the British reader's — reads *"The Referee **shall**, at their discretion, assess a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty if a Player is guilty of 'slew-footing' on an opposing Player."* **Read the frame:** *shall assess*, where the IIHF writes *may assess* in the neighbouring rules, and **Rule 52 writes no lesser tier beneath it** — 52.1 defines the foul and 52.2 is its only penalty clause. That is an ejection, and not a match penalty. **USA Hockey** excludes it from its own minor (639(a) covers the tripping family *"except slew footing"*) and sets the floor at a major plus a game misconduct, with 639(c) adding a match penalty for reckless endangerment. **Hockey Canada is the outlier and the one place the ladder starts small — note *starts*.** 8.8(a) is a **double minor** and 8.8(b) bars a major outright, but **8.8(c) reaches a match penalty**, discretionary on the degree of violence and mandatory where the foul injures, so its ceiling is the ejection the double minor is not.
