Hockey Canada also defines it more widely than the other three, reaching a leg that *"knock[s], drag[s], or sweep[s]"* the feet away rather than only knocks or kicks, and its Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a) says *"serious consideration for a Match penalty should be paid when an offending player is 'in motion' (skating) while committing this foul."* The leg through the skates is what makes it slew-footing, and it is an ejection at the floor in three books of four. What the NHL and IIHF definitions (52.1) both end on is a player made to fall *"violently to the ice"*. That the fall lands them backward and head-first is this guide's reading, not a sentence any rulebook writes. ⚠️ **If someone goes down backward and is not getting up, nobody moves them and nobody takes the helmet off**, and neck pain is an ambulance call. **If you went down and got up, come off and say so:** a suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, and a medical professional before you play again. [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md) carries the red flags and the sources for both. **None of that makes the arm legal.** The arm bar is conditional where it is written at all: NHL 54.2 allows an arm *"in a strength move, by blocking his opponent, provided he has body position and is not using his hands in a holding manner"* — **two conditions, not one** — and neither of them is pushing the upper body backward. **USA Hockey writes no such permission** — its Rule 622 Note names a free arm used *"to restrain or impede the opponent's progress"* as holding. See the Holding bullet below. See [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) for the battle position this is the illegal version of.
- **Elbowing** (Rule 45) — use of an **extended elbow**, whether or not injury results. Under the **NHL** a major must be assessed if it injures the face or head, and that brings a game misconduct (45.3, 45.5), with **45.4 reaching a match penalty** where the referee judges an attempt to injure or a deliberate injury. ⚠️ **The clause number does not travel.** **IIHF** 45.2 is a minor, **45.3 a bare major** where a minor would not suffice, and 45.4 a major plus a game misconduct for *reckless endangerment* — a lower threshold than intent, so the British reader's ejection route is easier to trip than the NHL's, and **there is no match tier above it**, because the IIHF book writes no match penalty for anything. **USA Hockey** runs a minor for elbowing *"or an attempt to elbow"* (611(a)), a major plus game misconduct for reckless endangerment (611(b)), **and a match penalty (611(c))**. **Hockey Canada writes no rule *titled* Elbowing** — there is none in its Section 7 list — so an elbow is handled under head contact, roughing or checking from behind, and **each of those routes reaches a match penalty**: 7.6(c) and 7.7(c) for head contact, 7.9(c) for a deliberate attempt to injure by roughing, 7.5(c) for checking from behind. Its Rule 7.5 also names a player who *"swings the elbow and forearm into the opponent's back with the intent of throwing the opponent into the boards"* among the situations referees are told to enforce strictly.
- **Kneeing** (Rule 50) — **leading with the knee**, sometimes extending the leg out. A major for kneeing carries an automatic game misconduct, and **50.4 reaches a match penalty** for attempting to or deliberately injuring. ⚠️ **The floors and ceilings differ in every book, and again the clause number does not travel — IIHF 50.4 is not a match penalty.** **IIHF 50.3** is a major plus an **automatic** game misconduct and there is no match tier above it. **USA Hockey 628(a) has no bare minor** — it is a minor **plus a misconduct** — with 628(b) major plus game misconduct and 628(c) a match penalty. **Hockey Canada 7.8(a) starts at a double minor**, four minutes — **and 7.8(b) is the rung between that and the match penalty, on two limbs**: a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, **with nobody hurt**, and that same pair **mandatory** where a knee that would otherwise be a double minor injures. 7.8(c) makes a match penalty **mandatory** for a deliberate attempt to injure by kneeing, and 7.8(e) makes the game misconduct automatic on any 7.8(b) major.
- **Clipping** (Rule 44) — throwing the body **across or below the knees** from any direction, or lowering your own body to hit at or below the knees. ⚠️ **A major is not the ceiling, and injury is not the trigger everywhere.** NHL 44.3 requires the major where injury results, 44.5 makes a **game misconduct mandatory with it**, and 44.4 reaches a **match penalty** for attempting to or deliberately injuring. **Under the IIHF book — the British one — nobody has to be hurt:** 44.3 gives a major plus an **automatic** game misconduct where the referee judges the clip *"recklessly endangers"* the opponent. USA Hockey 639(b) makes the same major plus game misconduct mandatory for reckless endangerment and 639(c) reaches a match penalty; Hockey Canada's tiers are at 8.7(b) and 8.7(c), below. **Both books that write a crouching clause put it in this rule** — see the ducking passage below.
- **Roughing** (Rule 51) — a **punching or slamming motion**, gloved or not, usually at the head or face; **and — in the NHL's book and the IIHF's — intentionally removing an opponent's helmet *during play*** (NHL 51.1 and 51.2, both routed through Rule 9.6; IIHF 51.1 and 51.2, identical in its 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions). ⚠️ **That helmet limb is written for open play — *"during play"* — and it is not the rule for a helmet that comes off in a fight. Each of the four books handles that somewhere other than roughing, and two of them are nowhere near a minor.** **USA Hockey puts it inside the fighting rule, at 615(c), and starts at a match penalty *"(for all age classifications)"*** — one for a player who deliberately removes their own helmet/facemask *"prior to an altercation"*, and a second for one who deliberately removes *"their opponent's helmet/facemask prior to or during an altercation"* — with **a game misconduct carrying an express two-game suspension** for a player *"whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"*, and a further game misconduct for a player who *"doesn't wear their helmet/facemask as designed and the helmet/facemask is removed during an altercation."* ⚠️ **And USA Hockey's Casebook widens it in the direction that catches ordinary players.** Its Rule 615, Situation 14 assesses the match penalty *"any time a player involved in that altercation deliberately removes their own helmet"* — *during*, where the printed clause reaches your own helmet only *"prior to"* — and adds that *"[f]ighting penalties do not have to be assessed when applying Rule 615(c)."* The Note applying to Situations 14–20 says *"an altercation does not need to be considered a fight for Rule 615(c) to be applied. This rule would apply in any altercation where at least one player will receive a penalty"*, and Situation 21 defines an altercation as *"[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed."* **So a post-whistle shove that draws one roughing minor is an altercation, and pulling your own lid off in it is a match penalty with no punch thrown.** **Hockey Canada files the same act under equipment rather than fighting — its roughing and fighting rules, 7.9 and 7.10, say nothing about helmets — and still ends your night:** **Rule 10.6(f) makes it a Gross Misconduct** — an order to the dressing room for the rest of the game plus a report to the league (4.9(a)) — to intentionally remove your own or another player's helmet before or during a fight, or to undo either chinstrap so that the helmet comes off during it; and 10.6(d) adds a misconduct for a player who *"undoes their chinstrap to incite or challenge an opponent but does not actually participate in a fight."* **The NHL and the IIHF are the lenient pair here, and they are the two books most readers have watched:** NHL 46.6, and IIHF 46.12 in the 2025/26 book and 46.6 in the 2026/27 one, all price removing *your own* helmet before a fight at a **minor for unsportsmanlike conduct**, and all three add that *"[h]elmets that come off in the course of and resulting from the altercation will not result in a penalty to either player"* — the NHL's wording, the IIHF's differing only in capitalising *Player*. **That sentence has no counterpart in USA Hockey's book, where the same helmet coming off is a game misconduct for somebody** — the player whose actions caused it, or the player whose chin strap was not properly fastened (Casebook, Rule 615, Situations 16 and 17). The NHL and the IIHF both define roughing as *"a minor altercation that is not worthy of a major penalty to either participant"*, and all four books define "altercation", though not identically: the NHL as *"a situation involving two players with at least one to be penalized"*, the IIHF and Hockey Canada as *"at least two"*, and USA Hockey more narrowly still in its Glossary — it wants a penalty actually assessed, a gathering of players, and officials having to separate them. **Do not read "minor" as the ceiling in any book, including your own.** Under the **IIHF**, which is the British reader's book, Rule 51.2 sends a player who *"persists in continuing to be involved in roughing"* to the fighting rule, and Rule 46.5 assesses *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"* to a player who punches *"an unsuspecting or 'unwilling combatant / opponent' (i.e., 'sucker punch') that could cause an injury"* — which is what the ordinary rec-hockey glove punch at somebody not fighting actually is. Under **Hockey Canada**, Rule 7.9's preamble says roughing *"should not be utilized in lieu of more serious penalties for Rule 7.6 – Head Contact or Rule 7.10 – Fighting. Therefore, if a player punches an opponent in the head, face or neck, then the appropriate penalty will be assessed under Rule 7.6 – Head Contact"* — where a major plus a game misconduct is mandatory if injury results and 7.6(c) reaches a match penalty. In Junior and Senior, Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.7(a) treats raising the *"stick, hands, forearm, shoulder, or elbow to hit an opponent in the head area"* as head contact *"rather than for roughing"*, at a minimum of a minor **plus a misconduct** — though 7.7's own preamble still lets a referee call minimal-violence head contact as roughing in those divisions, so Hockey Canada's text pulls both ways and only the punch-specific rule, 7.9, is unambiguous. Under **USA Hockey**, 640(a) sends anything major-worthy to Rule 615 Fighting, whose Note counts *"a punch, or an attempted punch, thrown by any player in the direction of an opponent, regardless as to whether contact is made"*, and 615(a) puts a **major plus a game misconduct** on any player who fights. (640(g) and 640(h) do not reach a punch — both enumerate subsections b to f, and roughing is 640(a) — so the escalation runs through 615.) Assume ejection, not a mutual two minutes. **If you are the one who was hit, USA Hockey writes you a way out — and it is USA Hockey's alone.** 615(a) says that *"a player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule"*, while a player who *"having been struck, continues the altercation by retaliating"* faces a minor, double minor, or major plus game misconduct at the referee's discretion. **If you threw the punch, none of that is for you.** **The IIHF writes no such exemption.** Its nearest provision runs the other way: 46.6 confers the status of *"unwilling combatant"* on a player who *"defends themself with a 'few punches'"* and assesses them nothing on that basis, while a player who *"retaliates to being punched"* gets *"at least a minor penalty ('Roughing') or a major penalty ('Fighting', without a game misconduct penalty), if the criteria for 'fighting back' with a few punches is not fulfilled."* So under the British reader's book skating away is the safer choice on the rules as written, not a written protection. So do not retaliate: turn away, get to an official or your bench, and let the referee sort it out — see [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md). **And if it landed on your head, come off and say so:** a suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, and a medical professional before you play again. [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md) carries the red flags that mean an ambulance instead, and the sources.
- **Interference** (Rule 56) — the most conceptually important body foul, and the one you should actually study. The rule turns on **body position**: you have body position when you are **in front of or beside** your opponent, travelling in the same direction. **Beside gives you the ice you are standing on; only in front gives you the block.** The NHL and IIHF write the permission into the interference rule in materially identical words — the NHL's reads that a player may block an opponent's path *"provided he is in front of his opponent and moving in the same direction"*, and the IIHF's is the same sentence in the plural, *"provided they are in front of their opponent and moving in the same direction"* — and **USA Hockey states the same permission in its Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play and Respect**, under the heading *Competitive Contact Category*, which opens *"Non-check hockey does not mean no contact"* — so it is written for non-check play and, on the same reading as the inference above, holds a fortiori where checking is legal — an inference, not the book's words — so from beside, moving across into their path is a pick and not a block. You are **entitled to the ice you are standing on** — you do not have to move for anyone. What you may not do is **move laterally into a player without having established position** (that is a "pick"), or use your **stick or free hand** to lengthen his route, or check a player who is **not in possession and unaware** of the hit. A player who has just lost the puck may be checked, but **only immediately** after losing it. The NHL and IIHF both open the rule with the same instruction — *"A strict standard on acts of interference must be adhered to in all areas of the rink"* — and the **NHL's** 2025-26 book then softened 56.2's operative verb from *"shall"* to *"can"*, formally acknowledging it as a judgement call. ⚠️ **That softening is the NHL's alone: IIHF 56.2 still reads *"shall be assessed"*.** ⚠️ **And interference is not capped at a minor in three of the four books.** NHL 56.4 allows a major on the degree of violence, and 56.5 adds a game misconduct where a major has been imposed under the rule and it injures; **IIHF 56.5 reaches a major plus a game misconduct for a late hit that recklessly endangers, with nobody hurt** — and IIHF 56.1 says in terms that a late hit *"constitutes reckless endangerment of a Player who 'no longer has control or possession of the puck'."* **Hockey Canada is harsher at the top, and it does not need anybody hurt:** 8.3(b)'s first sentence reaches a major plus a game misconduct *"at the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, and its second makes that tier mandatory where interference injures an opponent; 8.3(c) is a match penalty for an attempt to injure or a deliberate one. **USA Hockey writes no tier above the minor inside Rule 625 — but the late hit is not only in Rule 625 there.** Rule 640(b) names a player who has released a shot or pass as *"vulnerable or defenseless and are not eligible to be body checked"*, and 640(g) and 640(h) reach a major plus a game misconduct and a match penalty for recklessly endangering them — though 640(g) is written to reach only actions *"falling under Rule 640(b, c, d, e or f)"* and 640(h) is limited the same way in its own words, so how far they carry in adult men's play is not settled by the rulebook alone (see *What actually gets called*). **602(a) has no such limit**: it reaches any player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure"* an opponent. Treat the minor as the floor, not the ceiling. And NHL 23.6 puts interference in the same *Physical Infractions Category* as boarding, so two game misconducts there carry an automatic suspension too. ⚠️ **"Only immediately" is the NHL's bare word, and two books say more.** **Hockey Canada 8.3 writes the only test you can actually judge from the ice:** immediate contact *"is best described as"* contact occurring *"within a maximum distance of an arm and stick length between the puck carrier and the player delivering the check, at the instant the puck has been released."* Treat that as a stricter standard, not a window the other three books grant you — **the IIHF puts no measurable distance on it — proximity is a condition there, but a judged one**, and closing it while adding force is what *risks* turning a legal check into a late hit. **And the IIHF book — the British one — carries both halves of the answer:** a player losing the puck *"is subject to a bodycheck so long as the aggressor is in the immediate vicinity"*, judged by whether there was an *"objectively reasonable window of opportunity"* to deliver it; and its Definitions add the instruction that matters, that closing the distance and adding force *"risks turning a body check into a late hit by virtue of the vulnerable position of the opponent and the degree of force of the hit."* ⚠️ **Two things the two North American books penalise that the NHL and IIHF do not enumerate:** screening for your *own* puck carrier — USA Hockey 625(a) names *"providing a protective screen and limiting the opportunity for an opposing player to apply pressure to a teammate in possession or control of the puck"*, and Hockey Canada 8.3 says players in possession *"may not run deliberate interference for the puck carrier"* — and, on the defensive side, USA Hockey 625(a) reaching *"a defending player who changes their skating lane or foot speed in an effort to play the body of an opponent who is no longer in control of the puck."* **Nor is interference scoped by division** — **8.3 and 625 are unscoped** too.
- **Holding** (Rule 54) — **any action that restrains or impedes** an opponent, with or without the puck. **Hockey Canada's 8.1 is wider than that**, reaching any action *"prohibiting their ability to pass, shoot, receive, or otherwise propel the puck"* — so a hand on the arm that stops a shot without impeding a stride is holding there. You **may** use your arm in a strength move to block, provided you have body position and are not using your hands to hold — **but that permission is written in three books of four (NHL 54.2, IIHF 54.2, Hockey Canada 8.1) and not in USA Hockey's at all.** USA Hockey Rule 622's Note runs the other way, naming *"using a free arm/hand to restrain or impede the opponent's progress"* and *"wrapping one or both arms around the opponent along the boards in a manner that pins them against the boards **and prevents them from playing the puck or skating**"* as holding. On the wall in a USA Hockey game, win it with your feet and your hips rather than your arm. ⚠️ **And a minor is not the ceiling.** NHL 54 and IIHF 54 stop there, and the IIHF book writes no match penalty at all — §4 has why that is not leniency. **Hockey Canada 8.1(b) makes a major plus a game misconduct mandatory where a holding infraction injures an opponent** — the only book of four whose *holding rule* reaches an ejection for ordinary holding, though the NHL gets there another way, through Rule 21.1's match penalty for a player who deliberately injures *"in any manner"*. The IIHF writes no tier above the minor in Rule 54 at all. Whether its Rule 21 can reach a holding infraction is not settled by the text: 21.1 is confined to conduct *"not covered by the playing rules"*, yet 75.2 routes a covered offence — grabbing a face mask — onward to Rule 21 *"based on the degree of force."* Treat the minor as the floor and not a guarantee. And in USA Hockey the *facemask* is a different offence inside the same rule: 622(b) is a major plus a game misconduct for a hand used *"to rub, grab or hold the facemask"*, 622(c) reaches a match penalty, **and that limb is on Rule 411's aggressive-infractions list, where a third major from anywhere on that list costs you three further games and a fifth means a hearing** — with Rule 404(b) suspending you for your team's next game automatically on the very first game misconduct. **Never touch an opponent's cage, and assume you have no floor** — Hockey Canada files the same action under Rule 7.1, Attempt to Injure, from a double minor at minimal violence up to a match penalty. ⚠️ **Nor is holding scoped by division** — same as boarding above: **8.1 and 622 are unscoped** where the body-checking rules are not.
- **Illegal check to the head** (Rule 48) — a hit where **the head was the main point of contact and that contact was avoidable**. The rule lists what "avoidable" means: whether you tried to hit through the body and picked the head through poor timing or angle or by extending upward; whether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable posture; whether he changed his body or head position at the last instant. **This is a minor penalty in the NHL** — Rule 48.3 reads *"There is no provision for a major penalty for this rule"* and 48.4 says the same of a game misconduct — though a **match penalty** is available if the referee judges intent to injure, and supplementary discipline usually follows.

  ⚠️ **That ceiling is NHL-only, and it is the most consequential rule-set difference on this page. Do not read "minor" as the international standard.** The IIHF rule is headed **"Illegal Check to the Head or Neck"** and opens *"There is no clean check to the head or neck."* It then enumerates four triggers the NHL text does not: a player who directs a hit *"of any sort, with any part of their body or equipment, to the head or neck"* of an opponent, or drives an opponent's head into the glass or boards; who *"extends and directs any part of their upper body to contact the head or neck"*; who *"extends their body upward or outward in order to reach their opponent or uses any part of the upper body to contact an opponent's head or neck"*; and *"A Player who jumps (leaves their skates) to deliver a blow to the head or neck of an opponent."* **IIHF Rule 48.3 then reaches a major penalty plus an automatic game misconduct** where the referee judges that the player *"recklessly endangers their opponent."* **USA Hockey Rule 620(a)** is the same shape — *"A minor plus a misconduct or major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed for head contact to an opponent"* — with **620(b)** making the major and game misconduct compulsory for reckless endangerment **and for anyone who *"intentionally or carelessly contacts an opponent in the head, face or neck"*** — a materially lower bar than recklessness — and **620(c)** allowing a match penalty; and **Hockey Canada Rule 7.6** opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck."* **The NHL is the outlier: in the other three books a hit to the head can end your game, and under the IIHF book — which is the British one at every level — it can.**

  ⚠️ **What that same IIHF rule does *not* do is protect a puck carrier skating with their head down**, and this is the half of Rule 48 that changes how *you* skate rather than how you hit. Four lines below the sentence quoted above, 48.1 reads: *"A Player who delivers a bodycheck to an opponent who is skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player and does not use an upward motion or drive their body up into the opponent, shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'."* The avoidability factors point the same way — the referee is to consider *"whether the Player attempted to hit squarely through the opponent's body and the head was not 'picked'"* and *"whether the opponent put themself in a vulnerable position by assuming a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable."* **None of that reduces the checker's obligation under the IIHF book**: they must still avoid your head, and what that book declines to penalise is only the square hit with no upward motion. Read the carve-out no wider than that, because **the IIHF's own text does not say how the carve-out sits against the triggers**. The carve-out excludes upward motion only, while IIHF trigger (III) also catches a checker who extends *outward* to reach you, and trigger (III)'s second limb catches a checker who *"uses any part of the upper body to contact an opponent's head or neck"* — which is a description of the very hit the carve-out is about. The IIHF rule gives no ordering language between the two sentences, and no published IIHF interpretation found here resolves it: the IIHF Situation Handbook carries a single Rule 48 entry, on whether a later-discovered injury upgrades a minor, and it does not touch this. **Treat the point as unresolved in the published text rather than settled either way.** Keep your head up — **under the IIHF book** a legal, square hit with no upward motion that catches you bent over may well not be the checker's foul.

  ⚠️ **Hockey Canada writes a wider exception than the IIHF's, and it points at *you*.** Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) provides that in *"Junior and Senior divisions"*, a player who *"ducks to avoid a check"* and as a result *"is hit in the head area with an otherwise legal check"* draws no Head Contact penalty against the checker — and that the referee *"may assess a penalty to the player who ducks to avoid a check under Rule 8.7 — Clipping, if appropriate."* Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 states that *"Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked"*, and 8.7(a) makes that a Minor — **but only as a floor.** 8.7(b) makes a major plus a game misconduct **mandatory** for a clipping infraction that injures an opponent, and discretionary on the degree of violence *"including the impact with the ice or boards"*; 8.7(c) makes a match penalty **mandatory** for attempting to injure or injuring deliberately. So a Canadian junior or senior player who ducks can take the penalty while the checker takes none. ⚠️ **And this is not Hockey Canada's alone — the IIHF writes a crouching clause too, in the British reader's own book.** IIHF Rule 44.1 says a player *"may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them"*: narrower, because it wants the boards **and** the checker going over you, but real, with IIHF 44.2 a minor on the degree of violence and IIHF 44.3 a major plus an **automatic game misconduct** for reckless endangerment. Neither the NHL nor USA Hockey writes any crouching clause at all. That same Hockey Canada interpretation *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, so a low head while you shoot or take a pass is not a duck. **No equivalent exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules**, and Hockey Canada's is confined by its own words to junior and senior, so **the checker's amnesty does not reach Hockey Canada minor or female hockey.** **Rule 8.7 itself is not confined** — its prohibition on crouching to avoid a bodycheck carries no division scope, so the *ducker's* exposure reaches minor and female hockey too. **And no book outside Hockey Canada writes the checker any equivalent *automatic* amnesty:** USA Hockey Rule 620's Note puts the onus on the checker *"to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger of potential injury"*, and the Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 preamble requires that head contact be penalised *"[r]egardless of the circumstances… at all times."* *(That absence is a search of the USA Hockey Official Playing Rules, the separate USA Hockey Rules and Casebook, and the Hockey Canada Playing Rules. The Casebook writes the checker no amnesty and no crouching clause either; its only ducking provision is at Rule 639, Situation 5, on a player who "deliberately ducks or leaves their feet for the purpose of contacting an opponent at or below the knees (Clipping)" — which points at the ducker, and on a different trigger again. Where the Casebook does speak, it runs the other way. Rule 620, Situation 1 answers how much force is needed for a head-contact penalty: "The only thing that matters is whether there was contact made to the head, face or neck of the opponent with any part of the body or equipment." And Rule 607, Situation 2 puts a charging penalty on a player who crosses the ice at full speed to hit "an unsuspecting opponent skating up the ice with their head down" — though read that one no wider than its own reasoning, which is the distance and the speed, "for the purpose of punishing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent", and not the opponent's head position on its own.)* ⚠️ **The NHL and the IIHF do reach the same facts, though — as a factor weighed by the referee, not as a defence that ends the question.** Both direct that in determining whether contact with an opponent's head was **avoidable**, the circumstances to be considered include *"[w]hether the opponent materially changed the position of his body or head immediately prior to or simultaneously with the hit in a way that significantly contributed to the head contact"* and *"[w]hether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position by assuming a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable"* (NHL Rule 48.1(iii) and (ii); IIHF Rule 48.1(VII) and (VI) say the same in the plural). **That is not an amnesty: it acquits nobody automatically, and neither book attaches any penalty to the player who ducked.** What it does mean is that in **three** of the four books your own late movement can be the reason the checker goes unpenalised — automatically under Hockey Canada, as a weighed factor under the NHL and the IIHF. **So, the instruction all of that adds up to: never duck.** Head up — chin off your chest — is not standing tall into the hit either. Take it on a wide base with the knees bent, and near the wall get your skates parallel to it and take the contact along your side — **your forearm and hip meet the wall, your shoulder and hip turn toward the checker** — never the point of your shoulder and never your back to the boards. See [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md), which covers head-contact detail.
- **Fighting** (Rule 46) — a **five-minute major**. In the NHL, fighting is not an automatic ejection; the **instigator** gets an extra minor plus a misconduct, and the **aggressor** — the one who keeps throwing punches at a defenceless or unwilling opponent — **is ejected**, drawing *"a major penalty for fighting and a game misconduct"* under Rule 46.2. That leniency applies to a willing pair, and not to the player who would not stop. Deliberately removing your jersey beforehand is an unsportsmanlike minor plus a game misconduct. **The IIHF takes a harder line and says so** — *"'Fighting' is not part of international ice hockey's DNA"* (Rule 46.1) — giving referees *"very wide latitude"*, with a **major plus an automatic game misconduct** — *"at least"* that, at the referee's discretion — for a player who persists after being ordered to stop **or who resists a linesperson in the discharge of their duties**. **England Ice Hockey then pins a number to it:** in NIHL (all leagues), the SNL and BUIHA Varsity fixtures, *"Fighting shall be penalised with a 5-minute Major Penalty"*, and in NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2 any fighting major *"in the last 5 minutes of regulation time (including overtime) will also carry an automatic Game Misconduct"* (In-House Rules, Section 6). ⚠️ **That reaches the plain willing fight, in those three competitions, and nothing else** — and that scope is a reading, not something Section 6 states, since it names no rule number; IIHF 46.1 itself reaches the plain willing fight, so five minutes is a floor and not a ceiling — **and the current book raised it.** In the 2025/26 edition a willing combatant *"may be ejected from the game"*; in the 2026/27 edition a major plus an automatic game misconduct is *"the standard penalty for a Player engaged in a fight"* (46.1, repeated at 46.12), waived only for a player the referee judges to have *"defended"* themselves. Instigator (46.3), aggressor (46.4), sucker-punching (46.5), third man in (46.7), fighting outside the periods (46.9), fighting off the surface (46.11), persisting after being told to stop, **and the plain willing fight itself anywhere outside those three competitions** all keep their IIHF ejection — 46.1 is a Reference Table 6 entry, so 20.4 attaches the automatic game misconduct to the major. ⚠️ **Every clause number in that list is the 2025/26 book's, and the 2026/27 edition reorders Rule 46 so that not one of the six still points where it did** — third man in moves from 46.7 to 46.14, instigator 46.3 to 46.10, aggressor 46.4 to 46.2, sucker punch 46.5 to 46.13, fighting outside the periods 46.9 to 46.8, and fighting off the surface 46.11 to 46.7, where 46.11 is now *Jerseys*. **Name the offence to an official rather than the number, unless you know which book they are working from.** Table 6 is that same edition's number; the 2026/27 book has none, having merged it into a single Table 5 that still carries the entry. **Assume you are gone.** See [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md).

**Practically:** three things. First, **find out whether you are allowed to hit at all, in your own book.** USA Hockey Rule 604(a) prohibits body checking *"in the 12 & under youth age classifications and below, all Girls'/Women's age classifications and all non-check Adult classifications"*; a local governing body *"may prohibit body checking in any classification"*; and 604(b) makes any game between one of those teams and a body-checking team a no-checking game. Checking there is a minor under 604(c), a major plus a game misconduct under 604(d) where it recklessly endangers an opponent, and 604(e) allows a match penalty on that same trigger. **Hockey Canada** reaches the same ground by a different route: Rule 7.3 **penalises** body-checking, and scopes that penalty in its own words — *"This rule applies only in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada."* Within those divisions, 7.3(a) assesses a minor to any player who *"intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player"*, and **7.3(b) runs above it on two limbs**: a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* — **nobody has to be hurt** — and that same pair **mandatory** where a check that would otherwise be a minor injures. 7.3(c) reaches a match penalty. **The IIHF book sets no checking age at all**, so a British reader's answer is in their competition regulations rather than in any of these four books — see [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md). ⚠️ **And "no checking" is not "no contact":** USA Hockey says so twice, in the Note to 604(c) — *"Competitive Contact hockey does not mean 'no contact'"* — and in its front matter. Established body position, angling and puck battles remain legal, and the Note adds that *"legal competitive contact shall not be penalized under this rule"* — **but that sentence continues, and the second half is the half that penalises you:** *"However, deliberate physical contact with an opponent, with no effort to legally play the puck, shall be penalized."* Hockey Canada says the same in Rule 7.3's preamble — but note where it draws the line: body contact is *"incidental contact of two opposing players in pursuit of the puck on the ice **in the same direction**"*, and the next paragraph makes three things body-checking rather than contact: deliberate contact *"with opposite-directional force"*, leaving your skating lane to make it, and — **the trap here** — *"when a player extends an arm, shoulder, or hip after angling an opponent."* Angling legally and then extending the shoulder is a body-check under that rule. The rest of this paragraph assumes you are in a checking league.

**In a checking league**, then: second, **position, contact surface and force are all part of the test — not one of them on its own.** Three of the four books grade charging on the degree of violence in those words (NHL 42.2, IIHF 42.2 on *"the degree of force and violence of the check"*, Hockey Canada 7.4(b)), and USA Hockey reaches the same place by a different test — 607(b)'s reckless endangerment, and its Note on *"accelerating through a check for the purpose of punishing the opponent"*. Either way, force is never settled in advance by where your feet were. **Trunk on trunk, above their knees and at or below their shoulders — and this is in the British reader's own book, not only the American one.** The IIHF's Definitions appendix defines a legal body check as one delivered *"only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders)"* and *"above the opponent's knees and at or below the shoulders"*; USA Hockey's Declaration of Player Safety uses the same phrases and adds that *"the use of the hands, forearm, stick or elbow in delivering a body check is unacceptable"*. Keep your **stick blade below their knees** — USA Hockey puts it that way round, *"proper body checking technique starts with stick on puck"*, and 640(d) is the penalty side of the same point. Establish position and **arrive while he still has the puck, or do not arrive** — **and not into a battle.** Rule 640(e) makes it a minor to body check *"an opponent who is physically engaged for possession of the puck with one or more other players"*, and its Note is explicit that two or more players contesting a puck *"are considered vulnerable or defenseless and are not eligible to be body checked"* — a major plus a game misconduct under 640(g) if it recklessly endangers him, a match penalty under 640(h). **Having the puck is not the whole test**, and a wall or corner scramble is exactly the case the rule excludes. Do not chase across the ice and arrive, and **do not think of it as "finishing your check"** — USA Hockey names that phrase specifically: *"The concept of 'finishing the check' is an unacceptable action as it is one that is meant to intimidate or punish the opponent with no intent to gain possession of the puck."* There is no such thing as a check you complete after he has released it. Once he no longer controls the puck the obligation flips, and the same passage says so — *"The responsibility is on the player delivering the check to avoid forceful contact (minimize impact) to a vulnerable or defenseless player who is no longer in control of the puck."* One scope note, because it cuts against the reader here: the numbered rule penalising the ordinary late check, 640(b), is written *"(except Adult Male Classifications)"* — and 640(g) and 640(h) do not simply close that gap, because 640(g) reaches reckless endangerment only *"as a result of any actions falling under Rule 640(b, c, d, e or f)"* and 640(h) is confined *"under sub-sections (b), (c), (d), (e) or (f) of this rule"*. What does **not** carry the exception is 640(e), the battle rule above, which is division-scoped nowhere; and the Declaration of Player Safety, which is written for the whole book and says a check on a vulnerable or defenceless player **must** be penalised under the boarding, charging, checking-from-behind, head-contact or roughing rules. So the late hit in adult men's hockey loses one named subsection and keeps every other route. Third, **the onus rule on defenceless players is real.** If a man is facing the boards, or reaching for a puck, or has just released it, the rulebook puts the responsibility for the outcome on you. Let him go.

### Other infractions

- **Delay of game — puck over the glass** (Rule 63.2(iii)) — a minor on any player who shoots or bats the puck **directly, non-deflected, out of the playing surface from his defending zone**. **That is the strict-liability limb, and this rule has a second one that most players have never heard of.** ⚠️ **Rule 63.2(ii) reaches the same minor from anywhere on the ice** — it is assessed *"on any player who deliberately shoots or bats (using his hand, leg, foot, arm, or stick, etc.) the puck outside the playing area (from anywhere on the ice surface) during the play or after a stoppage of play"*, and **IIHF Rule 63.2(II) carries the same clause**, in the plural. Note how much wider it is than (iii): (iii) reaches only *"his hand or his stick"* and only your own end, where (ii) reaches a **leg, foot or arm** as well, **every zone**, and the period **after the whistle**. So **outside your defending zone you are safe only if it was an accident** — a deliberate flip out of the neutral or attacking zone to kill a shift, or a puck fired away after the whistle, is two minutes. **On that deliberate limb all four books agree, and none of them limits it by zone:** USA Hockey 610(c) — a minor on any player or goalkeeper who *"delays the game by deliberately shooting or batting the puck outside the playing area, including after a stoppage of play"* — and Hockey Canada 10.1(ii), a player who *"deliberately shoots, bats, or throws the puck outside the playing surface"*. **The after-the-whistle words are in all four books — but Hockey Canada's are in a different rule, and it is worth knowing why.** Three of the four write them into the delay-of-game rule itself: the NHL's and the IIHF's 63.2(II), and USA Hockey's 610(c). **Hockey Canada 10.1(ii) does not carry them**, and Rule 10.1 says how far it can go — **10.1(d)**: *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for Delay of Game"*, and **10.1(e)**: *"No Game Misconduct penalty may be assessed for Delay of Game."* **So Hockey Canada files the after-whistle shot under unsportsmanlike conduct instead, and writes it out expressly.** Rule **11.1, preamble item (v)** lists *"Shooting the puck out of the playing area, after the whistle"* — a **Minor or Bench Minor** under 11.1(a), and a **Misconduct** under 11.1(d)(i) for a player who *"[p]ersists in Unsportsmanlike Conduct, after receiving a Minor penalty under Rule 11.1 (a)"*; 11.1(b) and 11.1(c) rule out a major and a match penalty for it. ⚠️ **That (d)(i) wording is not a promise of one free minor first, and the rule's own Note says so.** Note 1 to 11.1(d): *"A Referee is not required to assess a Minor penalty under this rule before assessing a Misconduct penalty, but may assess either penalty initially"*, and Note 1 to 11.1(e) says the same of the Game Misconduct — *"not required to assess a Minor or Misconduct penalty under this rule before assessing a Game Misconduct penalty, but may assess either penalty initially."* So the ladder is the range available to the referee, not a sequence you are walked up. **That is an express provision, not an inference from the 10.1 preamble.** ⚠️ **And USA Hockey builds a ladder over the same act that runs to a match penalty. This is the part that is not two minutes.** Rule **601(a)(4)** makes it a minor for unsportsmanlike conduct to *"[s]hoot[] the puck after the whistle, if in the opinion of the Official such shot was avoidable"*. **601(c)(10)** is a **misconduct** for a player who *"[s]hoots or bats the puck outside of the playing area during a stoppage of play (when not done in protest of an official's decision or in the direction of a spectator)"*. **601(d)(7)** makes that same act a **game misconduct** *"when done in protest of an official's decision"*, and **601(d)(9)** makes a **game misconduct** of a puck shot *"in the general direction of an on-ice official, or in the direction of a spectator during a stoppage of play"*. The **Note to 601(d)** reaches a **match penalty** for a player who *"shoots the puck directly at an official or spectator in a manner that recklessly endangers the official or spectator."* ⚠️ **Which of 610(c) and 601(c)(10) governs the plain after-whistle clear, USA Hockey does not say.** The two rules describe the same act at different tiers, in different sections, and neither refers to the other. **No official interpretation deciding it was found**: the USA Hockey *Casebook* was searched for it, and its Rule 610 situations reach a puck put out of play only **during** play — Situation 5 turns on a goalkeeper who batted a rebound out of the rink without ever having gained control, *"No. Rule Reference 610(c)"* — while none of its Rule 601 situations concerns a puck put outside the playing area at all. **That is a reading of two rules side by side, not a ruling. Assume the misconduct is available and do not test it.** ⚠️ **And do not convert that misconduct into "ten minutes":** USA Hockey Rule 404's Note authorises affiliates and governing bodies, *"(Except for Adults)"*, to reduce a misconduct's length where periods are short, so the number is your classification's rather than the book's. **The NHL and the IIHF write no rung above the minor for this at all.** NHL Rule **63.9** lists the delay-of-game infractions — *"[d]eliberately shooting the puck out of play"* is the first of them — and scopes the whole list to *"a penalty (minor, bench minor, penalty shot or awarded goal)"*. The nearest misconduct either book carries is for a **different act**: knocking or shooting the puck *"out of the reach of an official who is retrieving it"* (NHL 39.4(ii); IIHF 39.4(II)). *(That negative is a search of both books for the puck leaving the playing area, for shooting and batting the puck, and of Rules 39, 63 and 75 read through; a puck sent at an official is reached in both, but as supplementary discipline under 40.4 rather than as a rung of this rule.)* **What is NHL and IIHF only is the strict-liability limb:** USA Hockey and Hockey Canada reach a skater's accidental clear from his own end not at all — under **USA Hockey Rule 631(d)** *"if the puck is accidentally shot or deflected out of play … a last play face-off will occur in the zone where the puck was shot or the deflection occurred"*, a draw rather than a penalty — though Hockey Canada 10.1(v) names a goaltender who shoots or bats it *"directly out of the playing surface, with their stick"*, and unlike 10.1(ii) it carries no deliberateness word of its own. ⚠️ **Whether that silence makes it strict liability is a reading of the rule's structure, not something the book settles**, because Rule 10.1's preamble reaches only a player or team who *"deliberately causes a stoppage of play or delays the restart of play"* and 10.1(a) assesses the minor on a player who *"in the opinion of the Referee, is deliberately delaying the game"* — and none of the twelve Interpretations to Rule 10.1(a) settles the intent question. ⚠️ **One of them is addressed to this limb, though, and it grants the goaltender two escapes worth knowing.** **Interpretation 5** — *"Clarifications as it pertains to goaltenders shooting or batting the puck directly out of play"* — provides that where the goaltender *"shoots the puck directly out of the playing surface where there is no glass, such as at the Players' Bench or Penalty Bench, **or if the puck touches the glass or deflects off a player or official, no penalty will be assessed"***; and it adds a rink condition on top — *"[i]n order to assess a penalty to a goaltender under this provision, both ends of the rink must be similar. If there is glass at one end only, then the rule would not apply."* **Those escapes are about where the puck went and what it touched. Intent is not among them**, so the reading question stands: under Hockey Canada, assume the harsher reading of (v) and ask your league. On the (iii) limb the determining factor is **where the puck was when you hit it**. Over the glass behind the bench counts; **into the bench does not**; **directly off a faceoff does not**; and it does not apply where there is no glass. ⚠️ **The IIHF writes the bench clause wider than the NHL and then narrows the carve-out further in its Handbook, so do not read the bench as somewhere to aim.** Its **Rule 63.2(III)** carves out *"except where there is no protective glass"* where the NHL writes *"no glass"*, and assesses the penalty *"when the puck is shot over the protective glass **surrounding** the Players' bench"* where the NHL writes *"'behind'"* — *"surrounding"* appears to reach glass at the **sides** of the bench that *"behind"* does not obviously reach, though neither book explains the difference and no officiating source was found that settles the reading. And the **IIHF Situation Handbook**, at **Situation 63.24** in the same words in the 2025/26 (v1.1) and 2026/27 editions, takes a clear that goes **over** the glass and *lands* in the Players' Bench and still calls the minor: *"the determining factor when deciding whether a delay of game penalty is to be assessed, is where the puck leaves the playing surface. If you use an imaginary plane that continues upwards to the roof from the top of the glass, should the puck break that plane as it goes out of play, then a minor penalty is to be assessed regardless as to the final resting place of the puck. When the puck is shot **directly** into the Players' Bench (where there is no glass), no penalty is to be assessed."* **Situation 63.28** adds the glove: a player whose stick has broken and who **scoops the puck over the glass behind the bench with a hand** takes the minor, *"considered shooting or batting the puck with the hand resulting in the puck leaving the playing surface."* **Situation 63.3** is the one that helps — where the puck *"clears the glass at a point in the Penalty Bench where the glass is lower than the normal height around the rink, no penalty shall be assessed."* The Handbook ranks below the rule book on its own Foreword, so read these as how it is officiated rather than as the rule. ⚠️ **One caution for the reader under IIHF rules: do not assume a skate falls outside this penalty.** The strict-liability limb reaches only a player who shoots or bats the puck *"(using their hand or stick)"* directly out of his defending zone (IIHF Rule 63.2(III)). But the **IIHF Situation Handbook 2025/26** puts exactly this case — *"A defending player, skating towards the attacking player, kicks at the puck as it goes by to block the shot or pass. The puck goes directly off the foot and over the glass. Does the minor penalty for delay of game apply?"* (Situation 63.19) — and **Version 1.1 of August 2025 answers *"Yes."***, citing no rule. Version 1.0 of June 2025 answered the opposite: *"No. Rule 63.2 (III) states that the player must have shot or batted the puck using the hand or the stick."* The answer has since held across a season boundary: the 2026/27 Handbook (Version 1.0, June 2026) puts Situation 63.19 in the same words and answers *"Yes."* again, while the 2026/27 Rulebook's 63.2(III) is unchanged, still naming only *"their hand or stick"*. ⚠️ **Whether that is a contradiction at all turns on a single word, and neither document defines it.** The deliberate limb already quoted, 63.2(II), reaches a *"leg, foot, arm"* from anywhere on the ice. If *"deliberately"* there attaches to the **act** — the kick — then a player who kicks at the puck is inside (II), the Handbook's "Yes" is (II) and nothing conflicts. If it attaches to the **outcome** — meaning to put the puck out — then (II) does not reach him, only (III) is left, and (III) names hand or stick alone. **The Handbook does not say which reading it is using, because it cites no rule.** ⚠️ **And 63.19 is not the Handbook's only answer on a kicked puck. Situation 63.34** — *"A defending player in the defending zone kicks the puck causing it to go over the glass and out of play. Does the minor penalty for delay of game apply?"* — answers **"Yes"**, also citing no rule, and does so in **all three editions alike**: v1.0 and v1.1 of 2025/26, and 2026/27. So v1.0 carried both answers at the same time. **Why 63.19 changed is stated nowhere, and any account of it would be inference about an unstated intention**; what is on the page is that the Handbook now answers the kicked puck the same way twice. ⚠️ **One Handbook answer runs the other way, and in the goalkeeper's favour. Situation 63.29** — a goalkeeper moving post to post whose stick save propels the puck over the glass — answers *"No. The goalkeeper was simply making a save and did not 'shoot' the puck over the glass. Rule 63.2. talks about deliberate, the save cannot be seen as falling under this criteria."* It gives two reasons, and **one of them, deliberateness, is a word 63.2(III) does not contain**, so on that reasoning the Handbook is reading (III) narrower than its own text. It is in the same words in all three editions. **The Handbook ranks itself below the rule book** — its Foreword says *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis"* — so where the two do conflict the rule text governs and the hand-or-stick words stand; but the document your officials are trained on has answered the kicked puck *"Yes"* in every edition of 63.34 and in the last two of 63.19, so **play as though a skate can cost you the two minutes, and clear it with your stick.** **This is about the IIHF's documents only.** The NHL publishes its own situation handbook, which has not been read for this document, so nothing here says how the NHL would answer the same case. This penalty is **challengeable** if the puck deflected on the way out (Rule 38.2(d)), and a failed challenge adds a further minor.
- **Delay of game — other forms** (Rule 63.2) — freezing the puck unnecessarily, **deliberately shooting or batting the puck out of the playing area from anywhere on the ice, in play or after a stoppage** (63.2(ii), above), deliberately displacing the net, a skater falling on or gathering the puck into his body, adjusting equipment.
- **Delay of game — goalie outside the trapezoid** (NHL Rules 27.8, 63.2(viii); IIHF Rule 27.7) — see section 7.
- **Goalie participating in the play beyond the centre red line** (NHL Rule 27.7; IIHF Rule 27.6; USA Hockey Rule 407(c); Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c), penalised as interference under its Rule 8.3(a)) — a minor in all four books, but **the books split on whether the puck's position or the goalie's skates decide it**. See section 7.
- **Too many men** (Rule 74) — covered above.
- **Unsportsmanlike conduct** (Rule 75) — obscene or abusive language or gestures **directed at any person**, hair-pulling, biting, grabbing a face mask, throwing objects onto the ice **from the bench or another off-ice location** (on-ice throwing is Rule 53: 53.6 is a match penalty where a player *"attempts to or deliberately injures"* by throwing a stick, and 53.7 awards a penalty shot in the narrower case of a defending player throwing at the puck or puck carrier in his own defending zone, if no goal results), or **sitting on the boards with skates exposed during play** — that last one, and only that one, gets the **team** a single warning first; nothing else on this list does. Escalates: minor, then misconduct, then game misconduct if you persist — ⚠️ **though 75.4(v) and 75.5(vi) preface that ladder with *"In general"*, and both subrules carry limbs that start at a misconduct or a game misconduct with nothing below them, so it is the usual course rather than an order the referee must follow** (the un-gated limbs are listed under [Talking to officials](#talking-to-officials)). ⚠️ **And do not read that escalation as the ceiling, and never touch an opponent's cage.** The NHL's own clause covering those physical acts ends with a route out of it — *"If warranted, and specifically when injury results, the Referee may apply Rule 21 – Match Penalties"* — and **under USA Hockey a facemask grab is not on this escalation at all**: Rule 622 — *Holding an Opponent*, which is where to look for it — puts a **major plus a game misconduct** on any player who uses their hand *"to rub, grab or hold the facemask of an opponent"* (622(b)), and 622(c) allows a match penalty *"for reckless endangerment"*. **And a USA Hockey game misconduct is not just tonight**: Rule 404(b) says the player *"shall also be suspended for the team's next game (the game already appearing on the schedule of that team at the time of the infraction)"*, automatically — and where the game misconduct comes from a second major in the game, *"there shall be a minimum two-game suspension."* **The other two books write it too, and one of them harder.** **IIHF 75.2(II)** lists *"grabbing hold of a face mask"* too — not biting, and it adds banging a stick on the boards or glass — and routes it onward to a differently named rule on a different trigger: its pointer reads *"Rule 21 – Dangerous Actions"*, not the NHL's Rule 21 Match Penalties, and it fires *"if the Referee classifies physical offences committed to be serious and severe, based on the degree of force"*, with **no injury required**. IIHF 21.1 is a major plus an automatic game misconduct. **Hockey Canada does not file this under unsportsmanlike conduct at all** — Rule 7.1, *Attempt to Injure or Deliberate Injury*, makes grabbing *"the facial protector, helmet, chin strap, or throat protector"* a **double minor** even at a minimal degree of violence, a major plus game misconduct on the referee's reading of the violence and mandatory if it injures, and a **match penalty** where it is done *"with a moderate or high degree of violence and/or using this to gain an advantage or to inflict punishment or injury"*. **Do not count on two minutes in any of them.** The NHL's escalation clause turns on *"if warranted"* and only names injury as its clearest case, and Rule 21's own trigger is a player who *"deliberately attempts to injure"*, which needs no injury at all — the NHL index lists a facemask grab under *Injury* with a pointer to 21.1. **Never touch an opponent's cage, and assume you have no floor.** **Hair-pulling sits in the same clause as the facemask** in the NHL and IIHF books, so the same escalation reaches it, and Hockey Canada names the two together at 7.1(b) and 7.1(c)(ii). **USA Hockey does not use the word at all**, so do not carry 622(b) across to it.
- **Biting** — no book treats this as an ordinary foul, and the four route it four ways. The **NHL** lists it in the unsportsmanlike clause above. The **IIHF** does not list it there but reaches it at 23.8(IV), where it *"could result in"* a game misconduct. **Hockey Canada is the most direct** — its Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.1(e) says *"biting is an attempt to injure. A Match penalty will be assessed…"* **USA Hockey does not use the word anywhere in its Playing Rules — but its separate Casebook rules on it outright, twice, of an opponent.** Casebook **Rule 602, Situation 3** puts the question directly — *"If a player 'bites' an opponent during an altercation, should this be considered reckless endangerment and a match penalty assessed?"* — and answers *"Yes. Rule Reference 602(a)."* Its stated reason: *"The only possible purpose of this action is to endanger the opponent, so a match penalty must be assessed."* 602(a) puts a **match penalty** on any player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"*. ⚠️ **And in a fight the two penalties stack rather than merge.** Casebook **Rule 615, Situation 3** has one player bite another on the finger before the Linesperson can break the fight up, and rules that the referee assesses *both* *"the match penalty (attempt to injure) and major plus game misconduct for fighting"* — *"Rule References 615(a) and 602(a)."* (Its third use, Casebook **Rule 601, Situation 23**, is a player biting a *Linesperson*: a match penalty under 601(e.1).) USA Hockey's own discipline policy puts a 602(a) match penalty in its most severe category. On the printed suspension ranges it may be the harshest of the four.
- **Diving / embellishment** (Rule 64) — a minor on any player who **blatantly dives, embellishes a fall or reaction, or feigns injury**. A goalie who deliberately initiates contact with an attacker, or acts to make incidental contact look like more, can be penalised under this rule too. 🇬🇧 **In Britain it costs you twelve minutes, not two, and this is new for 2026-27.** The IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 amend Rule 64 in one line — *"Adding a misconduct to a minor. The required call is now 2 plus 10"* — so an embellishment call under those rules is a minor **plus a ten-minute misconduct**. The 2025-26 edition had no Rule 64 amendment at all.
- **Closing your hand on the puck** (Rule 67.2) — you **may catch the puck out of the air**, but you must **immediately place or knock it down to the ice**. It is a minor if you catch it and **skate with it**, if you **cover it on the ice to conceal it**, or if you **pick it up off the ice** during play. **USA Hockey splits this differently:** Rule 618(a) makes a closed hand that is not immediately dropped a stoppage and last-play faceoff rather than a penalty, attaches the minor to picking the puck up, and still makes a hand closed on a puck **in the crease** a penalty shot or optional minor. **Hockey Canada 10.2(a)(iii)** gates its minor on the closed hand *"gain[ing] an advantage"*. **If a skater falls on or gathers a puck that is itself inside his own goal crease, it is a penalty shot** (Rule 67.4); outside the crease it is a minor. **The deciding factor is where the puck is, not where the player is** — Rule 63.6 says so in terms: *"If the puck is outside the crease and gathered into the body of a player (other than the goalkeeper) who is inside the crease, minor penalty."* **Note: this is a penalty, not a faceoff violation** — the fact that it often happens at a draw does not change what it is. ⚠️ **And if you are one of the two taking that draw, the permission this bullet opens with is suspended for you.** The note to NHL and IIHF Rule 67.2, printed again inside the face-off rule as **Rule 76.5**, bars *"the two players involved in the actual face-off (the centers)"* from playing the puck with their hand *"until such time as a third player (from either team) has at least touched the puck."* That is *playing* it, not merely closing on it, and it falls on the two takers and on nobody else on the ice. **No counterpart was found in USA Hockey or Hockey Canada** — see the note under Sources for exactly what was searched.
- **Hand pass** (Rule 79) — you may bat or push the puck with an open hand, but play is stopped if you **direct it to a teammate** and your team then gains possession. **No hand pass is called in your own defending zone** — that is a free tool for a defenceman under pressure. The two centres at a faceoff may not **play** the puck with a hand at all — not merely bat it — until a third player from either team has touched it (the note to NHL and IIHF Rule 67.2, printed again at Rule 76.5), and a taker who *"attempts to win the face-off by batting the puck with their hand"* takes a **minor for delay of game — faceoff violation** (Rule 67.2).
- **High-sticking the puck** (Rule 80) — playing the puck with your stick **above shoulder height**. The whistle is conditional, not automatic: under Rule 80.1 play stops *"when a puck is struck with a high stick and subsequently comes into the possession and control of a player from the offending team"* (including the player who struck it), directly or by deflection. Otherwise play continues — and it expressly continues if you batted it to an opponent or a defender batted it into his own net. A goal cannot be scored by contacting the puck **above the height of the crossbar** — and the reference height for the goal is the **crossbar**, not the shoulders. ⚠️ **Hockey Canada turns this puck rule into a *person* rule in two of its divisions, and that is the one place where a swing at a high puck stops being a mere stoppage.** Its Rule 6.9(c): *“In Minor of Female divisions, any player who high-sticks **or attempts to high-stick** the puck and, in doing so, contacts an opposing player with their stick, will be penalized under Rule 7.6 – Head Contact.”* (*“Minor of Female”* is the book’s own slip for *or*.) So in Hockey Canada minor and female hockey a missed swing at a puck above your shoulders that catches somebody is judged under the head-contact rule, whose preamble says it *“supersedes any other rules, with the exception of Rule 7.10 – Fighting”*: a minor for accidental contact, a double minor for intentional, a **mandatory** major plus game misconduct where head contact that would otherwise be a minor injures (7.6(b)), and a match penalty at 7.6(c). **Minor and female divisions only — 6.9(c) states that scope in its own words**, and no such routing appears in the NHL’s Rule 80, either IIHF edition’s Rule 80, USA Hockey’s 621(d) or CARHA’s 62(d), each of which keeps the puck case a stoppage rather than a penalty. Adult play under Hockey Canada is not covered by it, and the stick-fouls list above has what *is*.

### Broken sticks

This is a penalty, it catches beginners constantly, and almost nobody is taught it.

**A broken stick is one which, in the opinion of the referee, is "unfit for normal play"** (Rule 10.3). That is a judgement, not a definition — a shaft snapped in half is obvious, but so is a blade hanging off, and a stick you think is still usable may not be.

**The player rule (Rule 10.3):**

- **You may keep playing without a stick.** Rule 10.3 says so in terms: *"A player without a stick may participate in the game."* There is nothing wrong with backchecking, blocking a lane or finishing a check empty-handed.
- **What you may not do is keep hold of the broken one.** *"A player whose stick is broken may participate in the game provided he drops the broken stick. A minor penalty shall be imposed for an infraction of this rule."* So the instant it goes, **let go of it** — do not carry it to the bench while the play is still live — and under the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, do not carry it at all — and above all do not play the puck with the stub. This is the version that costs beer-leaguers two minutes: they break a stick in the corner, hang on to it out of instinct, and poke at the puck with what is left.
- **Getting a replacement.** You may take one **handed to you from your own bench**, **handed to you by a teammate on the ice**, or you may **pick up your own unbroken stick, or a teammate's, off the ice**. What is penalised is that you **"throw or shoot a stick to a teammate on the ice"** — the IIHF's version of the same rule spells the same prohibition out as *"throw, toss, slide or shoot"* — or **pick up and play with an opponent's stick**. A skater also **may not play using a goalkeeper's stick**. Each of those is a minor.
- **If a stick is thrown to you from the bench, you are not the one penalised** — you may use it; the person who threw it earns a **bench minor**.
- 🇬🇧 **In Britain a broken stick must go on the ice the instant it breaks, and you may not carry it anywhere — this is new for 2026-27.** The IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 add a Rule 10.3 that the 2025-26 edition did not have — *"Skating with a broken stick anywhere on the ice is not permitted under IHUK In-House Rules. A player whose stick is broken must drop their stick immediately. Failure to do so will incur a 2min penalty for illegal equipment"* — so the USA Hockey allowance set out below, which lets you carry a broken stick to your bench on conditions, is **not yours**: the In-House rule writes no proximity test and no out-of-play test, because it reaches the act *"anywhere on the ice."* **Hockey Canada writes the same flat drop** at Rule 3.2(a) — *"A player whose stick has been broken must drop all pieces of the broken stick to the ice immediately"* — penalised under its Rule 10.6 Illegal Equipment. ⚠️ **The In-House rule is the skater rule only**, headed *"Broken Stick: Player"*; IHUK writes no goalkeeper counterpart, and its Introduction makes silence adoption — *"Unless otherwise stated, all rules will be enforced in line with the IIHF Rule Book"* — so on the document's own terms IIHF Rule 10.4 still governs a British goalie. **In practice this changes nothing for you if you already do what this section says: stick breaks, hand opens.**

**The goalkeeper rule is different, and it is the difference that matters (Rule 10.4):**

- **Under the NHL and IIHF books a goalie may carry on playing with a broken stick** until a stoppage, or until a teammate legally hands them a new one. A goalie is not required to drop it — the opposite of the skater rule. ⚠️ **That permission is the NHL's and the IIHF's, and neither of the other two books gives it to you.** USA Hockey writes no equivalent of it, and **Hockey Canada bars it outright** — Rule 3.2(c): *"A goaltender may not continue to play with a broken stick of any kind."* Under either of those books a goalie who plays on with a broken stick takes a minor.
- **The replacement must be passed hand to hand.** A teammate may not throw or shoot it to them; doing so is a minor to **that teammate** for throwing the stick, not to the goalie for receiving it.
- **A goalie may not go to the bench for a replacement.** They must get it from a teammate on the ice. And **a goalie may play with a skater's stick** in the meantime, until a proper goalkeeper's stick reaches them. ⚠️ **Three books write the bench bar in terms; Hockey Canada does not — but stay in your net under all four.** **NHL Rule 10.4 and IIHF Rule 10.4** carry that sentence to the same effect, in their own words: the IIHF's is *"A goalkeeper whose stick is 'broken or illegal' may not go to the Players' bench for a replacement but must receive their stick from a teammate as described above"*, and the NHL's is the same sentence with *"his stick"* and without the closing cross-reference. **USA Hockey 605(c)** writes it narrower and attaches a price: *"A goalkeeper whose stick is broken may not go to the players' bench for a replacement during a stoppage of play, but must receive their stick from a teammate. For an infraction of this rule a minor penalty for delay of game shall be assessed to the goalkeeper."* ⚠️ **Hockey Canada writes no bench bar, so do not cite it for one** — the bar it does write, further down, is on playing *on* with a broken stick, which is a different rule. Its Rule 3.2(b), the clause that says where a replacement stick may come from, is written for *"A player"*; 3.2(c) requires only that *"[a]t the next stoppage of play, the goaltender must receive a regular goaltender's stick"*, without saying from where; and **Interpretation 1 to 3.2(c) runs the other way, permissively** — a teammate *"may carry a new stick back to the goaltender, allowing the goaltender to receive a new goaltender's stick without proceeding to the Players' Bench"* (the same ruling appears again at Interpretation 5 to Rule 10.6(a) / 3.2). **It prices the trip by a different route instead, and the advice survives intact.** Rule 10.1's delay-of-game list reaches *"[a] goaltender who goes to the Players' Bench to adjust any equipment without being replaced by the alternate goaltender"*; Interpretation 10 to 10.1(a) has the referee warn a goaltender who asks to go that *"if they go, they must be replaced by the alternate goaltender"*; and **Interpretation 11 to 10.1(a) prices it whatever the reason** — once the referee notices the goaltender at the bench, the team must *"replace the goaltender with the alternate goaltender, keep the original goaltender in the net and be assessed a Minor penalty, play with six players, or take their time-out, if allowed."* So under Hockey Canada the trip is not forbidden; it costs you your place in net, or two minutes. **Take the stick from a teammate in every book.** **The separate permission to use a skater's stick in the meantime does hold in all four:** USA Hockey's Casebook at Rule 605, Situation 5 allows it *"[a]s long as the stick does not exceed the maximum measurements set forth for goalkeeper sticks"*, and Hockey Canada 3.2(c) allows *"a player's stick that has been handed to them until the next stoppage of play"*, a stick that *"will not be considered illegal equipment"*, with a proper goalkeeper's stick required at that stoppage.

**Practically:** as a skater, train the reflex — *stick breaks, hand opens.* Get to the bench for a new one if you can do it without leaving a hole, and play the shift stickless if you cannot. As a defenceman losing a stick in your own zone, drop it, get your body into the lane and let a forward hand you theirs. **As a goalie, the practical answer does not vary by book: you are not going to the bench for one, so call for a stick from a teammate on the ice and play blocker, glove and body until it arrives.** As a forward whose defenceman has lost a stick in front of the net, **hand yours over** — that is what the rule contemplates, and you can go to the bench far more cheaply than they can.

> **Rule-set differences:**
> - **IIHF Rules 10.3 and 10.4** reach the same results as the NHL's, but they are not the same words and they add two things. **The opening clause of 10.4 is the IIHF's own:** a goalie may play on *"until they have one legally **handed** to them by a teammate"* where the NHL writes *"until he has one legally **provided** to him by a teammate"* — and the IIHF then spells the method out, *"The stick must be passed from hand to hand to the goalkeeper by a teammate on the ice"*, where the NHL says only that the teammate *"cannot throw or shoot it to the goalkeeper"*. Hand to hand is the answer under both. **The first addition** is at 10.4: an *intact* stick a goalie has accidentally dropped may be **pushed or slid** to them along the ice by a nearby teammate, provided it does not interfere with play. **The second is at 10.3**, where the IIHF spells the throwing bar out more fully than the NHL does — it penalises a player who *"throw, toss, slide or shoot a stick to a teammate (skater) on the ice"*, where the NHL's list is *"throws or shoots"* alone. Read the two together and the contrast is the useful part: a stick **slid to a skater** is named as a minor, while an intact one **slid to the goalie who dropped it** is expressly allowed.
> - **USA Hockey Rule 605** is stricter in two ways worth knowing. First, the replacement sources are narrower: 605(b) allows a replacement *"only … from the player's bench or from a teammate on the ice"* — it does **not** carry the NHL's express permission to pick a loose stick up off the ice. Second, **not knowing your stick is broken is not a defence.** The rule text allows *"reasonable time to be aware that the stick is broken"*, but the casebook is blunt about the limit, at Rule 605, Situation 2: a player *"is accountable for their actions, so the minor penalty shall be assessed if they participate in the play in any manner … there is no provision of the rule that allows them to legally participate in play."* "Participate" is defined there as playing or attempting to play the puck, body-checking or attempting to, or *"continuing any action that directly affects the play."* You **may** carry the broken stick to the bench, but only if you are close to it, out of the play, and go immediately — and Situation 3 adds a condition the others do not: not if you would have to *"go a great distance and/or in the proximity of opposing players"*, in which case *"the stick should be dropped or a penalty assessed."* ⚠️ **That situation also gives the reason the rule exists, and it is not bookkeeping:** *"The intent of the rule is to manage the safety of all of the players participating in the game – a broken stick can certainly be considered a safety hazard."* The book does not say what the hazard is, and this guide does not fill that in for it — what it does say is that the drop-it rule is a safety rule. ⚠️ **That carry is USA Hockey's alone — no other book here writes one.** The **IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 forbid it in terms**, at their own Rule 10.3: *"Skating with a broken stick anywhere on the ice is not permitted under IHUK In-House Rules. A player whose stick is broken must drop their stick immediately. Failure to do so will incur a 2min penalty for illegal equipment"*. **Hockey Canada Rule 3.2(a)** requires you to *"drop all pieces of the broken stick to the ice immediately"* — though its own Note 1 adds that no penalty follows where a player discards the pieces *"by tossing them to the side of the playing surface (not over the boards) in such a way that they do not interfere with play or opposing players."* And the NHL and IIHF Rule 10.3 quoted above grants no carry either — it permits you to *participate* only once you have dropped it.
> - **USA Hockey's goalkeeper clause (605(c)) covers only where the stick comes from** — the goalie may not go to the bench for one during a stoppage and must receive it from a teammate, on pain of a minor for delay of game. It contains **no equivalent of the NHL's Rule 10.4 permission to keep playing with a broken stick**, and 605(a) is written to cover *"a player or goalkeeper"* — *"Any player whose stick is broken must drop their stick prior to participating in the play. A player or goalkeeper shall be allowed reasonable time to be aware that the stick is broken."* **The Casebook writes a goalkeeper no allowance either**: its Rule 605 situations reach the goalkeeper only at Situation 5, which is about *which* stick they may use once theirs has broken, and Rule 301, Situation 6 answers the same question again. ⚠️ **On the rule as written, a USA Hockey goalie who plays on with a broken stick takes the minor a skater would.** Do not assume the NHL allowance travels to USA Hockey play; if you are a goalie under USA Hockey rules, ask your league.
> - **Hockey Canada is the one book that bars it in terms, and it names the goalie twice.** Rule 3.2(c) reads *"A goaltender may not continue to play with a broken stick of any kind"*, and 3.2(d) penalises any violation of the rule under Rule 10.6(a) – Illegal Equipment, a minor. What 3.2(c) does allow is *"a player's stick that has been handed to them until the next stoppage of play"*, at which point a proper goalkeeper's stick must reach them. ⚠️ **And here the goalie can be penalised for taking the replacement.** Interpretation 1 to 3.2(c) requires a teammate to **carry and hand** the stick over: a player who *"throws, shoots, kicks, or slides"* it takes a minor under 10.5(a) — and *"[i]f the goaltender picks up the stick, they will be assessed a Minor penalty under Rule 10.6 (a) – Illegal Equipment."* **Under the NHL and IIHF books the receiving goalie is expressly not penalised; under Hockey Canada, leave a slid stick where it lies** — the same interpretation says *"[n]o penalty is to be assessed against the goaltender in this situation if they do not pick up the stick."*

### When penalties add up

⚠️ **Under Hockey Canada, three stick minors in one game ends your night.** Rule 4.8(b)(i) assesses a **Game Ejection** *"when a player is assessed three Minor penalties for 'stick infractions' in the same game"*, and names them: butt-ending (9.1), cross-checking (9.2), slashing (9.3), spearing (9.4) and high-sticking in junior and senior (9.5).
