Interpretation 2 to 4.8(b) counts a **double minor as one infraction** for this purpose. **No other book counts *stick* minors specifically** — but do not read that as no accumulation risk elsewhere. **USA Hockey Rule 401(b)** counts penalties of any kind: a game misconduct on a fourth in one game outside Adult classifications, and **an Adult player is "immediately ejected" on a fifth**. Rule 411 then adds a season-long ladder over **major** penalties for butt-ending, cross-checking, high-sticking, slashing, spearing and tripping — a different count from 401(b)'s. A rec player under Hockey Canada taking two stick minors should know the third is not another two minutes — and a rec player under USA Hockey should know the count that ends their night is not stick-specific at all.

### What actually gets called at rec level

In my reading of the rulebooks against how adult recreational hockey is officiated, the calls you should expect to see, in rough order of frequency:

1. **Hooking, slashing and tripping.** Overwhelmingly the most common, and overwhelmingly the product of being beaten and reaching.
2. **Holding and interference.** Both are usually late-arriving players grabbing rather than skating.
3. **High-sticking.** In leagues where most players are not used to controlling a stick in traffic, this is common, the **double minor for blood** is a real risk, and visors are not universal in beer-league play. **Under the IIHF book — which is the British one — and under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, the same stick can reach a major and a game misconduct** — so find out which book your league actually runs before you rely on the ceiling being a double minor. ⚠️ **And do not assume a rec league is the lenient one.** Under **CARHA Hockey Rule 62(b)** — the one standalone adult-rec rule book this document cites — a high stick that contacts an opponent above the shoulders *starts* at a **major**, accidental or intentional, *“whether or not injury results”*, with no minor rung above the shoulders. ⚠️ **And the major is not the ceiling there either** — the Note to 62(b) adds a **game misconduct** where the contact was intentional, or where an accidental one injures, so only an accidental high stick that hurts nobody leaves you a major alone. **CARHA-affiliated leagues only**, but it is the book to read before guessing.
4. **Too many men.** Bench discipline is worse in rec hockey than anywhere else.
5. **Body checking**, in the very large number of rec leagues that are **non-check**. Officials in those leagues call contact tightly and inconsistently by NHL standards, because the standard they are applying is not the NHL's.
6. **Roughing and unsportsmanlike conduct** after the whistle.

Conversely, the calls you will rarely see in rec hockey: **delay of game for puck over the glass** (many rinks do not have full glass, and the rule does not apply where there is none), **goalie trapezoid violations** (neither North American governing body's book has a trapezoid, and CARHA's has none either — **but that is not the same as your goalie being unrestricted behind the net: USA Hockey Rule 614(c) still penalises *freezing* the puck outside the privileged area, and Hockey Canada bars freezing it at all once the goaltender has left the crease, with no warning**, see §7), and **diving**.

**The practical conclusion:** in rec hockey, the entire penalty-avoidance problem reduces to *stop reaching with your stick, and stop being late*. Both are fixed by conditioning and gap control, not by trying harder to be careful.

---

## 5. Delayed Penalties

### Two different things share this name

The rulebook's **Rule 26 "Delayed Penalties"** is about a **third penalty** to a team that already has two players in the box: the third player's clock does not start until one of the first two expires, and a substitute keeps the team at three skaters (Rule 26.1). A team never plays below **three skaters plus a goalie**.

What players and commentators mean by "delayed penalty" is different: it is the **delayed call** under **Rule 15.1**. That is what this section covers.

### How the delayed call works

If a player of the team **in control of the puck** commits an infraction, the referee **blows the whistle immediately**.

If a player of the team **not in control** commits an infraction, the referee **raises his arm** and lets play continue (signal 29.8). Play is stopped, and the penalty imposed, **when the team to be penalised gains control of the puck**.

Read that condition carefully: **control**, not touch. A puck deflecting off a penalised-team player's skate does not end the play. Play continues until they actually have it.

### Pulling the goalie

Because play cannot be stopped until the offending team gains control, the non-offending team is **guaranteed possession or a stoppage**. There is no risk of a goal against from open play. So the goalie skates to the bench and the team plays with a **sixth attacker**.

This is a tactic, not a rule — nothing in the rulebook requires it. But it is universal at every level where the goalie can be trusted to make the trip, and you should treat it as automatic.

Two rules that constrain it:

- The goalie leaving the crease legitimately to be replaced by an extra attacker is **not** penalised for leaving the crease if an altercation then breaks out (**NHL Rule 27.6**; **IIHF Rule 27.5**, same words, different number), and does **not** wave off a potential icing (Rule 81.3).
- **In regular-season overtime**, pulling the goalie **forfeits the guaranteed standings point** if you lose while he is off — **except** when he was pulled for a delayed penalty (Rule 84.2).

### What happens if you score

- **Minor penalty:** if the **non-offending team scores** during the delay, **the minor is not imposed at all** (Rule 15.2). It is washed out entirely — you get the goal instead of the power play. If two or more minors were coming, the **captain chooses** which one is washed out.
- **Double minor:** one half is washed out; the player serves the remaining **two minutes** (Rule 18.2).
- **Major and match penalties are imposed regardless** of whether a goal is scored (Rule 15.2).

If the non-offending team, with their net empty, **puts the puck into their own goal**, that is a goal for the offending team and the delayed penalty is still assessed. In regular-season overtime this ends the game immediately in the offending team's favour (Rule 84.2).

### What to do as a player during a delayed penalty

- **Look for your bench immediately.** The moment you see the referee's arm go up and you are not directly involved in the play, the extra attacker is coming and someone has to come off. If you are the closest player to the bench and not part of the immediate play, you are the one.
- **Keep the puck.** The single rule of a delayed penalty is: **do not give the offending team control.** That means no risky cross-ice passes through the middle, no blind dumps that they will simply pick up.
- **Do not force a shot from a bad angle** just to "use" the extra man. A shot that misses and rims around is exactly how the offending team gains control and ends your six-on-five for a two-minute five-on-four instead. A missed shot that they collect has cost you the better of the two options.
- **Regroup if the zone is not open.** Take it back to your own end if you have to. You have the puck and they cannot force a whistle without taking it from you.
- **Six attackers changes the shape, not the principles.** Most teams simply add a fourth high forward and play a 2-1-2 or an umbrella. See [Special Teams](../systems/special_teams.md) and [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md).
- **If you are the team about to be penalised:** touch the puck as fast as possible to end it. Diving on it, deflecting it out, batting it — anything that gets you control. A hand pass in your defending zone is legal (Rule 79.2) and it counts.

**USA Hockey note:** if a team **ices the puck during a delayed penalty against their opponents**, the ensuing faceoff is at a **neutral-zone spot nearest the icing team's defending zone**, not in their end (Rule 624(a) note). The NHL rulebook contains no equivalent provision, largely because in the NHL play would already be whistled the moment the offending team gained control.

---

## 6. Power Play and Penalty Kill Basics

This section covers only what the **rules** determine. The tactics — formations, entries, seam passes, what to do when the puck goes to the corner — live in [Special Teams](../systems/special_teams.md).

### The strengths you will actually see

- **5-on-4** — one minor. The standard power play.
- **5-on-3** — two overlapping minors. Lasts only as long as both are on the clock.
- **4-on-4** — one minor to each team at the same stoppage, with nothing else on the clock (Rule 19.1). Not a power play at all; both teams have a man off. Enormous open ice. 🇬🇧 **This trigger is NHL-only.** IIHF Rule 19.1 requires immediate substitution and its casebook says the teams play five a side. Britain reaches 4-on-4 by other routes — a minor against a major at the same stoppage — just not by coincidental minors.
- **4-on-3** — a minor to each team while one team is already down one, or a penalty during 3-on-3 overtime (Rule 84.3).
- **3-on-3** — regular-season overtime, or the residue of overlapping penalties in overtime.

Teams **never go below three skaters** (Rules 26.1, 84.3). A third penalty to an already two-men-down team is queued behind the others.

### When a penalty ends early

A **minor or bench minor penalty terminates immediately** when the opposing team scores — but only if **both** conditions hold (Rule 16.2):

1. The team scored against is **shorthanded**, and
2. The team scored against is **serving a minor on the clock**.

The penalty that ends is the one with the **least time remaining**. Two important exceptions:

- **Majors do not end on a goal** (Rule 20.2). Five minutes is five minutes.
- **No penalty expires when a goal is scored on a penalty shot** (Rules 16.2, 24.6).

Coincidental minors do not make either team shorthanded, so they do not expire on a goal either.

### Shorthanded teams may ice the puck — with two exceptions

**A team below the numerical strength of its opponent may shoot the puck the length of the ice with no icing called** (Rule 81.6). This is the structural core of penalty killing. Every kill is built on the fact that clearing the puck costs you nothing but the time it takes them to bring it back.

**It is not unconditional, and it is easy to state bare when it should not be.** There are two exceptions, and the wording of both is quoted in full below.

**Exception 1 — the NHL's empty penalty bench (Rule 81.6).** Quoted in full: *"When a team is 'short-handed' by reason of a major penalty, and they have neglected to ensure there is a player on the penalty bench to exit upon the expiry of the penalty, they will continue to play short-handed but are not permitted to ice the puck. Icing will be called. They may substitute for this penalized player at the next stoppage of play."* (The underlying substitution requirement is Rule 20.3.) This is a bench administration failure rather than a playing decision, but on a five-minute major it is real: somebody must be sitting in the box ready to come out. Note also the timing test in the same rule — when a penalty is **about to expire**, icing is judged at the instant it expires, and a puck released before expiry is not icing.

**Exception 2 — USA Hockey restricts the exemption by classification, and if USA Hockey's book is the one your league runs, this is the one that matters.** The shorthanded nullification in **Rule 624(b)(1)** is prefixed with a classification limit: *"(For all Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult classifications only) The offending team is short-handed (below the on-ice numerical strength of their opponent) when the puck is shot."* Below those levels the nullification simply is not available, so **a shorthanded team cannot ice the puck at all** — an attempted length-of-the-ice clear on the kill comes straight back for a defensive-zone draw. If you play or coach youth hockey under USA Hockey rules, the single biggest structural advantage the NHL penalty kill has does not exist for you, and a kill built on clearing the length of the ice will simply feed the power play repeated defensive-zone faceoffs. (USA Hockey does, however, allow a line change after an icing — see above — so the cost is not identical.)

**IIHF gives the same exemption, with the same "instant the penalty expires" test** (Rule 81.6) — but the IIHF text has only those two paragraphs and **does not carry the NHL's major-penalty carve-out**, so internationally the exemption reads as unconditional.

### Practical consequences

- **On the kill, the clear is the whole game.** You are not trying to make a play; you are trying to make them skate 200 feet again. Get it out, get it down, change.
- **On the power play, the clock is your opponent as much as the killers are.** Two minutes is roughly two possessions if you have to re-enter the zone each time. A failed entry is far more costly than a failed shot.
- **When your penalty is about to expire and you are killing**, the player in the box is a **breakaway waiting to happen**. Somebody has to call it out. See [On-Ice Communication](on_ice_communication.md).
- **4-on-4 is not a power play and should not be played like one.** Both teams have space; both teams are equally exposed to a turnover.

---

## 7. Goaltender-Specific Rules

For the position itself, see [Goaltender](../positions/goaltender.md). This section is the rules layer.

### The crease

The **goal crease** is the light-blue area in front of the net — **8 feet wide at the goal line, 6 feet deep** at its deepest point — and it is a **volume, not an area**: it extends **vertically four feet**, to the height of the top of the goal frame (NHL Rule 1.7). Those figures come from [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md), which covers the rink's geometry and gives the full Rule 1.7 construction, the goaltender's reference marks and the IIHF equivalents.

### Goaltender interference

The governing principle (Rule 69.1) is stated explicitly and is not what most players think it is:

> An attacking player's **position, whether inside or outside the crease, should not by itself determine** whether a goal is allowed.

Goals are disallowed only if **(1)** an attacking player, **by his positioning or by contact**, impairs the goalie's ability to move freely within his crease or defend his goal; or **(2)** an attacking player initiates **intentional or deliberate contact** with the goalie, inside or outside the crease.

⚠️ **That "only if" is a ceiling and not a direction — read it before carrying clause (1) anywhere.** It says one of those two has to be true for a goal to come off the board; it does not say a goal comes off whenever positioning impairs the goalie. **The sentence in 69.1 that actually disallows a goal is keyed to the crease** — an attacking player who *"enters the goal crease"* and, by his actions, impairs the goalkeeper's ability to defend it. [Screening is legal outside the crease](#screening-is-legal-outside-the-crease), below, sets that out with 69.3 and 69.4 — and with the crease line, where what is unsettled is narrower than it sounds. Three of the four books say in terms that the line is part of the crease. What nobody has settled is that the IIHF's own book then answers the same play both ways: **Rule 1.7** puts the marked line inside the crease, while its **Situation 5E** allows the goal to a screen planted on it. [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md) sets that clash out in full.

**Inside the crease** (Rule 69.3): if an attacking player initiates contact with the goalie — **incidental or otherwise** — and a goal is scored, **the goal is disallowed**. This is the strict zone. ⚠️ **Rule 69.3 then has two paragraphs about contact the *goalkeeper* initiates, and they are different paragraphs — the first of them costs you the goal even if you do everything right.** If a goalkeeper, *"in the act of establishing his position within his goal crease"*, initiates contact with an attacking player who is in the crease and that *"results in an impairment of the goalkeeper's ability to defend his goal"*, a goal scored on the play is disallowed. Those are the NHL's words; the IIHF writes the same paragraph in its own house style. **Giving ground does not save that goal, and both books say so in a worked case.** Each puts the fact pattern of an attacking player who *"vacates the position immediately at the time a goal is scored"* and answers *"Goal is disallowed"*, with the reason in the description column: *"Even though the attacking player vacates his position immediately, the contact impairs the goalkeeper's ability to defend his goal"* (NHL Reference Table 14, Situation 6C, and the IIHF the same situation at its 2025/26 Appendix IV, Table 16; the IIHF Situation Handbook puts the same play at Situation 69.6 and answers the same way). ⚠️ **Whether a penalty goes with that one is stated nowhere.** Unlike 6D, 6C's result column says nothing about a penalty in either direction — so do not read "no penalty" out of its silence. ⚠️ **None of that makes giving ground optional — 69.3 goes on to require it, and standing your ground pays nothing on either reading.**

**The next paragraph of 69.3 is the one that names a duty of yours, and it is the paragraph the books then contradict.** If, after any contact by a goalkeeper attempting to establish position, the attacker **does not immediately give ground**, and a goal is scored, it is disallowed and the attacker is penalised for goaltender interference — **a minor under the NHL, whose 69.3 writes *"a minor penalty"*, but the IIHF's 69.3 writes *"the appropriate penalty"* and caps nothing, so under the British reader's book the severity of that clause is left open.** ⚠️ **Do not read that as the NHL capping Rule 69 at a minor, because it does not.** The severity range is common to both books — the parenthetical *"(minor or major, as the Referee deems appropriate)"* appears word for word at **69.2** in the NHL's book and the IIHF's alike, so Rule 69 as a whole reaches a major in each of them. ⚠️ **And 69.2 is not the clause that governs this play, so do not lean on it here.** Its own opening confines it to cases in which an attacking player initiates *"intentional or deliberate"* contact with the goalie — and in the give-ground case it is the **goaltender** who initiated the contact. What separates the two books on this clause is 69.3's own wording and nothing else: the NHL writes a ceiling into it and the IIHF does not. An IIHF major is five minutes with no substitute (Rule 20.1). **No automatic game misconduct attaches to it:** Rule 69 is on none of the IIHF's major-penalty reference tables — not Table 5, not Table 6, not Table 7, and not the single Table 5 the 2026/27 edition merges those three into — and Rule 20.4 attaches the automatic ejection only to a second major, a second misconduct in the same game, or a major for one of the infractions those tables list. ⚠️ **Do not take what follows as a reassurance.** The rule text and the appendix pull apart, and in Britain neither of them writes you a ceiling — so do not plan on two minutes. **Both books' appendix tables then answer this play more leniently than the NHL's rule text does, and more precisely than the IIHF's.** The NHL sets it out in Reference Table 14 under *"Crowding the Goalkeeper"* at D, and the IIHF at its 2025/26 Appendix IV, Table 16, Situation 6D — goalkeeper establishing position, attacker refuses to give ground, goal scored — and both answer *"Goal is disallowed. A minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)."* ⚠️ **And it is the NHL's book, not the British reader's, that contradicts itself outright here.** NHL 69.3 leaves the referee nothing — *"In all such cases, whether or not a goal is scored, the attacking player will receive a minor penalty for goalkeeper interference"* — against a table that answers *"A minor penalty is not assessed."* The IIHF's 69.3 writes *"the appropriate penalty"* in that same slot, and no penalty at all is one of the things that phrase can carry, so the IIHF book accommodates its own table where the NHL's cannot. Where the tables do assess something is the same contact with **no** goal scored, at B in each — and **read B's description column, not only its result**: both books make it conditional in identical words, *"A possible penalty depends on the Referee's judgment as to the degree of contact and degree of resistance with the attacking player and whether the goalkeeper was truly trying to establish a position."* The NHL's result column then answers *"Minor penalty"* and adds *"In this example it is a minor penalty"* — an illustration, not a floor — while the IIHF's says only that the referee *"must assess the appropriate penalty."* **The IIHF Situation Handbook does not settle it either.** It carries six worked situations under Rule 69 — where Rule 43 gets *"No situation."* — and two of them pair a disallowed goal with nothing for the attacker in terms: 69.1 (*"The goal shall be disallowed, and no additional penalty assessed"*) and 69.2, which puts this exact give-ground play and answers *"No goal – no penalty."* A third, 69.6 — the vacate-immediately case set out above — names no penalty either, though not in as many words. That corroborates the tables, and it still cannot break the tie: the handbook's own Foreword ranks it below the book — *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis"* — and that Table 16 **is** Appendix IV of that Rulebook, so the NHL's conflict is internal to its own rulebook and nothing outside it ranks one side above the other. A player "establishes a significant position within the crease" when his body, or a substantial part of it, is in the crease for **more than an instantaneous period**.

**Outside the crease** (Rule 69.4): **the rule asks two questions and answers them from different clauses, so keep them apart.** **The goal question** is its first paragraph, and there incidental contact is the whole of the test: *"If an attacking player initiates any contact with a goalkeeper, other than incidental contact, while the goalkeeper is outside his goal crease, and a goal is scored, the goal will be disallowed."* ⚠️ **The penalty question is the next paragraph, and there the permission is conditional — the condition is the half that gets dropped.** The restriction comes first: *"A goalkeeper is not 'fair game' just because he is outside the goal crease. The appropriate penalty should be assessed in every case where an attacking player makes unnecessary contact with the goalkeeper."* Then the permission, and its opening clause is the operative one: *"However, incidental contact will be permitted **when the goalkeeper is in the act of playing the puck outside his goal crease** provided the attacking player has made a reasonable effort to avoid such unnecessary contact."* **So the permission is not "he was outside the crease". It is "he was outside the crease *and playing the puck*, and you tried to avoid him."** A goaltender who has come out to challenge, or who is set up outside the paint with the puck somewhere else, is not inside that clause at all — and a forechecker who bumps him there has the books' own answer, that a penalty *"should be assessed in every case"* of unnecessary contact. **How the restriction and the permission interact where the contact was genuinely incidental but the goalkeeper was not playing the puck is stated in neither book, and is not resolved here.** 🇬🇧 **The IIHF writes the same condition at the same number**, in the 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 editions alike: *"'incidental contact' will be permitted when the goalkeeper is in the act of playing the puck outside their goal crease provided the attacking Player has made a 'reasonable effort' to avoid such unnecessary contact."*

⚠️ **The other two books reach the same play in their own words, and neither writes an "outside the crease" permission you can lean on.** **USA Hockey files it under charging.** **Rule 607(c)** assesses *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty"* to a player *"who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"* — an area **607(d) Note 2** defines by *"connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"*, which its Casebook at **Rule 607, Situation 6** confirms *"extends to the closer end boards"*, so it takes in the ice behind the net and not the corners. **Rule 607(d)** then protects the goalkeeper outside it: *"A goalkeeper is NOT 'fair game' because they are outside the privileged area. A penalty for interference or charging should be called in every case where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with a goalkeeper."* ⚠️ **And USA Hockey's Casebook keys this to the puck exactly as the NHL and the IIHF do, in words no search for their phrasing would ever find.** **Rule 607, Situation 5:** *"Although a goalkeeper can be legally checked when outside the privileged area, they are not considered to be 'fair game.'"* — then *"When the goalkeeper has possession of the puck, the attacking player is permitted to physically engage with the goalkeeper in an effort to gain possession of the puck. However, any check delivered with the intent to intimidate or punish the goalkeeper should be penalized as charging."*, and *"When the goalkeeper is no longer in control of the puck, any avoidable contact (meaning the attacking player has sufficient time to avoid the contact) must also be penalized under this rule."* **Hockey Canada writes no possession condition and no permission at all.** Rule 8.5 concedes only that *"While incidental contact with the goaltender may occur, attacking players must make an effort to avoid contact in all circumstances"*, hangs the penalty on that effort — *"The onus is always on the attacking player and players who do not make an effort to avoid the goaltender must be penalized"* — and gives the "not fair game" protection an explicit reach *"anywhere on the ice."* **What survives all four books is narrower than "outside the crease is fine": a goaltender who is not dealing with the puck is not to be contacted, and under Hockey Canada the duty to try to avoid him does not lift even when he is.**

**Standing in the way of a goalie getting back to his crease** (Rule 69.4 as well, its returning-goalkeeper paragraph). This is the half of Rule 69 that never gets quoted, and it is the one that catches a net-front forward who has been told only that an empty crease is a free crease. The NHL: *"When a goalkeeper has played the puck outside of his crease and is then prevented from returning to his crease area due to the deliberate actions of an attacking player, such player may be penalized for goalkeeper interference."* 🇬🇧 **The IIHF writes the same paragraph at the same number**, in its own house style — *"prevented from returning to their goal crease area due to the deliberate actions of an attacking Player, such Player may be penalized for 'Goalkeeper interference'"* — identical in the 2025/26 v1.0 and v1.1 books and the 2026/27 book, so a British reader has it too. **It runs both ways in each book:** the next sentence penalises the *goalkeeper* who, by his actions outside his crease, *"deliberately interferes with an attacking player who is attempting to play the puck or an opponent."*

⚠️ **Do not blend that with USA Hockey's answer to the same play, because the two thresholds point in opposite directions.** USA Hockey's lives in its Casebook, at **Rule 625, Situation 9**, and it is **mandatory and broad**: where the goalkeeper *"is attempting to return to the goal crease and there is any interference by the attacking player who prevents the goalkeeper from making a play, then the goal must be disallowed and an interference penalty shall be assessed."* Any interference, no goal, penalty assessed. The NHL's and the IIHF's is **permissive and narrow**: it needs *"deliberate actions"*, the player *"may be"* penalised, and the clause says nothing at all about the goal. **Hockey Canada writes no returning-goalkeeper clause** — Rule 8.5 and the whole book were searched, flattened, for one — but its *Protection of the Goaltender* paragraph reaches the contact version of the play from a different direction, since *"a penalty under this rule will be called where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with the goaltender **anywhere on the ice**."* **What survives all four books is the instruction, not the threshold: a goaltender on his way back to his crease is not an obstacle to stand in front of.**

**Rebounds and loose pucks** (NHL and IIHF Rule 69.7). ⚠️ **This is the rule that gets quoted for its permission and then stopped, and it runs to four limbs — two of which take the goal off the board.** The permission first, in the NHL's words: *"In a rebound situation, or where a goalkeeper and attacking player(s) are simultaneously attempting to play a loose puck, whether inside or outside the crease, incidental contact with the goalkeeper will be permitted, and any goal that is scored as a result thereof will be allowed."* **Incidental, and both of you going for the same loose puck.** It is not a licence to drive the goaltender backwards, and the next paragraph says so: *"In the event that a goalkeeper has been pushed into the net together with the puck by an attacking player after making a stop, the goal will be disallowed. If applicable, appropriate penalties will be assessed."* ⚠️ **That sentence needs all three of its conditions** — the goalkeeper into the net *together with the puck*, *after making a stop*, and pushed there *by an attacking player* — so it is not a rule that any contact which moves a goaltender kills a goal. ⚠️ **And read the sentence after it, because it runs your way:** *"If, however, in the opinion of the Referee, the attacking player was pushed or otherwise fouled by a defending player causing the goalkeeper to be pushed into the net together with the puck, the goal can be permitted."* **So a defenceman who shoves you into his own goalie can hand you the goal your own shove would have cost you.** A third paragraph closes the other shape of the same play: where the puck is under a player in or around the crease area, deliberately or otherwise, *"a goal cannot be scored by pushing this player together with the puck into the goal"* — with the appropriate penalties again *"[i]f applicable"*, and a penalty shot where the puck is deemed to have been covered in the crease deliberately. **The IIHF prints all four limbs at the same number and in the same order**, differing only in its capitalised *Player*, its *"goal crease"* where the NHL writes *"crease"*, quotation marks of its own around *"incidental contact"* and *"Penalty Shot"*, its opening *"In the event"* for the NHL's *"In the event that"*, and its setting of all four limbs off as separate paragraphs where the NHL prints three and keeps the relief inside the second — identically in the 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 editions. Both books also carry the push-in on their disallowed-goal lists, at **NHL 78.5(ix)** and **IIHF 78.5(IX)**: *"When a goalkeeper has been pushed into the net together with the puck after making a save."* ⚠️ **Those are cross-references and not the rule.** Both drop *"by an attacking player"* and write *"after making a save"* where 69.7 writes *"after making a stop"*, and both send you back to 69.7 — so 69.7's wording is the one to hold.

**Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes a rebound carve-out, and Hockey Canada runs the other way.** **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.5(a) is strictly narrower:** *"Any goal scored on a play where an attacking player initiates contact with the goaltender will be disallowed, regardless of whether the contact occurs inside or outside of the goal crease."* That clause binds **the attacking player**, not the goaltender, and its only stated exception is an attacker who is fouled by a defending player and as a result cannot avoid the contact. **USA Hockey's nearest equivalent is keyed to possession rather than to a rebound** — its *Casebook* at Rule 607, Situation 5, quoted above, lets an attacker engage a goalkeeper physically *"in an effort to gain possession of the puck"* only *"When the goalkeeper has possession of the puck"*, and withdraws that permission once they are *"no longer in control of the puck"*. **Both negatives are a search by concept as well as by phrase** — *rebound*, *loose puck*, *simultaneous* and *incidental contact*, across USA Hockey's playing rules, its separate *Rules and Casebook* volume, and Hockey Canada's playing rules with the interpretations printed alongside them. ⚠️ **And USA Hockey's Casebook answers a rebound in the crease the opposite way**, at **Rule 625, Situation 12**: an attacker carries the puck into the crease, the goalkeeper knocks it clear, a teammate gains it and shoots it straight back in with the first attacker still standing there, and *"the total elapsed time between when the puck is cleared from the crease and when it is shot back into the crease is at most one second"* — the goal is disallowed, because *"[a]t the time the shot was taken, an attacking player was illegally in the crease."* **That answers USA Hockey's position bar rather than a contact question**, so read it as showing there is no rebound exception to Rule 625(b), not as a ruling on contact.

**Being pushed into the goalie** (Rule 69.1): if a **defender** pushes you into the goalie, that is **not** contact initiated by you, provided you made a reasonable effort to avoid it. If **you** push a defender into his own goalie, that **is** treated as contact initiated by you.

> **⚠️ The Rule 69 framework above — 69.1's premise and 69.3's crease test — is the NHL's and the IIHF's. USA Hockey and Hockey Canada do not run 69.1's premise at all** — neither book contains the sentence about position not by itself determining the goal. **And they do not agree with each other.** USA Hockey replaces the impairment test with a **flat bar on position in the crease**, and it does not matter who scored. **Hockey Canada's bar is not flat:** where the player in the crease stays completely passive and a teammate outside it scores, the goal stands. So where your body is in the paint and a teammate scores from outside it, the two books give **opposite** answers. Hockey Canada also keeps an impairment test of its own, and it names vision: Interpretation 3 to Rule 8.5(a) has it that an attacker *"is NOT committing a foul by simply standing in the goal crease. However, if while standing in the crease, the attacking player attempts to play the puck, interfere with the play, or impede the goaltender's vision or movements, then no goal may be scored."* That makes it the counterpart of NHL **69.3**, which names vision too, and not of 69.1 — what Hockey Canada has no counterpart to is a no-contact screen ground of any kind — though, on the reading set out under [Screening is legal outside the crease](#screening-is-legal-outside-the-crease) below, the NHL's and the IIHF's is keyed to the crease as well, so out there the gap is narrower than the rule texts make it look. The two bars' preconditions differ as well: Hockey Canada asks only where the puck **is**, while USA Hockey asks who reached the crease **first** and adds a goalkeeper-out-of-the-crease-area exception Hockey Canada does not write — an exception its own Casebook narrows again, at Rule 625, Situation 9, for a goalkeeper who is skating back to the crease.
>
> - **USA Hockey Rule 625(b)** — in Section 6 under *Interference*, not in a rule of its own: *"No goal may be scored with an attacking player in the goal crease unless the puck has preceded the player(s) into the goal crease or the goalkeeper is out of the goal crease area."* No contact and no impairment required. ⚠️ **Do not take that second carve-out on its own — its own Casebook reverses it while the goalkeeper is skating back.** Rule 625, Situation 9 allows the goal where the goalkeeper is *"completely out of their crease"* and an attacker precedes the puck in, and then adds: *"However, if the goalkeeper is attempting to return to the goal crease and there is any interference by the attacking player who prevents the goalkeeper from making a play, then the goal must be disallowed and an interference penalty shall be assessed."* A goalkeeper on his way back is not an empty crease — get in his path and you lose the goal **and** go to the box for two minutes. The same clause costs you the zone before that: a neutral-zone face-off *"any time an attacking player stands, holds their stick, or skates through the goal crease provided the puck is in the attacking zone, the attacking team has possession of the puck and the goalkeeper is in contact with the crease."*
> - **Hockey Canada Rule 8.5**, filed under *Restraining Fouls*: *"Unless the puck is in the goal crease area, a player of the attacking team may not stand in the goal crease. If the puck should enter the net while such conditions prevail, the goal will NOT BE ALLOWED."* Hockey Canada writes the onus into the rule — *"The onus is always on the attacking player and players who do not make an effort to avoid the goaltender must be penalized"* — and defines the foul as impeding the goaltender *"by actual physical contact"*. Its position bar is not absolute: where an attacker is in the crease but does not interfere and **another** attacker outside it scores, *"the goal WILL BE ALLOWED provided that the player who was in the goal crease does not attempt to play the puck, interfere with the play or obstruct the Goaltender's view or movements."* **On *contact* outside the crease, though, Hockey Canada reaches further than the NHL's 69.4 does, not less far.** Its *Protection of the Goaltender* paragraph gives the same "not fair game" protection an explicit geographic reach the NHL's version does not write: *"A Goaltender is not 'fair game' just because they are outside their goal crease. A penalty under this rule will be called where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with the goaltender **anywhere on the ice**."*
>
> **The push-in carve-out is the sharpest divergence of the four, and it is not a tier ordering.** USA Hockey's relief is the **broadest** — *"if the attacking player has been physically interfered with by the actions of a defending player that causes them to be in the goal crease, play shall not be stopped and any legal goal scored shall be allowed"*, with **no** reasonable-effort proviso. But it reaches your **position** only: Rule 617(c)(4) disallows the goal for an attacker *"positioned in the crease"* **or** *"interfering with the goalkeeper"*, and the push-in parenthetical is written into the first limb alone, while **625(a)(8)** stays a minor for *"physical contact, using their stick or body, in a manner that interferes with the movement of the goalkeeper, unless otherwise specified in the rules."* **Hockey Canada writes the strictest version of it — but it is not the only book that reaches the pushed-in attacker, and the place to look is the reference table rather than the rule.** ⚠️ **The NHL and the IIHF each give this exact play a numbered section of their Rule 69 table, under a heading that names it.** The NHL's **Table 14**: *"A player pushes, shoves, or fouls another player into the goalkeeper, who is in or out of the crease."* The IIHF's **Table 16** in the 2025/26 book, renumbered **Table 14** in the 2026/27 edition and reprinted there word for word: *"A Player forces another Player into the goalkeeper, who is inside or outside the goal crease."* Both allow the goal where the pushed-in attacker *"made a reasonable effort to avoid contact"*, and both take it away and penalise him where he did not. The NHL's entries B and C under that heading give *"a minor and/or major penalty"*, C adding *"at the discretion of the Referee"*; the IIHF's Situations 3B and 3C both read *"The Referee in their judgment **must** assess the appropriate penalty to the attacking Player."* ⚠️ **So do not read 69.2's *"intentional or deliberate"* wording as your protection here** — both books decide this play on reasonable effort rather than on intent, and under the IIHF the penalty is mandatory. **USA Hockey is the one that says nothing about the push-in case either way** on the penalty side: its 625(a)(8) minor could reach him, while its relief in 625(b) speaks only to whether the goal counts. Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 is its relief, and it is narrow — the exception is an attacker *"fouled by a defending player and, as a result, is unable to avoid contact with the goaltender."* Interpretation 2 then says that where an attacker is *"tripped, hooked, cross-checked, or otherwise interfered with, falls, and makes contact with the goaltender ... If the player does not make an effort to avoid contact with the goaltender, then they must be penalized for interference with the goaltender"* — and *"The referee should also penalize the defending player who committed the initial foul under the appropriate rule."* So being fouled into the goalie is a defence in all four books and a **complete** one in none of them.
>
> **⚠️ And Hockey Canada's penalty ladder for this rule does not stop at a minor.** Everything above is about whether a goal counts; 8.5's lettered clauses are about what happens to the player, and they run further than the NHL's do. **8.5(a)** is the minor. **8.5(b)** reaches *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty"* three ways: at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of impact"*, and then **mandatorily** — *"will be assessed"* — in two named cases. *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who **charges the goaltender**"* (its Note 1 refers the definition of charging to Rule 7.4(b) Interpretation 2), and *"…to any player who **injures an opponent** by an Interference with the Goaltender infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty."* **8.5(c)** is a **match penalty** *"to any player who attempts to injure or deliberately injures a goaltender by Interference."* **8.5(e)** makes the game misconduct automatic on any 8.5(b) major, and **8.5(d)** removes the ten-minute rung entirely: *"No Misconduct penalty may be assessed for interference with the goaltender."* So under Hockey Canada the tiers are two minutes, ejection, or match — nothing in between — and a foul that would have been two minutes becomes an ejection the moment it injures. This is the same shape as Hockey Canada's 9.2(b) for cross-checking and 7.3(b) for body checking: discretionary on violence, mandatory on injury. ⚠️ **The ejection tier is not Hockey Canada's alone, though — what is Hockey Canada's alone is that it is mandatory.** All four books reach a major and a game misconduct on this class of play, by four different routes. **The NHL's own Rule 69 does not stop at a minor:** **69.2** gives *"a penalty (minor or major, as the Referee deems appropriate)"* for intentional or deliberate contact, and **69.8** reaches *"an appropriate penalty (minor or major and/or game misconduct)"* plus supplementary discipline under Rule 28. **Charging a goalkeeper is a separate route again, and the NHL names all three tiers in the rule:** **Rule 42.1** — *"A minor, major or a major and a game misconduct shall be imposed on a player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within his goal crease."* 🇬🇧 **The IIHF's 42.1 writes only *"A penalty shall be imposed"* on the same facts**, leaving the tier to 42.2 to 42.4 — and its **42.4** needs **both** limbs, a Player who *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'charging'"* **and** who *"would not be sufficiently sanctioned by imposing a major penalty"*. **USA Hockey writes no bare minor for it at any limb of Rule 607:** **607(c)** is *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty… to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"*, quoted in full above. ⚠️ **What is narrower, and is the part not to carry across, is Hockey Canada's *injury* limb** — a foul that would have been a minor becoming a mandatory ejection the moment it injures. **NHL Rule 69 carries no injury trigger of any kind**, read through from 69.1 to 69.8; **USA Hockey Rule 625's only penalty limbs are 625(a) and 625(c), both minors**, so its ejection tier comes from Rule 607 and a charge, not from an injury; and **IIHF 69.8 is a pointer to supplementary discipline and nothing more**, where the NHL's names a game misconduct. **The IIHF's Rule 69 was read for its tiers only** — no comparison of its 69.1 to 69.7 text against the NHL's is made here beyond the passages quoted earlier in this section.
>
> **The practical instruction is the same under all four and the reason differs:** get out of the paint. Find out which book your league runs.

### Screening is legal outside the crease

**Standing in the goaltender's line of sight, without contact and without being in the crease, is normally legal.** ⚠️ **The paragraph of Rule 69.3 that reaches a screen asks for three things at once, and the test is conjunctive.** It disallows the goal, **with no contact at all**, where an attacker *"establishes a significant position within the goal crease, so as to obstruct the goalkeeper's vision and impair his ability to defend his goal"* — that is the NHL's wording; the IIHF prints *"impair their ability to defend their goal"* and puts quotation marks of its own around *"significant position"*. **Every part of that is load-bearing:** the position inside the paint, the obstructed vision **and** the impaired ability to defend, not any one of them on its own. Both books then define the position — the player's body, *"or a substantial portion thereof, is within the goal crease for more than an instantaneous period of time"* — the NHL printing *"his body"* and the IIHF *"their body"*. ⚠️ **That is the fourth paragraph of 69.3, and it is not the whole rule:** the first paragraph, set out above, costs you the goal for **any** contact with a goalkeeper in his crease, incidental or otherwise. So what makes a screen illegal is being in the crease **and** what your position does to the goalie once you are there — screening itself is not the offence.

**But read clause (1) of Rule 69.1 whole before leaning on it in either direction.** It names an attacking player who *"either by his positioning or by contact, impairs the goalkeeper's ability to move freely within his crease or defend his goal"* — 🇬🇧 **and your book writes that clause in its own words**: the IIHF's 69.1 reads *"either by their positioning or by a 'relevant contact', impairs the goalkeeper's ability to move freely within their goal crease or defend their goal"*, and defines that term in the same rule as *"any contact that is made between or among a goalkeeper and attacking Player(s), whether by means of a stick or any part of the body."* The positioning limb is the same in both books. ⚠️ **What that clause is not is a direction to disallow the goal.** It is introduced by *"Goals should be disallowed **only if**"*, which states a **necessary** condition — a ceiling on when a goal may be taken off the board — and a necessary condition being met is not the same as the rule's own test for voiding a goal being met. **The sentence in 69.1 that actually disallows one is keyed to the crease, in both books:** *"If an attacking player **enters the goal crease** and, by his actions, impairs the goalkeeper's ability to defend his goal, and a goal is scored, the goal will be disallowed."* **69.3's no-contact screen ground is keyed to the crease too** — a *"significant position within the goal crease"* — and **69.4, the rule for outside the crease, has no positioning limb at all**, only contact. ⚠️ **That is a reading of the rule's structure rather than something either book states in terms**, and both provide that Rule 69 *"will be enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s)"*. **So the honest statement is: a clean screen from outside the paint is legal, referees allow it constantly, and no clause of Rule 69 read here takes the goal away for where you stood unless you were in the crease — but enforcement is expressly the referee's judgement, so this is not something you will get to argue from the ice.**

**⚠️ And here the two North American amateur books are *more* permissive than the NHL, not less** — the opposite of the direction they run inside the crease. **USA Hockey's 2025-29 playing rules contain no provision about screening a goaltender or obstructing a goaltender's vision at all**; its only goaltender-interference grounds are position in the crease and physical contact. **Hockey Canada** speaks of the goaltender's view in exactly two places, Rule 8.5's crease paragraph and Interpretation 3, and **both reach only a player who is in the crease** — Interpretation 3 saying an attacker is *"NOT committing a foul by simply standing in the goal crease"*, and its Note 1 that *"No penalty would be assessed unless the attacking player's body or stick makes actual physical contact with the goaltender."* So the no-contact screen ground the NHL and the IIHF write at 69.3 **has no counterpart in either book**. Under those two the screen from outside the paint is simply legal, and the crease line does all the work — which, on the reading above, is what it does under the NHL and the IIHF as well. *(Both negatives are a search of the two **playing rule** books, and of USA Hockey's separate Rules and Casebook, which carries no screening or goaltender-vision provision either: its only two uses of "screen" are Rule 625(a)(1)'s protective screen set on a checker and a sled-hockey pushers' rule, and none of the seventeen Casebook situations under Rule 625 reaches a screen. Hockey Canada prints its interpretations inside its playing rules, so both of its mentions were searched with them.)*

**⚠️ The NHL and the IIHF then work the outside-crease screen through in their own situations, and the answer lines up with the rule's crease keying rather than against it.** The NHL heads a block of Reference Table 14 *"Screening Situations"*; the IIHF reproduces the same five situations at its 2025/26 Appendix IV, Table 16, Situation 5, under a heading reading *"Contact with the goalkeeper"* instead. **Situation 5C** — an attacker in front of the goalkeeper, **outside** the crease, in motion, who *"impairs the goalkeeper's ability to defend their goal"* — is answered *"Goal is allowed."* So is **Situation 5E**, where the attacker *"plants themself on the crease line or outside the goal crease, as to obstruct the goalkeeper's vision and impair their ability to defend their goal, and a goal is scored."* The only two of the five the books disallow, 5B and 5D, both put the attacker **inside** the crease. **These are appendix interpretations rather than operative rule text** — but they are printed in both rulebooks, Rule 69 sends the reader to them (the NHL at 69.3 and 69.4, the IIHF at 69.1, 69.3 and 69.4), and they **apply** the crease keying the rule already carries rather than repealing anything: every clause of Rule 69 that voids a goal for a player's position names the goal crease, and the rule for outside it reaches contact only. So the four-book gap described just above is narrower than the rule texts make it look. ⚠️ **Take them as strong support for the instruction below and not as a guarantee — and not as covering the crease line itself**, which Situation 5E treats as outside the crease while the IIHF's Rule 1.7 has it that *"[t]he marked line belongs to the Goal Crease"*. [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md) sets that collision out; this document does not resolve it, and the instruction is to keep clear of the line as well as of the paint.

This is the single most valuable rules fact for a forward. **Get in the goalie's eyes; keep your skates out of the blue paint.** See [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md) and [Winger](../positions/winger.md).

### The trapezoid (goalkeeper's restricted area)

This section is the **penalty**. The geometry — the NHL figures, the depth, the IIHF metric equivalents and where the lines actually run — belongs to [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md), which covers the rink's dimensions and gives them in full.

**The goalie may not play the puck behind the goal line outside the marked trapezoid behind his net.** Violation is a **two-minute minor for delay of game** (**NHL Rules 27.8 and 63.2(viii)**; the IIHF numbers the same rule **27.7**). The determining factor is the **position of the puck**, not the position of the goalie — and both books say so in those words.

**There is exactly one exception, and it is not the one people think:** the minor is **not** assessed when the goalie plays the puck **while maintaining skate contact with his goal crease**. There is no teammate-based exception and no "he was only touching it" exception.

Related, and often forgotten: a goalie who **participates in the play beyond the centre red line** takes a **minor**. All four books carry this rule, under four different numbers, and **all four use the same operative words: *"participates in the play in any manner"*** — which is broader than playing the puck. **NHL Rule 27.7** and **IIHF Rule 27.6** are the same sentence, and set *"(intentionally plays the puck or checks an opponent)"* inside that phrase as a parenthetical gloss rather than as a limit on it. **USA Hockey Rule 407(c)** assesses the minor where a goalkeeper *"deliberately participates in the play in any manner when they are beyond the center red line."* **Hockey Canada Rule 4.13(c)** reaches a goaltender who *"intentionally participates in the play in any manner when they are beyond the centre red line"* — and carries no penalty of its own, sending the offence instead to **Rule 8.3(a) – Interference**, which is a minor.

> **⚠️ The centre-red-line test is the opposite way round in the two North American amateur books, and this is the half almost nobody knows.** This is about the red line and not about the trapezoid. Under the **NHL and the IIHF** the red-line rule says in terms that *"the position of the puck is the determining factor for the application of this rule"* — NHL 27.7 and IIHF 27.6, word for word. Under **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada it is the position of the goalkeeper's skates.** USA Hockey's *Casebook*, **Rule 407 Situation 13**, asks whether it is legal for a goalkeeper *"whose skates are beyond the center red line to play the puck which is still on the same side of center red line as the goal they are defending"*, answers **no**, and gives the reason: *"In this situation the determining factor is the position of the goalkeeper's skates and not the puck."* Hockey Canada's **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.13(c)** puts the same facts and rules the same way — *"The determining factor is the position of the goaltender's skates and not the position of the puck. Assess the goaltender a Minor penalty for Rule 8.3 (a) – Interference."* **And Hockey Canada does not leave the skates test to an interpretation**: Rule 8.3(a) itself assesses a minor to any goaltender who *"plays the puck with their skates over the centre red line"*, in operative rule text — worded narrower than 4.13(c)'s *"in any manner"*, though both routes end at the same minor.
>
> **So a goalie who steps over the line to reach back for a puck still on his own side is unpenalised under the NHL's and the IIHF's rule as written, and takes two minutes under both North American amateur books.** How much North American rec and youth hockey runs on USA Hockey's book or Hockey Canada's is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it. **Find out which book you are under before your goalie starts handling pucks anywhere near the red line** — and if you do not know, keep the skates behind it, which is legal in all four.
>
> **On their own words all four require something deliberate — but the NHL and the IIHF carry the intent word only inside their parenthetical.** Their operative phrase is *"participates in the play in any manner"*, with *"intentionally plays the puck or checks an opponent"* bracketed after it, so reading those two books as requiring intent depends on treating that parenthetical as defining the phrase. USA Hockey's rule needs the participation to be *"deliberate"* and Hockey Canada's *"intentional"*, both in the operative sentence itself. **Only USA Hockey publishes the worked case**: *Casebook* **Rule 407 Situation 12** has a goalkeeper on the way to the bench, over the red line, *"accidentally struck by the puck"* — no penalty, because *"the goalkeeper is not considered to be participating in the play if the puck contact was accidental."* The other three books have not been searched here for a situation of their own on accidental contact, so read that one as USA Hockey's answer and the other three as a reading of their rule texts.

> **⚠️ Cite 27.8 for the penalty and never for the dimensions.** Rule 27.8 still describes the area as beginning *"six feet (6') from either goal post"* — the pre-2014 wording, from before the trapezoid was widened by two feet a side for 2014-15. It is **stale, not self-contradictory**: six feet outside each post gives 18 feet at the goal line, diverging perfectly coherently to the 28 feet at the boards that both rules quote. It is the old shape, accurately described. **Rule 1.8 is the geometry**, and every figure here follows it.

**Who has the trapezoid:** the **NHL** (Rules 1.8 and 27.8) and **IIHF play** (Rules 1.8 and 27.7) — both read directly in the rulebooks. **The KHL is commonly said to have one as well, though that one is unverified here: no KHL rulebook was available to check it against.** The IIHF's own 2021 announcement of its unified rulebook dates the IIHF's adoption to the **2021-22 season**; **the NHL's commonly given 2005-06 date is in no rulebook held here.** **USA Hockey has no trapezoid** — no restriction on where a goalkeeper may play the puck *behind the net*; the words *"trapezoid"*, *"designated area"* and *"restricted area"* are in neither the 2025-29 playing rules nor the *Casebook*. **Hold on to that scope, because it is not a general licence.** USA Hockey restricts where a goalkeeper may **play** the puck in one place — **Rule 407(c)**, which makes it a minor to participate in the play beyond the centre red line — and where they may **freeze** it in another, **Rule 614(c)**, which confines that to the goalkeeper's privileged area. **Hockey Canada has none either** — no goalkeeper's restricted area anywhere in its rink section, and the single *"designated area"* in the book is the one Rule 5.4(b) stations a Goal Judge in. **But it bars *freezing* more broadly than USA Hockey does:** Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), clause v, provides that once a goaltender leaves the crease *"they will not be allowed to freeze the puck. If they do, they will be assessed a Minor penalty. No warning will be issued."* **CARHA has none either** — *"trapezoid"*, *"restricted area"* and *"designated area"* appear nowhere in its 2020 book. **But it bars *freezing* on the same shape USA Hockey does**, at Rule 58(b): a minor to a goalkeeper who deliberately falls on or gathers the puck into their body, or holds it against the goal or the boards, *"when the puck is behind the goal line and the goalkeeper's body is entirely outside the boundaries of the goal crease area."* **So all three of the no-trapezoid books here still limit where a goalkeeper may freeze the puck, even though none of them limits where they may play it behind the net** — do not read the missing trapezoid as a licence to smother pucks back there in any of them. **How much rec, beer-league and youth hockey that adds up to is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it.**

> **🇬🇧 In England and Wales the trapezoid's status is unsettled, and this is the one place on this page where "the IIHF column is yours" is not the whole answer.** The IIHF book carries the rule at **27.7**, so on the face of it a British goaltender is bound by it. But England Ice Hockey's *Rules & Regulations* **22.3** say the *"Goalkeepers' Restricted Area is not currently enforced to allow ice rinks the necessary time to make the changes to implement this rule."* That is the **2024-25** edition, and whether the suspension still stands is not settled by anything published here.
>
> **The suspension does not reach all of Britain, because the body issuing it does not.** England Ice Hockey's own Rule 1.1 makes it the National Governing Body *"in England and Wales"*. The playing instrument everywhere else is the **In-House Rules**, which IHUK and the British Universities Ice Hockey Association adopt *"to be applied in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"* — and **neither the 2025-26 nor the 2026-27 edition mentions the restricted area at all**, both having been searched for it, and neither amends Rule 27. So in **Scotland, Northern Ireland and BUIHA hockey, IIHF 27.7 reads as live.** The Elite League runs the IIHF book with its own casebook, which has not been obtained here, so none of this speaks to an EIHL game.
>
> **Play it the safe way round in both directions: if it is your goalie, assume the rule binds — keep him out of the corners. If it is theirs, assume he may come for the puck anyway, and do not build a dump-in on the trapezoid holding.** Ask your league, and look behind the net to see whether the lines are painted — a hint rather than a ruling, since 27.7 turns on where the puck is and not on the markings. [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md) carries the full amendment list.

**⚠️ But USA Hockey is not restriction-free behind the net — it restricts where the goalie may *freeze* the puck, and the penalty is the same two minutes.** **Rule 614(c)** assesses a minor for delay of game to a goalkeeper who falls on or gathers the puck and causes a stoppage when *"the puck is behind the goal line and their body is entirely outside of the goal crease"*, or when *"the puck is outside the boundaries of the 'goalkeeper's privileged' area."* The rule's own Note defines that area as *"an area outlined by connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"* — a rectangle from the faceoff-dot line back to the end boards. It is far more generous than a trapezoid and it does include the ice directly behind the net, but **it excludes the corners**. The same area also protects the goalie: Rule 607(c) penalises checking or charging a goalkeeper *"while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area."*

**Practical consequence:** if you play without the trapezoid, your goalie is a third defenceman on every dump-in and your breakouts can be built around him retrieving pucks in the corners — **but under USA Hockey he must *play* those corner pucks, not smother them.** If you play with it, your defencemen must retrieve everything wide, and the goalie's job is to stop the puck behind the net and leave it. Confirm which before your first game — it changes your breakout. See [Breakouts](../systems/breakouts.md).

### Freezing the puck

The goalie is allowed to cause a stoppage, but only in defined circumstances. Holding the puck with the hands for longer than three seconds unless actually being checked, throwing it forward toward the opponents' net, dropping it into his pads or onto the goal net, racing out of the crease to beat an attacker to a loose puck and then jumping on it, and piling snow or obstacles near the net are each a **minor** under the **NHL and IIHF** books, which write this rule in the same terms (Rules 63.2, 67.3) — and the last becomes an **awarded goal**, but only once you have been replaced for an extra attacker, *"otherwise a minor penalty shall be assessed"* (Rule 67.5 in both). Coming out to cut down the angle, making the save and *then* covering the puck is legal.

> **⚠️ One of these reads far stricter than it is enforced.** Rule 63.2(vii) penalises a goalkeeper who, inside his own crease, deliberately falls on or gathers the puck into his body *"unless he is actually being checked by an opponent."* Read literally, that would make the routine freezes you see in every game illegal, and it is not how any game is officiated. **[Goaltender](../positions/goaltender.md) covers this one** and sets out how the written rule and the "unnecessary stoppage" standard of Rule 67.3(ii) interact in practice, together with the published **Hockey Canada** (Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a)) and **USA Hockey** (Rule 610(b)) interpretations, both of which apply a pressure test the NHL does not publish. Do not act on the bare literal reading — and if your league's referees are strict about it, find out early.

There is one further consequence that is not a penalty at all but hurts more — and **it belongs to the NHL and the IIHF alone**: if the **attacking team shoots the puck in from beyond the centre red line and the goalie freezes it**, the faceoff is in the **goalie's own end**, the **defending team may not change lines**, and they may **not take a timeout** (Rules 63.8 and 87.1, which both books write in the same terms). The attacking team also **chooses which dot**. **The change bar is not absolute**, and the same paragraph of 63.8 says so: it permits a substitution *"to replace an injured player, or when a penalty has been assessed which affects the on-ice strength of either team"*. Those two are the whole list here — the pulled-goalkeeper limb that appears elsewhere in 63.8 and in Rule 81.4 is absent, because the team being restricted is the goaltender's own. **Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes this consequence at all.** In both of them a goalkeeper's freeze is a penalty question rather than a substitution one, and neither book ties a no-change or a no-timeout to a frozen shoot-in. Hockey Canada goes the other way on the timeout even after an icing: Rule 6.7(d)(iii) provides that *"Either team is permitted to take their time-out… following an icing"*, barring only substitutions during it. So under the NHL and the IIHF, a soft freeze on a routine dump-in is punished exactly like an icing. ⚠️ **Under the other two it costs you no change and no timeout — but do not read that as a free freeze**, because both of them make an unpressured one a penalty in its own right: USA Hockey Rule 610(b) assesses a minor to a goalkeeper *"who has an opportunity to play the puck prior to being pressured by an attacking player, but instead intentionally causes a stoppage of play"*, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 10.1(a), clause iii, assesses one *"after one warning"* to a goaltender who *"freezes the puck in the crease when not under pressure from attacking players"* — the pressure test flagged in the warning above.

### The goalie playing the puck

Beyond the trapezoid and the red line, the goalie is a skater with the puck. He can pass, he can rim, he can set up a breakout. The rules that constrain him are:

- **Hand passes** by the goalie in the defending zone are legal, like anyone else's (Rule 79.2).
- The goalie **cannot participate in a faceoff** (Rule 76.1).
- A **minor penalty to a goalie is served by a teammate** who was on the ice at the stoppage, designated by the coach through the captain, and **that substitute may not be changed** (Rule 27.1). A **penalised player may not serve a goalie's penalty.**
- A goalie may **not go to his bench during a stoppage** for a stick, equipment or instructions **without the referee's permission** — otherwise he must be replaced immediately or his team takes a bench minor (**NHL Rule 27.6**; **IIHF Rule 27.5**, which carries this limb and the next one under a single number).
- A **minor for leaving the immediate vicinity of the crease during an altercation** (**NHL Rule 27.6**; **IIHF Rule 27.5**). This is why goalies stay in their net during scrums.

> **⚠️ Two of the 27.x numbers in this section belong to different rules in different books, and following the wrong one lands you on the wrong offence.** The **NHL** numbers the crease-and-bench rule **27.6**, the centre red line **27.7** and the restricted area **27.8**. The **IIHF shifts all three down one** — **27.5**, **27.6** and **27.7**. So the "27.7" cited above for the centre red line is the **NHL's**; in the IIHF book, which is the base rule set everywhere in Britain, 27.7 is the trapezoid. [Goaltender](../positions/goaltender.md) sets out the full numbering, including where the IIHF's own Situation Handbook disagrees with its own Rule Book.
>
> **⚠️ And the crease-during-an-altercation minor travels with two carve-outs that are easy to miss, both written into the rule's own text.** Where the altercation is *"in or near the goalkeeper's crease"*, the referee *"should direct the goalkeeper to a neutral location and not assess a penalty for leaving the immediate vicinity of the goal crease."* And where the goalkeeper is legitimately outside the crease *"for the purpose of proceeding to the players' bench to be substituted for an extra attacker"* and is then drawn into an altercation, *"the minor penalty for leaving the crease would not be assessed."* Those are the NHL's words at 27.6; **IIHF Rule 27.5 carries both sentences too**, differing only in capitalising *"Players' bench"* and in writing *"they subsequently become"* where the NHL writes *"he subsequently becomes"*. **Both carve-outs are permissive, so the bare rule reads stricter than it is.**
>
> **⚠️ All four books print this offence in their playing rules, and the crease-adjacent altercation is the one where they part — on whether you need the referee's permission first.** Under the **NHL and the IIHF** the referee *"should direct the goalkeeper to a neutral location and not assess a penalty"*, and nothing is asked of the goalie. **USA Hockey builds the same offence around the referee's instruction rather than around the crease line.** **Rule 615(e)** assesses a minor to any player who *"fails to move immediately and directly to their players' bench (or goal crease for a goalkeeper) when instructed to do so by the Referee during an altercation"*, and the book's own Summary of Penalties files that clause under Goalkeeper Infractions as *"Leaving crease area during altercation."* Its *Casebook* then applies the rule to this case the same generous way the NHL and the IIHF do: **Rule 615, Situation 26** has an altercation in the vicinity of the crease and the goalkeeper leaving *"to remain out of the altercation"*, and rules **no penalty**, with the referee directing them *"to go to their players' bench or at least away from the altercation"* — though *"if the Referee does not give their permission and the goalkeeper gets involved in the altercation outside of the crease, the minor penalty must be assessed."* **Situation 27** asks flatly whether a goalie may go to their own bench when a fight breaks out near the crease, and answers **"Yes."** Both give their authority as *"Rule Reference 615(e)"* — they are applying a playing rule, not supplying one. **Hockey Canada carries it in the rule text as well.** **Rule 7.10(a)**: *"A Minor penalty will be assessed to a goaltender who leaves their crease during a fight. This should be recorded on the Official Game Report as 'Leaving the Crease'."* Its **Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.10(a)** settles only the crease-adjacent case, and answers: *"With the permission of the Referee, the goaltender may proceed to their bench. If they do so on their own accord, they are to be assessed a Minor penalty for leaving their crease during a fight."* Hockey Canada has a neutral-location route too — **Rule 7.10(d)** requires that *"The goaltenders must stay in their goal creases or proceed to a neutral zone designated by the Referee."*
>
> **On the narrow question of moving before the referee has told you to, Hockey Canada is the strictest of the four.** Its Interpretation 1 puts a minor on a goaltender who goes to the bench *"on their own accord"* and asks nothing further, where USA Hockey's Situation 26 needs the goalkeeper to have got *"involved in the altercation outside of the crease"* as well, and the NHL's and the IIHF's books answer the crease-adjacent case by instructing the referee rather than by asking anything of the goalie. **So the safe instruction is Hockey Canada's, because it satisfies all four: stay in your net, and if you need to move, get the referee's permission first.** **And nothing here licenses joining in — in either book, and the ejection is in the playing rules rather than in a casebook.** **USA Hockey Rule 615(d)**: *"A game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player **or goalkeeper** who is the first to intervene in an altercation then in progress. This penalty is in addition to any other penalty incurred in the same incident."* Note how wide that is — it names goalkeepers expressly and attaches **no crease condition**, so it reaches a goalie who intervenes from anywhere on the ice. The *Casebook* then works the goalie's case at **Rule 615, Situation 10** — a goalie who leaves the vicinity of the crease and is first in takes *"a minor penalty for leaving the crease, plus a game misconduct for being the first to intervene"* — and gives its authority as Rule References 615(a), 615(d) and 615(e), so it is applying 615(d) rather than supplying it. **Hockey Canada ejects the same act at Rule 7.10(e)(ii)**, a game misconduct to a player who *"joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker"* — and read that second limb, because going in to **break it up** is caught as squarely as going in to fight. **And it reaches a goaltender, even though it says only "a player."** Hockey Canada's **Glossary** settles it: *"PLAYERS: The members of the team physically participating in the game. **The goaltender is considered a player, except where explicitly stated** because special rules or privileges apply to them."* Rule 7.10(e) states no such exception, so a goalie who goes in — to fight or to separate them — is ejected under it. *(Rules 7.10(a), 7.10(d) and 7.10(f) do name goaltenders in their own text; that is the Glossary's "except where explicitly stated" limb at work, not a sign that a bare "player" excludes them.)* **The second carve-out — the goalie already on the way to the bench for an extra attacker — was found only in the NHL and IIHF texts**; no USA Hockey or Hockey Canada counterpart was located here, which is a limit of this search rather than a finding that none exists.

---

## 8. Faceoffs

**[Faceoffs](../systems/faceoffs.md) covers this material** and carries it in full — the procedure, the stick-down order, the restraining lines and the fifteen-foot rule, every violation, the ejection rules, the post-icing free violation, and the technique. What this section keeps is the **location table**, which is a rules reference you will want in one place, and the two things you must not leave this document without.

### The nine spots

**Every faceoff in the game must be taken at one of nine marked spots** — one at centre ice, four in the neutral zone, four in the end zones — and **no faceoff may be taken within 15 feet of the goal or the side boards**, or anywhere other than a spot (Rule 76.2). What is painted at each of them is Rule 1.9, and belongs to [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md).

### Why the draw is where it is

Faceoff location is a **penalty and reward system**, and reading it tells you who just did what. The principle is that the draw goes **into the zone of whoever caused the stoppage**, and the wronged team is often given the choice of which of the two dots. **Three of those choices are worth knowing because you can exercise them.** After a penalty, the draw is in the offending team's end and **the team going on the power play picks which dot** (**Rule 76.2**). After an icing, it is in the offending team's end and **the attacking team picks** (**Rule 81.2**). The same choice goes to the attacking team when a goalie freezes a shoot-in from beyond the red line, or when a defender knocks his own net off. **It is a real choice and somebody on your team should be making it deliberately** — [Faceoffs](../systems/faceoffs.md) covers how to use it. The full location list:

| Situation | Where the draw goes |
|---|---|
| Start of a period, or after a goal | Centre ice |
| **Penalty assessed** | One of the two end-zone spots in the **offending team's zone** — and **the team awarded the power play chooses which dot** (Rule 76.2) |
| Penalty after a goal, or at the end/start of a period | Centre ice |
| **Icing** | End zone of the offending team; the **attacking team chooses the dot** (Rule 81.2) |
| **Offside — carried in** | Neutral-zone spot nearest that attacking zone |
| **Offside — passed or shot in** | The **faceoff spot in the zone closest to** where the pass or shot came from — a spot, not the patch of ice it left |
| **Intentional offside** | End-zone spot in the **offending team's** defending zone |
| Goalie freezes a shoot-in from beyond the red line | Goalie's own end zone; attacking team chooses the dot |
| Defender accidentally dislodges the net | Defender's own end zone; attacking team chooses the dot |
| Puck out of bounds | Nearest spot in the zone **from where it was shot or deflected out** |
| Hand pass in the attacking zone | Neutral zone, outside the defending team's blue line |
| High-sticking the puck | The spot giving the offending team **least territorial advantage** |
| Goal disallowed for goalie interference | Nearest **neutral-zone** spot outside the offending team's attacking zone |
| Officials erred on an icing or offside | Centre ice (icing) or nearest neutral-zone spot (offside, USA Hockey) |
| Two violations at once | The spot giving the offending team **least territorial advantage** |

One further location rule changes behaviour after the whistle, and it can cost you the zone you have just earned: **if a point man or a player off the bench from the attacking team enters the zone past the outer edge of the end-zone faceoff circle during a scrum, the draw is moved out to the neutral zone** (Rule 76.2). If your team has just earned a defensive-zone draw in the other end, **do not go join the scrum** — you will give the zone back.

### The two things you must not leave without

**1. A winger encroaching gets the *centre* ejected from the draw.** Two rules do the work together, which is why both get cited: **Rule 76.6** supplies the consequence — a player other than the one taking the draw moving into the faceoff circle before the drop means *"the offending team's player taking the face-off shall be ejected from the face-off circle"* — and **Rule 76.7** enumerates the violations and adds the escalation: a **second violation by the same team on the same draw is a bench minor**. Both skates stay outside the circle and outside the hash marks until the puck is down; **skate contact with the line is permitted, crossing it is not**, and your stick may be inside provided it touches neither an opponent nor his stick.
