Your best faceoff man being removed because a winger leaned in early is the most consequential rules mistake a winger makes, and it is entirely avoidable.

**2. ⚠️ The IIHF used to answer that the other way, and as of its 2026/27 edition it does not — but one IIHF divergence survives, and it deletes a technique.** The **IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26** ejected nobody: a teammate moving into the circle meant *"the offending team taking the 'face-off' shall receive a warning for a 'face-off' violation"* (IIHF 2025/26 Rule 76.6), and the same centre took the re-drop. The **2026/27 edition, published June 2026**, replaced that sentence with the NHL's — *"the offending team's player taking the face-off shall be ejected from the face-off circle"* (IIHF 2026/27 Rule 76.6) — and at 76.3 and 76.4 it now has the offending centre **replaced** where the older book had the linesperson assess a warning. **So paragraph 1 above is the answer under both books now, and a British reader reads it as written rather than as somebody else's rule.** ⚠️ **And in Britain that no longer rests on working out which IIHF edition your league is administering, because IHUK has written the removal into its own rules.** The **IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27**, published 25 July 2026, add a *Section 10 – Game Flow* amendment to Rule 76 that the 2025-26 edition did not contain at all: *"With the exception of face-offs following icings, the linesperson shall immediately replace the centre for all violations."* Those rules are the *"overriding interpretation of how the IIHF Rule Book is to be applied domestically"*, and they apply *"in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland"* — **the Elite League is not named in them, so they do not answer for an EIHL game.** ⚠️ **The same amendment changes one thing in the *other* direction, and it is the stick order**: *"Either centre can now put their stick down first for a face-off."* Both IIHF editions give that order to the defending centre at eight of the nine spots; in Britain neither centre has to go first, and the linesperson is told to require *"the whole of their stick blades (not toe or heel) in the white portion of the face-off circle"*, which is more than IIHF Rule 76.7's *"at least the toe"*. What has **not** changed is a violation the NHL does not have at all: **"one of the centers wins the face-off by using the skates"** (IIHF Rule 76.3(VI), both editions). Kicking the puck back with your feet is **legal in the NHL and a violation internationally** — under the 2025/26 book it burned your team's one warning, and under the 2026/27 book it gets your centre replaced before the puck is dropped. [Faceoffs](../systems/faceoffs.md) sets all of it out in full, along with the USA Hockey differences.

Two category distinctions that matter more than they look, both fully treated in [Faceoffs](../systems/faceoffs.md). **First: closing your hand on the puck is never a faceoff violation — it is a penalty in three of the four books, and worse than a penalty near your own net.** Under the **NHL and IIHF** it is a **minor** (Rule 67.2), and the note to 67.2(ii) escalates it: *"When this is done in his team's goal crease area, a penalty shot shall be assessed (67.4) or a goal awarded (67.5)."* **Under USA Hockey Rule 618(a) it is not a penalty at all outside the crease** — play stops for a last-play faceoff — **but inside the crease that same 618(a) makes it a penalty shot or optional minor**, and an awarded goal on an empty net — ⚠️ **though that book alone conditions the awarded goal**, on the act having *"prevented an obvious and imminent goal"* (614(b)). **Hockey Canada 10.2(a)(iii)** makes the closed hand a minor only where it *"gains an advantage"*, and **10.2(a)(v)** a penalty shot where the infraction occurs inside the offending team's crease. Batting the puck with the hand to win a draw is a different offence again: a **minor for delay of game — faceoff violation** (Rule 67.2). And while the draw is live the two takers may not play the puck with a hand at all, however briefly, until a third player has touched it (Rule 76.5) — **NHL and IIHF only; no counterpart was found in USA Hockey or Hockey Canada.** **Second: no timeout may be granted following a faceoff violation** (Rule 87.1), so there is no way to buy yourself out of one. (On high sticks at the dot, see the high-sticking bullet in section 4: accidental contact on a centre bent over at a faceoff is explicitly not a penalty under Rule 60.1 — **under the NHL and IIHF books; USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such exception**, though a wild swing at a bouncing puck still is.)

---

## 9. Officials and Signals

### Who is on the ice

The NHL uses the **four-official system**: **two referees** (orange armbands) and **two linespersons**.

**Referees** (Rule 31) have general supervision of the game and full control of all officials and players; their decision is final. They **impose penalties**, give the final on-ice decision on **disputed goals**, and one of them **drops the puck to start each period**. They may consult the linespersons before deciding.

Critically: **referees do not call offside or icing.** Rule 31.4 says so explicitly — those are the linespersons' duties, unless a linesperson is somehow unable to act.

**Linespersons** (Rule 32) are responsible for **offside** and **icing**, and they **conduct every faceoff except the one that starts each period** (Rule 32.2). They also:

- May **stop play and report** too many men, thrown objects, interference with an official, an illegally received stick, or bench interference.
- **Must stop play** for premature goalie substitution, a serious injury the referees missed, **faceoff encroachment**, an illegal **hand pass**, **high-sticking the puck**, **icing**, **offside**, the puck out of bounds or unplayable, a goal the referees did not see, and spectator interference.
- **Must report to the referees** the circumstances of majors, match penalties, misconducts, game misconducts, and abuse of officials. If a linesperson sees such an infraction by an attacking player before a goal that the referees missed, **the goal is disallowed and the penalty assessed**.
- **May call too many men** (Rule 74.2).

**Under CARHA Hockey's book the on-ice crew is two rather than four, and both of them are Referees.** Rule 40(a) provides that *"[f]or all CARHA Hockey games, there shall be two Referees, one Official Scorer, one Game Timekeeper, one Penalty Timekeeper, and two Goal Judges when available"*, and 40(d) that *"[t]wo Referees will be used in all games and they shall have equal authority. If the Referees are not of the same opinion on any certain play, the Referee who was closest to the play shall have the final decision."* **Five of those seven are off the ice**: the book's Glossary defines On-Ice Officials as *"[t]he Referees who are appointed to directly supervise the application of the Rules from the ice"*, and lists the Off-Ice Officials as *"Official Scorer, Game Timekeeper, Penalty Timekeeper and two Goal Judges."* So the two Referees do both jobs — Rule 41(d) has them stop play *"for any other infractions of the rules. (e.g. icing the puck, offsides, etc.)"*, and the signal pages are headed *"Referee's Signals"* and describe a **back** and a **front** Referee where the NHL's name a linesperson. ⚠️ **The book does contradict itself once here, and that is worth knowing rather than being told flatly that CARHA has no linespersons:** its icing signal, on page 82, reads *"The back Referee or Linesman in the play signals a possible icing by fully extending either arm over their head"* — the only occurrence of the word *"Linesman"* anywhere in the book, naming a position Rule 40 never appoints and no other rule gives a duty to. **CARHA never reconciles the two and nothing in it says which governs.** Take Rule 40 and the Glossary as the answer and expect two Referees, but know the stray clause is there. **CARHA governs CARHA-affiliated leagues only, and how much rec, beer-league and youth hockey runs on its book is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it** — elsewhere the size of the crew is set by the league rather than by a rulebook, so ask. Where the crew is two rather than four, that is a genuine difference in what they can see, and it is worth adjusting your expectations accordingly.

### The signals you should recognise

From Rule 29. These are the ones with immediate consequences for what you do next:

| Signal | What it looks like | What it means for you |
|---|---|---|
| **Delayed offside** | Non-whistle arm **fully extended straight above the head** (29.7) | Get out of the zone and tag up. Do not touch the puck or chase the D. |
| **Delayed penalty** | Non-whistle arm **fully extended above the head** (29.8) — same as delayed offside | Look at who is doing it. **Linesperson at the blue line = offside. Referee = penalty.** Head to the bench for the extra attacker. |
| **Icing (pending)** | Back linesperson **raises either arm fully overhead**, holds it until the whistle or the wave-off (29.19) | Puck is going down; if you are chasing, the race is to the **puck**, and it will be judged by the time someone reaches the dot. |
| **Icing (completed)** | The back linesperson **points to the faceoff spot**, skates there, turns backwards near the blue line and **crosses his arms on his chest** (29.19) | Icing confirmed. No change for the offending team. |
| **Wash out** | **Sweeping sideways motion of both arms** across the body at shoulder level, palms down (29.35) | No goal (referee), or no icing / no offside (linesperson), or hand pass / high-stick washed out. **Play on.** |
| **Time-out / unsportsmanlike conduct** | Both hands forming a **"T"** in front of the chest (29.31, 29.34) | Identical signals — context decides. |
| **Misconduct** | **Both hands on hips** (29.25) | Ten minutes, but the team is not shorthanded. |
| **Penalty shot** | Arms extended, pointing to the **centre-ice faceoff spot** (29.26) | — |

Common infraction signals worth knowing on sight: **hooking** (tugging both arms toward the stomach), **slashing** (chopping one hand across the opposite forearm), **tripping** (striking the leg below the knee, both skates on the ice), **holding** (clasping one wrist), **cross-checking** (both fists forward and back from the chest), **interference** (arms crossed in an "X" on the chest), **elbowing** (tapping an elbow), **boarding** (fist into the open palm), **charging** (fists rotating around each other), **high-sticking** (both fists clenched, one above the other, at forehead height), **roughing** (fist clenched, arm extended to the side), **hand pass** (open palm pushing forward), and **checking from behind** (both arms extended forward from the chest at shoulder level, palms out).

*Note: three further signals are not described here because there are none to describe. For match penalty (29.24), throwing equipment (29.30) and too many men on the ice (29.32) the rulebook prints "No signal in the National Hockey League. NO SIGNAL" — the same entry it gives for head-butting and kicking below.*

The rulebook also notes there is **no signal in the NHL** for **head-butting** (29.14) or **kicking** (29.22).

### Talking to officials

The rules on this are unusually blunt. Under **Rule 39 (Abuse of Officials)** and **Rule 75 (Unsportsmanlike Conduct)** the ladder runs **minor, then misconduct, then game misconduct** — but ⚠️ **the books state that as the usual course, not as a step the referee has to take**, and the two words that make the difference are their own. NHL 39.4(ix) reads *"**In general**, participants displaying this type of behaviour are assessed a minor penalty, then a misconduct penalty and then a game misconduct penalty if they persist"*, and the sentence is repeated at **39.5(vii), 75.4(v) and 75.5(vi)** — and at **IIHF 39.4(IX), 39.5(VII) and 75.4(V)**, so a British reader is under it too. ⚠️ **So do not count on a free minor first.** Named limbs of both rules start higher with nothing below them: a **misconduct** on a penalised player who does not proceed directly to the penalty bench when instructed (NHL 39.4(vi)), who enters or remains in the Referee's crease while he is reporting to another official (39.4(v)), or who disputes a ruling **after he has entered the penalty box and play has resumed** (75.4(iv)); and a **game misconduct on the first offence** for obscene gestures anywhere in the rink (75.5(ii)), for leaving the penalty bench to challenge a ruling (39.5(iv)) — ⚠️ **and the automatic suspension the NHL attaches to that one, for *"the next three (3) regular League and/or Play-off games of his Club"*, is the NHL's own figure and does not travel: the IIHF's own Rule 39.5(IV) states the offence and omits the penalty entirely, leaving Rule 70.4 to supply a game misconduct with **discretionary** supplementary discipline rather than a fixed suspension** — and for deliberately applying physical force to an official (39.5(iii)), which routes to Rule 40. **39.5(ii) makes the point in the book's own voice:** it gates that game misconduct on a bench minor having been assessed already, then lifts the gate where the conduct happens after the game, *"without the necessity of having been assessed a bench minor penalty previously."*

Practically:

- **The standing is much narrower than most players think.** Rule 6.1: *"Only the Captain, **when invited to do so by the Referee**, shall have the privilege of discussing any point relating to the interpretation of rules."* Two things follow. First, it is the **captain alone** — an alternate inherits the privilege only when the captain is not on the ice (Rule 6.2). Second, and more important, **you do not have standing until you are invited.** Approaching an official to open a discussion is not a right the letter confers.
- **A complaint about a penalty is not a "rule interpretation", and complaining is itself a minor.** Rule 6.1 is explicit: *"Any Captain, Alternate Captain or any player who comes off the bench and makes any protest or intervention with the officials for any purpose shall be assessed a minor penalty for unsportsmanlike conduct under Rule 39."* So the captain may ask about how a rule is being interpreted, when invited — but may not dispute a call, and is penalised for trying.
- **The moment to say something is not the moment you disagree.** It is at the next natural break, and only if the official opens the door.
- **Ask, do not tell — and only when invited.** A calm question about interpretation may get an answer. "That's terrible" gets ten minutes. Note that even the mild-sounding opener is a protest if the official did not invite it.
- **You will never get a call reversed.** What you can occasionally influence is what the officials are watching for. That is worth having a captain who is good at it, and worth everyone else keeping quiet.
- **In rec hockey with two officials**, half the ice is unwatched at any moment. Complaining that something was missed is worse than pointless — everything is missed, in both directions.

See [Team Play and Culture](../off-the-ice/team_play_and_culture.md) and [Mental Game](../off-the-ice/mental_game.md).

---

## 10. Rule Set Differences That Actually Matter

The differences below are the ones that change **what you should do on the ice**. Everything else is trivia.

**Read the Home Countries column against the IIHF one beside it.** IHUK and the BUIHA run the IIHF book and amend it in writing, across *"England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"* and in university hockey, so **"As IIHF" is a positive statement, not a blank**: it means the In-House Rules carry no amendment to that rule, and the IIHF cell to its left is therefore the rule you play under. Only the cells that say **Amended** are British departures, and each of those quotes the In-House Rules. The Elite League runs the IIHF book with its own casebook, which has not been read here — nothing in the Home Countries column should be taken as describing EIHL interpretation. ⚠️ **Several individual amendments still scope themselves more narrowly than the document does**, writing *"in EIH or SIHA competition"* where the Introduction reaches all four home nations; where a cell quotes that wording, it is the source's.

| | **NHL** | **IIHF** | **Home Countries** | **USA Hockey** | **Typical rec / beer league** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Trapezoid** | **Yes** (Rules 1.8, 27.8) — minor for delay of game; only exception is skate contact with the crease | **Yes** (Rule 27.7) — same rule; 6.80 m at goal line, 8.60 m at boards | **Unsettled in England and Wales.** The In-House Rules amend Rule 1 but not Rule 27; England Ice Hockey's *Rules & Regulations* 22.3 say it is *"not currently enforced"* — and EIH governs England and Wales only, so in Scotland, Northern Ireland and BUIHA hockey, whose instrument is the In-House Rules and is silent, 27.7 reads as live. The Elite League runs its own Casebook, unread here. If it is **your** goalie assume it binds; if it is **theirs** assume they may come for it | **No trapezoid** — the goalie may *play* the puck anywhere **behind the net**, corners included. Two other limits still bind: Rule 614(c) makes it a minor to *freeze* it outside the "goalkeeper's privileged area" (dot line back to the end boards; corners excluded), and Rule 407(c) makes it a minor to play it beyond the centre red line — see the row below | **Usually no** (varies — check) |
| **Goalie participating beyond the centre red line** | **Minor** (Rule 27.7). **The position of the *puck* is the determining factor**, in the rule's own words | **Minor** (Rule 27.6 — the same sentence, one number down). **The *puck* decides**, same wording | **As IIHF** — the In-House Rules carry no amendment to Rule 27 | **Minor** (Rule 407(c)), and **the test is reversed**: the *Casebook*, Rule 407 Situation 13, has it that *"the determining factor is the position of the goalkeeper's skates and not the puck"* | Follows the book adopted. **Hockey Canada reverses it too** — Rule 4.13(c), penalised under Rule 8.3(a) Interference, with Interpretation 2 giving the goaltender a minor because *"the determining factor is the position of the goaltender's skates and not the position of the puck"* |
| **Icing type** | **Hybrid** (since 2013-14) — race is to the **puck**, decided by the time a skate reaches the **end-zone dot** | **Hybrid** (since 2014) — wording near-identical to NHL, but adds the no-race paragraph noted above | **As IIHF** — hybrid, including the no-race paragraph | **No-touch / automatic** — complete the instant the puck crosses the goal line | **Usually no-touch**; some touch, some hybrid (varies — check). **Hockey Canada is split by category**: hybrid icing applies in U18AAA, Junior, and Senior at the Member's option (Hockey Canada Rule 6.7(e)); in every other category, including the rest of minor and female hockey, it is automatic |
| **Icing: "gaining the line"** | Puck **on the stick, not the skate**, must contact the centre red line to nullify a potential icing (Rule 81.1) | **The same sentence, word for word** (Rule 81.1) | **As IIHF** — the In-House Rules carry no amendment to Rule 81 | **No such concept.** The phrase is not in the book, and neither is the stick-versus-skate distinction; Rule 624 (Note) judges *"the point of last contact with the puck"* | Follows the book adopted. **Hockey Canada likewise has no "gain the line"** — Interpretation 5 to Rule 6.7(c)(ii) judges *"where the puck was last contacted, relative to the centre red line"* |
| **Icing: line change** | **Not permitted** for the offending team; three exceptions (pulled goalie, injured player, strength-changing penalty) | **Not permitted** — same bar (Rule 81.4), and that rule adds a fourth exception, *"a Player with a broken skate"*, which is its only difference from NHL 81.4. ⚠️ **The fourth one is in 81.4 only:** Rule 82.1 restates the same icing bar with the other three and not this one, the book does not say which governs, and the Situation Handbook does not settle it — its Situation 81.7 in fact answers that a change is permitted *"only"* on a strength-changing penalty, which is over-broad but adverse. **Do not count on it** — and do not send a replacement over on it: **Rule 82.2(V)** sends them back and warns the coach, and that warning is one per team per game, after which it is a bench minor for delay of game | **As IIHF** — including the broken-skate exception the NHL does not have, **and including the 81.4/82.1 mismatch behind it**: the In-House Rules amend neither rule, and their Introduction makes silence adoption — *"Unless otherwise stated, all rules will be enforced in line with the IIHF Rule Book"* | **Permitted after an icing** — Rule 624 restricts no substitution. **The change is still not free, because the change window closes at every stoppage and being late is penalised in two tiers, each after a warning.** Rule 204(a) allows no further substitution *"[o]nce the line change procedure has been completed"* and makes a subsequent *"undue delay by either team in placing their lineup on the ice or changing players"* a **minor**; Rule 610(g) makes a team that *"causes any delay by making additional substitutions"* a **bench minor**. The *Casebook* at Rule 613 Situation 15 — a false face-off — orders players attempting to change *"back to the players' bench"*, the same remedy as IIHF 82.2(V) — citing the provision as *"610(h)"*, which the printed rule does not have | Varies. **Hockey Canada bars it in U18AAA, Junior and Senior-at-the-Member's-option only** (Hockey Canada Rule 6.7(d)); in every other category the change is permitted. Three exceptions, and the third is **wider than the NHL's** — a pulled goaltender, an injured player, and *"when a penalty or penalties have been assessed to either team"*, where the NHL requires a penalty that **affects on-ice strength**, so coincidental penalties permit a change under Hockey Canada and not under the NHL. Senior is at the Member's option, so in an adult Hockey Canada league **assume you may not change** until told otherwise; 6.7(d) states no penalty itself, but Hockey Canada Rule 10.1 – Delay of Game names *"'no change' situations (Rule 6.1 (f))"* expressly: a warning first, then a **bench minor** under 10.1(a) |
| **Accidentally knocking your own net off: line change** | **Not permitted** — Rule 63.8: *"[t]he offending team shall not be permitted to make any player substitutions prior to the face-off"*, on three exceptions — a pulled goalkeeper, an injured player, a strength-changing penalty — with the attacking team choosing the dot. **The bar follows the *defending* team's act only** — an attacker who displaces the net is Rule 63.4, which lets play run on and attaches no bar | **Not permitted** — IIHF Rule 63.8 is the same rule, writing *"a defending Player"* for the NHL's *"a defending skater or goalkeeper"*, and identical across the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions | **As IIHF** — the In-House Rules contain nothing against Rule 63 | **No such bar was found.** Rule 610(d) stops play for a displaced goal frame and makes a deliberate displacement a minor, but attaches no substitution consequence; Rule 612 sets no faceoff location for it; nothing in Rule 204 or in the *Casebook* situations on Rules 610 and 617 supplies one | Varies. **Hockey Canada writes the bar at Rule 6.1(g), but only *"[i]n categories of U18 AAA, Junior and at the option of the Member in Senior"*** — the scope of its icing bar — and routes it to the same restrictions as Rule 6.7(d)(i). Below U18 AAA, and in Senior unless the Member has adopted it, there is none. **CARHA's rule book writes no substitution bar for a dislodged net either** |
| **Change on the fly: how far from the bench** | **Five feet (5')** from the players' bench **and out of the play** before the substitute steps on (Rule 74.1). Two triggers turn the overlap into a bench minor: either player plays the puck, or makes physical contact with an opponent | **1.50m** and out of the play (Rule 74.1) — the same measurement in metric, and the same two triggers, in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions alike | **As IIHF** — the In-House Rules carry no amendment to Rule 74 | **No distance at all.** Rule 204(b) requires only that the retiring player *"shall always be at the players' bench and out of the play before any change is made."* ⚠️ **And it adds a third trigger the other three books have not got** — a bench minor where the change *"causes their team to gain a competitive advantage"*, with no puck played and nobody hit. The *Casebook*, Rule 204 Situation 8, applies it to a change that turns a 2-on-1 against into a 2-on-2: *"Team A gained a territorial or positional advantage as a result of the change… and should be penalized"* | **Hockey Canada gives twice the NHL's room** — Rule 6.1(a), *"at the Players' Bench (within 3.05m (10 ft.)) and out of play"*, penalised under Rule 10.7(a). **CARHA Rule 19(b) writes the same 10 ft** |
| **Icing: shorthanded exemption** | **Yes — with one carve-out.** Rule 81.6's third paragraph withdraws it from a team shorthanded by a **major** that has *"neglected to ensure there is a player on the penalty bench to exit upon the expiry of the penalty"* — they *"are not permitted to ice the puck"* | **Yes** — but without the NHL's major-penalty carve-out (IIHF Rule 81.6 has only two paragraphs) | **As IIHF** — unconditional | **Yes only at Youth 15-Only+, Girls 16U+, HS and Adult** | Usually yes |
| **Icing: goalie waves it off** | **Yes** — feigning, attempting or skating toward the puck nullifies it | **Yes** | **As IIHF** | **No** — only actual contact | Varies |
| **Icing: timeout** | **Not permitted** to the defensive team (Rule 87.1) | **Not permitted** — IIHF 87.1 is the NHL sentence verbatim, third limb included | **As IIHF** — no relief anywhere on that list | **Permitted** — Rule 636(f) attaches no icing condition, **but 636(f) gives no timeout at all in a curfew game** | Varies. **Hockey Canada expressly permits it** in the three categories where the no-change bar applies (Hockey Canada Rule 6.7(d)(iii)), with the icing team still barred from changing during it; its timeout is **30 seconds**, and in minor and female hockey it exists only in stop-time games where the local Member has approved it — though it is mandatory at Hockey Canada minor and female regional and national championships (Note 3 to Hockey Canada Rule 6.17(e)) |
| **Body checking** | **Legal** (men's) | **Legal** (men's). **Women's:** hitting only with clear intent to play the puck; no using the boards to eliminate an opponent (Rule 101.1) | **As IIHF** for the checking rules themselves — but the **categories they attach to are British**: junior is U19/U16/U14/U12/U10 by birth year and **unisex**, women's Adult runs from the 14th birthday (In-House Rules, Rules 100.1, 200). Neither book sets a checking age — see [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) | **Prohibited** at 12U and below, all girls'/women's, all non-check adult classifications (Rule 604) | **Usually prohibited** — non-check leagues are the norm |
| **Illegal check to the head** | **Minor only.** Rule 48.3: *"There is no provision for a major penalty for this rule"*; 48.4 the same for a game misconduct. Match penalty available for intent to injure (48.5) | **Head *or neck*, and it can end your game.** *"There is no clean check to the head or neck"* (48.1), with four enumerated triggers including *"jumps (leaves their skates) to deliver a blow"*; **48.3 gives a major plus an automatic game misconduct** for reckless endangerment. **One carve-out, and all of it is conditions:** IIHF 48.1 does not penalise a square bodycheck delivered with no upward motion on an opponent *"skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player"* — so it reaches only the carrier coming toward the checker, not a head-down player hit from the side — and the IIHF book attaches no penalty **under Rule 48** to the player whose head was down. **But the low player is not unpenalised generally** — IIHF 44.1 says a player *"may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them"*: a minor under 44.2 on the degree of violence, and a major plus an **automatic game misconduct** under 44.3 for reckless endangerment. | **As IIHF** — the In-House Rules carry no amendment to Rule 48, the carve-out included. Below adult, majors bite harder still: see [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md). The In-House Rules amend neither Rule 48 nor Rule 44, so **the carve-out and the 44.1 crouching clause are both yours.** | **Minor + misconduct *or* major + game misconduct** (Rule 620(a)); the major and game misconduct are **compulsory** for reckless endangerment **and for contact that is merely careless** (620(b) — *"intentionally or carelessly contacts an opponent in the head, face or neck"*); match penalty available (620(c)). **No head-down or ducking exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules** — USA Hockey Rule 620's Note puts the onus on the checker *"to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger of potential injury"* | **Assume ejection.** Both books the column rests on allow it, and Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"*. **But Hockey Canada carves out ducking — the *checker's* amnesty in junior and senior divisions only, while Rule 8.7's prohibition on crouching carries no division scope:** Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) gives no Head Contact penalty against a checker whose otherwise legal check hits the head of a player who *"ducks to avoid a check"*, and lets the referee give that player a Clipping Minor (Hockey Canada Rule 8.7(a), rising to a mandatory major plus game misconduct under 8.7(b) if the duck injures the checker — or at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence with nobody hurt — and a **mandatory** match penalty under 8.7(c) for an attempt to injure as well as a deliberate one; the prohibition itself is in 8.7's preamble — *"Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked"*). **And the crouching prohibition is not Hockey Canada's alone — IIHF 44.1 carries a narrower one, in the book this column rests on:** a player *"may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them"*, a minor under 44.2 and a major plus an automatic game misconduct under 44.3 for reckless endangerment. Hockey Canada excepts a player vulnerable *"as the result of a legitimate hockey play"* — shooting, making or receiving a pass — and confines the **checker's amnesty** to junior and senior, so a checker in minor or female hockey has none — but Rule 8.7's prohibition on the ducker is not confined and reaches every division |
| **Checking from behind** | **No minor exists.** Rule 43.2: *"There is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"*; 43.3 is a major, and 43.5 makes a game misconduct **mandatory** *"anytime a major penalty is applied"*. Match penalty for intent to injure (43.4) | **No minor either.** Rule 43.2: *"there is no option to award a minor penalty"*; 43.3 gives a **major plus an automatic game misconduct** where the check *"recklessly endangers the fouled Player"* | **As IIHF** — the In-House Rules carry no amendment to Rule 43. Section 6 (Physical Fouls), where such an amendment would sit, contains only the fighting provision, and no rule numbered 43 appears anywhere in the document. So a check from behind ends your game in Britain | **The one book that lets you finish the game.** Rule 608(a) allows *"a minor plus a misconduct penalty, or a major plus a game misconduct penalty"* — **never a bare minor**, but the lighter option leaves you on the bench. 608(b) makes the major and game misconduct **compulsory** where the check *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame, as a result of checking from behind"*; 608(c) reaches a match penalty. **Its *Casebook* narrows the lighter option further than 608(b) does** — Rule 608, Situation 1 holds the minor plus misconduct to an open-ice push and a low-force *"'pinch' against the boards"*, and requires the major plus game misconduct or the match penalty *"In every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"* and wherever the check recklessly endangers, *"regardless of whether or not board contact is made."* **For the hit this table is about, assume you are ejected here too** | Follows the book adopted — and **Hockey Canada is not the soft option either**: Rule 7.5(a) assesses *"A Minor penalty **and** a Game Misconduct penalty"*, **7.5(c) makes a match penalty mandatory where the player was "unable to protect or defend themselves"**, and 7.5(e) makes the game misconduct compulsory *"any time a Minor or Major penalty is assessed"*. **Assume you are ejected** |
| **High-sticking** | **Double minor** for injury (60.3); **no major** tier; **match penalty** where a player *"attempts to or deliberately injures"* with a high stick (60.4). Waist floor is in **60.1 and 60.3 but not 60.2**, whose *"Any contact made by a stick on an opponent above the shoulders is prohibited"* matches both IIHF editions' — do not plan a stick on a crouching opponent on the strength of the floor | **Major + automatic game misconduct** available where the high stick *"recklessly endangers"* (60.4). **The waist floor depends on which edition your league is on** — the 2026/27 book has it at 60.1 and 60.3 in the NHL's words; the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) book has it at neither, so under that one the line is the opponent's shoulders where they actually are. **Not outright, though** — 60.1 in both editions permits *"'accidental contact' on the opposing center who is bent over during the course of a 'face-off'"*. Under both, 60.2 carries no floor | **Amended in one respect.** The In-House Rules (Section 8) remove the 60.3 on-ice video review — *"On-ice video review is not available in EIH or SIHA competition"* — so the call on the ice stands. Otherwise as IIHF | Minor baseline (621(a)); **major + game misconduct mandatory** for reckless endangerment (621(b)); match penalty available (621(c)). ⚠️ **The playing rule and the Casebook disagree on whether contact is needed.** 621's Note requires the stick above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders *"and makes contact with the opponent"*; Casebook Rule 621, Situation 2 answers *"No."* to whether contact must occur, adds that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*, and requires the call *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions."* Assume the wider of the two | **Assume a major and a game misconduct — and a match penalty if the opponent you hit could not protect himself, or the referee reads the injury as deliberate — not the NHL's double minor.** Which book binds you depends on the one your league adopts, and **Hockey Canada's playing rules never state which division adult recreational hockey sits in** — an equipment note in passing implies Senior registration, but no playing rule says it. If you are registered **Senior**, the rule you are actually exposed to is **Hockey Canada 7.7 – Head Contact – Junior/Senior**, whose preamble says that *"in Junior and Senior divisions, this rule supersedes any other rules regarding contact to the head"* bar fighting. Its floor is not a bare minor — 7.7(a) is a minor **plus a misconduct**, made mandatory by 7.7(d) — and 7.7(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct for a high degree of violence, for a cross-check above the shoulders *"whether or not injury results"*, and where the foul drives your opponent's head into the glass, ice or boards and injures it; 7.7(c) is the match penalty. Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 narrows that cross-check limb, though — it says the rule is *"not intended to penalize players who make minimal, accidental contact"* but those who *"make a deliberate cross-checking action and strike an opponent in the head or neck"*. It is headed to **7.6(b)**, the minor-and-female rule, and 7.7 has no equivalent interpretation; it is read across here because 7.6(b) and 7.7(b) carry that cross-check sentence in identical words. Hockey Canada's high-sticking rule **9.5** cannot itself go above a minor, or a discretionary double minor for an accidental high stick of minimal violence that injures: 9.5(b) and 9.5(c) bar a major and a match penalty **and route each to 7.7**; 9.5(d) and 9.5(e) bar a misconduct and a game misconduct outright, routing nothing anywhere, and 7.7(d) supplies the misconduct instead. 9.5's preamble also lets a penalty be assessed *"regardless of whether or not contact occurs"*, though 9.5(a) — the only clause that assesses one — requires contact, so the text does not settle whether the carry alone can do it; assume it can. In minor and female hockey the route is **Rule 7.6** instead, and the full routing is in the stick-fouls list above |
| **Broken stick: what a skater does with it** | **Drop it, and you may play on without one** (Rule 10.3) — a minor if you keep hold of it and participate. A replacement may be handed over from your bench or by a teammate on the ice, or you may pick an unbroken stick up off the ice | **The same drop rule** (Rule 10.3), word for word in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions — but its throwing bar is fuller than the NHL's, penalising a player who *"throw, toss, slide or shoot a stick to a teammate (skater) on the ice"* where the NHL's list is *"throws or shoots"* alone | **Amended, and new for 2026-27.** The In-House Rules add their own Rule 10.3, which the 2025-26 edition did not have: *"Skating with a broken stick anywhere on the ice is not permitted under IHUK In-House Rules. A player whose stick is broken must drop their stick immediately. Failure to do so will incur a 2min penalty for illegal equipment"*. **No proximity test and no out-of-play test** — it reaches the act *"anywhere on the ice."* It is headed *"Broken Stick: Player"* and writes no goalkeeper counterpart, so on the document's own silence-is-adoption terms IIHF Rule 10.4 still governs a British goalie | **Drop it — but a short carry to the bench is allowed on conditions.** Rule 605(a); *Casebook* Rule 605 Situation 1 permits it *"only if they are in the immediate vicinity of the bench, is completely out of the play and does so immediately upon breaking the stick"*, and Situation 3 withdraws it where the player must *"go a great distance and/or in the proximity of opposing players"*. Rule 605(b) allows a replacement *"only … from the player's bench or from a teammate on the ice"* — not off the ice | Follows the book adopted. **Hockey Canada is the flat version** — Rule 3.2(a), *"A player whose stick has been broken must drop all pieces of the broken stick to the ice immediately"*, penalised under Rule 10.6 – Illegal Equipment |
| **Fighting** | **5-minute major**, not an automatic ejection for a willing pair; extra minor + misconduct for the **instigator**, and the **aggressor is ejected** — major plus game misconduct (Rules 46, 46.2) | Penalised hard, and **46.1 was rewritten between the editions.** Under the **2026/27** book a major plus an **automatic game misconduct** is the standard penalty for any player *"willing to fight and/or actively participat[ing] in a fight"* — not just for persisting; under the **2025/26 (Version 1.1)** book a willing combatant *"shall be penalized accordingly by the Referee(s) and may be ejected from the game"*, with the automatic game misconduct reserved for persisting. *"'Fighting' is not part of international ice hockey's DNA"* in both (Rule 46.1). **Assume you are gone either way** | **Amended.** *"Fighting shall be penalised with a 5-minute Major Penalty"* in NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity; in NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2 a fighting major in the **last 5 minutes** of regulation or overtime *"will also carry an automatic Game Misconduct"* (In-House Rules, Section 6). ⚠️ **That is the plain willing fight only.** Section 6 names no rule number, and the In-House introduction says *"Unless otherwise stated, all rules will be enforced in line with the IIHF Rule Book"* — so the rest of Rule 46 stands: **instigator (46.3)** and **aggressor (46.4)** add a major and an automatic game misconduct to a minor; **sucker-punching (46.5)** and **fighting outside the periods (46.9)** are a major plus an automatic game misconduct; **fighting off the playing surface (46.11)** adds a game misconduct *"in addition to any other time penalties assessed, including the major penalty"*; **third man in (46.7)** is a game misconduct *"at the discretion of the Referee"*, except where one is already being imposed in the original altercation; and **46.1 itself** lets the referee impose *"at least"* a major plus an automatic game misconduct on a player who persists after being ordered to stop **or who resists a linesperson**. **Assume you are gone.** Two things about the five-minute treatment are readings rather than things Section 6 says: that it reaches a plain willing fight and nothing else, and that an In-House **penalty** displaces 20.4's automatic game misconduct for a Table 6 entry. Section 6 states neither. It also binds only NIHL, SNL and BUIHA Varsity. Anywhere else in British hockey 46.1 is a Table 6 entry — major plus automatic game misconduct — and 46.1 lets the referee eject a willing combatant in any case. In junior hockey any major adds a game misconduct. Rule 28 supplementary discipline sits above all of it. | **Major + game misconduct**; second fighting major **with the same team** in a season = **3-game suspension** (Rule 615(f)) | **Ejection and suspension**, nearly always |
| **When majors and misconducts eject you** | **A first major can do it, and the book flags its own tension about when.** 20.4's second sentence attaches no condition to a Table 5 major; the individual rules do. Of Table 5's thirteen non-fighting rows it is **unconditional for seven** — checking from behind 43.5, head-butting 47.5, kneeing 50.5, butt-ending 58.5, cross-checking 59.5, slashing 61.5, spearing 62.5 — and **injury-conditional for six**: face or head at boarding 41.5, charging 42.5, elbowing 45.5; any injury at clipping 44.5, hooking 55.4, interference 56.5. Fighting's **six** rows carry it unconditionally too — thirteen non-fighting plus six is the nineteen used elsewhere in this document. **No major at all** for high-sticking, slew-footing or an illegal check to the head — **all three are a match penalty there instead (60.4, 52.2, 48.5), and 52.2's is mandatory and unconditional** — 60.4 and 48.5 both require an attempt to injure or a deliberate injury. Assume the harsher reading. Residually a **third major** (20.4). **No second-misconduct trigger** in Rule 22 | **A first major ends your game for any of the twenty infractions in Reference Table 6 — the 2025/26 book's number, merged into the 2026/27 edition's single Table 5 along with Tables 5 and 7** — checking from behind, clipping, illegal check to the head, high-sticking, cross-checking, slashing, hooking and slew-footing among them — and 20.4 makes that game misconduct **unconditional**, where the NHL's equivalent turns on injury for half its list. **And Table 6 is not the whole of your first-major risk: Table 7 adds five more, each written as a single major-plus-game-misconduct act — boarding 41.4, charging 42.4, elbowing 45.4, interference 56.5 and tripping 57.4**. Table 7's heading rests the game misconduct on the referee's judgement, so this is not beyond doubt; **play as though the ejection follows the major.** Residually a **second** major *or* a **second misconduct** in the same game (20.4, 22.1); the NHL waits for a third major and has no second-misconduct trigger at all. No face-or-head-injury clause | **As IIHF** at adult level — the In-House Rules amend neither Rule 20 nor Rule 22 on their own triggers — **but they reach the outcome by other routes**: fighting is a five-minute major in NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity, with the automatic game misconduct attaching in NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2 only in the last five minutes of regulation or in overtime; a second neck-guard violation escalates to a game misconduct; a second facial-protection misconduct does the same. **And the equipment section is a route by arithmetic** — a missing neck guard is an immediate 10-minute misconduct with **no warning**, while a tucked jersey, an uncovered elbow pad and a chin-strap gap are each a warning first and a 10-minute misconduct on the repeat — and IIHF 22.1 ejects you on the second misconduct of any kind in the same game, with no foul committed against anybody. Below adult they are stricter: see [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md) | **Any game misconduct** already costs the team's next game automatically (Rule 404(b)). A **second major** to the same player in a game brings one plus *"suspended for their team's next two games"* (403(b)); a **second misconduct** becomes a game misconduct (404(a)); season-long accumulation is separate (411) | Varies — **read your league's sheet** |
| **Offside review** | **Coach's Challenge** (Rule 38.2(a)); failed challenge = **2:00 minor**, further failures **4:00** | Coach's Challenge available (Rule 83.5 → Rule 38); same 2:00 / 4:00 escalation (Rule 38.7) | **Amended — none at all.** Video review is *"not available in EIH or SIHA competition"* (Rule 37) and a Coach's Challenge is *"not permitted"* (Rule 38). The referee's call stands | **None** — no video review in the playing rules | **Usually none.** One published rec book has none at all: CARHA Hockey's writes no video review and no coach's challenge, and settles a disputed goal on the ice — the Referee decides *"after having consulted with the other Referee and the Goal Judge involved"* (CARHA Rule 41(e)) |
| **Airborne trail skate at the blue line** | **Onside** — judged on a vertical **plane**; a raised skate that has not broken it is onside (Rule 83.1) | **Onside** — same plane concept (Rule 83.1) | **As IIHF** — onside; drag or lift, either works | **Offside** — Rule 630(a) requires **skate contact** with the Neutral Zone or the blue line; only a skate *touching* is onside | Follows whichever book the league adopts — **assume contact is required** unless told otherwise. **Hockey Canada Rule 6.11 is the same as USA Hockey**: *"Only the player's skate(s) that are in physical contact with the ice surface will be used in determining an off-side"* |
| **Faceoff violation** | **Centre ejected from the draw** (Rules 76.6, 76.7); second violation by the same team on the same draw is a **bench minor**; winning the draw with your skates is **legal** | **Centre removed — but only since the 2026/27 edition.** 76.6 has the encroaching team's draw-taker *"ejected from the face-off circle"*; 76.3 and 76.4 have the offending centre replaced rather than warned. Second violation still a bench minor (76.7). **Winning the draw with your skates is itself a violation** (Rule 76.3(VI)) — in both editions. The **2025/26** book ejected nobody: every first violation was a warning to the team, on every draw | **Amended, and the amendment settles it — your centre goes.** The In-House Rules **2026-27** add a Rule 76 the 2025-26 edition did not have: *"With the exception of face-offs following icings, the linesperson shall immediately replace the centre for all violations."* So the answer does not turn on which IIHF edition your league runs. The **skate win** is a violation under both editions and IHUK does not amend 76.3, so it costs you the centre too — take "kick it back" out of your repertoire. **Second violation is still a bench minor** (IIHF 76.7, unamended). ⚠️ **And the stick order is gone**: *"Either centre can now put their stick down first for a face-off"*, with *"the whole of their stick blades (not toe or heel)"* required in the white | **Centre ejected** if a teammate is out of position (Rule 613(d)); second violation is a **minor to the player who committed it**, not a bench minor. Note also that **the *attacking* player puts his stick down first** at every spot except centre ice (Rule 613(a)) — the reverse of the NHL | Often not called at all, or simply re-dropped (varies — check) |
| **Tagging up on a delayed offside** | **Yes** | **Yes** | **As IIHF** — at every age and level | **Only at Youth 15-Only+, Girls 16U+, HS and Adult** (Rule 630(d)) — below that, offside is immediate | Usually yes. **Hockey Canada sets no classification limit** — Hockey Canada Rule 6.11 (Section 6 – Game Flow) carries no category or division clause at all, where the same book scopes its icing rule in terms (Rule 6.7(e), *"In categories of U18AAA, Junior, and at the option of the Member in Senior"*). So a delayed offside tags up the same way in every Hockey Canada division, and the limit in the USA Hockey column beside it is USA Hockey's alone |
| **Overtime** | Regular season: **5 min, 3-on-3**, then **3-round shootout**. Playoffs: **unlimited 20-min sudden-death periods at even strength** — Rule 84.5 states the periods, the first intermission and the change of ends, but no on-ice strength at all, so the even strength is an inference from its silence | Prelim: **5 min 3-on-3** + 5-shooter shootout. Playoff, relegation, classification and bronze: **up to 10 min 3-on-3** + shootout (84.5 names all four). Gold medal **of a top-category championship**: unlimited 3-on-3 sudden-death periods, no length stated in the rule | **Amended, and often absent.** Played *"only … in NIHL fixtures and in SNL fixtures where specifically provided for in competition regulations"* (Rule 84). NIHL League and group-stage playoffs: **5 min 3-on-3** then a **3-shooter** GWS. Playoff Final Weekend splits: **semi-final** — one **20-min 5-on-5** period, *then the same 3-shooter GWS*; **final** — successive **20-min 5-on-5** sudden death ("golden goal"), **no GWS** (Appendix 1) | **Recommended** 10-min sudden-victory period after a 5-min rest; shootout optional; **affiliates may set their own** (Rule 638) | **Usually none — ties stand.** Where it exists, short 4-on-4 or 3-on-3, sometimes a shootout |
| **Period length** | **3 × 20 min**, stop time; **18-min** intermissions | **3 × 20 min**; **15-min** intermissions | **As IIHF** | **Max 3 × 20 min**; **15-min** intermissions; **minor** penalty times pro-rated for shorter periods (Rule 402), Adults always 2:00 — majors, misconducts and match penalties only where an affiliate has chosen to reduce them (Notes to Rules 403, 404, 405), and never for Adults | Often shorter and often **running clock**; curfews common |

**A note on the rec column:** unlike the other four, it is not one book. There is no single governing document for rec hockey, and the column describes what is common across leagues that mostly adopt USA Hockey or Hockey Canada rules with local amendments. Where a cell in it says *"usually"*, *"often"*, *"varies"* or *"nearly always"*, that is a statement about how leagues tend to write their own rules and not a rule itself — **how many leagues fall either side of it is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it.** Where a cell instead names a book and a rule number, that part is checkable and is cited.

**A note on the Home Countries column:** unlike the rec column, it *is* a book — two, in fact, and both are published. Read the IIHF Rule Book for the game and the In-House Rules for the amendments; neither works without the other. The Home Countries comparison covers only the amendments that touch the rules compared here, and it is deliberately short, because the In-House Rules are short. **The amendments that will catch you out first are not in this table at all** — neck protection, facial protection, helmet fit, rink size and the junior and women's age categories. [UK and England Rules](uk_rules.md) covers all of them, and [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md) and [Rink Map and Glossary](rink_map_and_glossary.md) carry the equipment and geometry consequences. One further caution: the In-House Rules are revised in-season and IHUK *"may also issue Rule Bulletins from time to time"*, so check for a later revision before relying on a detail. **The British face-off answer given here is written to the 2026-27 edition, Revision 1, published 25 July 2026**, which added a Rule 76 the season before did not have — and the broken-stick row above is written to the same edition, which added a Rule 10.3 the season before did not have either. **The rows that carry an In-House amendment from the previous season were re-checked against that edition and carry forward unchanged** — video review and the Coach's Challenge (Rules 37, 38), goaltender interference (69.1), fighting (Section 6, still headed *"Applicable Leagues: NIHL (all leagues), SNL, and BUIHA Varsity"*) and overtime (Rule 84 with Appendix 1) — and it still amends neither Rule 81 nor Rule 83, so the icing, off-side and tag-up rows stand. **The rows that turn on the junior and women's age categories (Rules 100.1, 200, 201) were not re-compared**, so check those against the current edition yourself.

**Published rec rulebooks do exist and can be read** — "no single book" is not the same as "no book at all". **CARHA Hockey** — the Canadian Adult Recreational Hockey Association — publishes a full standalone *Official Rule Book*, which governs CARHA-affiliated leagues and is not a general rec-hockey standard. **How much Canadian adult hockey runs on it is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it.** Where it does apply it bears out most of the column:

- **No trapezoid — and that is not the same as no restriction.** Neither *"trapezoid"* nor *"restricted area"* appears anywhere in the book, and nothing in it limits where the goalkeeper may **play** the puck. **What it does limit is where they may freeze it:** Rule 58(b) assesses a minor to a goalkeeper who *"deliberately falls on or gathers the puck into their body, or holds or places the puck against any part of the goal or boards when the puck is behind the goal line and the goalkeeper's body is entirely outside the boundaries of the goal crease area"* — so a CARHA goalie may **fetch** a puck behind their own goal line but may not **smother** it out there, which is the same trade USA Hockey makes at its Rule 614(c)(1).
- **Automatic icing** (Rule 65(a)) — play stops when the puck crosses the goal line, and *"any contact with the puck over the centre red line, with the puck continuing down over the goal line nullifies 'icing' the puck."* Note also that *"some CARHA Hockey Leagues will use the blue lines instead of the red line to divide the ice"* for icing purposes. That sentence is printed unlabelled inside Rule 65 itself, between (a) and (b); **Rule 65(b) and Rule 65(c)** are the clauses that say what changes when a league does it. (b) moves the nullification line from the red line to the blue one — *"any contact with the puck over the defensive teams blue line WILL NOT be called icing"* — and (c) makes the offence a shot from behind your own blue line, rather than from your own half, beyond the opposing goal line. It is a variation with no professional equivalent.
- **A team below the opposing team's numerical strength cannot be called for icing** (Rule 65(d)) — *"'Icing the puck' shall not be called if a team is below the numerical strength of the opposing team at the instant the puck is shot."* There is no classification limit on it and no empty-penalty-bench carve-out, and Rule 65(a) writes the same condition into the offence itself, which reaches only a player *"of a team, equal or superior in numerical strength to the opposing team"*. **The limit that bites on the ice is Note 2 to 65(a):** *"A team shall not be considered below the numerical strength of its opponent by reason of a penalized player remaining in the penalty box after their penalty has expired."*
- **Body contact is itself the penalty** (Rule 49(a)): a minor or major *"to any player who intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent."* You do not need to hit anybody; failing to get out of the way is enough.
- **Face-off violations are written in, and they cost you the draw.** This is the one place where the column does not bear out: *"often not called at all"* is a remark about how the draw is officiated, not about what a rec book says. Rule 57(a) provides that if a player other than the one taking the draw *"moves offside, makes physical contact with an opponent, or encroaches on the face-off circle prior to the dropping of the puck, then the offending team's player taking the face-off shall be ejected from the face-off."* Rule 57(d) then lets the referee assess a minor for *"face-off violation"* on a team committing a second violation of (a) in the same face-off.
- **Fighting means the dressing room.** The instigator or aggressor gets a **match penalty** (Rule 59(a)), and a match penalty means *"ordered to the dressing room immediately for the balance of the game"* plus no further games until the case is dealt with by the Chairman (Rule 34(a)).
- **Two twenty-minute periods, not three** (Rule 84(a)), with a 10-minute intermission — though Section Seven item 21 hands period length back to *"the local committee or teams"* for league and exhibition games, which is exactly the kind of local variation the column is warning you about. The mercy rule is in the playing rules themselves, at **Rule 84(f)** — **running time** at a five-goal spread with 10 minutes left, with a minor stretched to three minutes, a major or match penalty to seven and a misconduct to twelve, to compensate — and Section Seven, which is the tournament section, repeats it at item 16.
- **A high stick that contacts an opponent above the shoulders is a major, and injury is irrelevant.** Rule 62(b): *“A player who accidentally or intentionally contacts an opponent above the normal height of the opponent’s shoulders with a high stick shall be assessed a Major penalty, whether or not injury results.”* ⚠️ **Rule 62 writes no minor rung for contact above the shoulders, and Rule 50 offers no route back down to one** — CARHA’s head-contact rule reaches contact made with *“any part of the player’s body or equipment other than a stick”*, and the Note opening it sends the stick case straight back: *“If a stick is used on contact this infraction falls under Rule 62 – High Sticks.”* The minor that Rule 62 does write, at 62(a), is a different act altogether — checking or intimidating an opponent *“while carrying their stick above the waist height of their opponent”*, which needs no contact at all. The Note to 62(b) adds a **game misconduct** where the high stick was intentional, or where an accidental one injures; 62(c) is a match penalty for deliberately attempting to injure or injuring. CARHA writes **no windup or follow-through carve-out** either — searched across the book by concept, its only *“wind up”* is the fifteen-inch figure in the slap-shot definition — where the NHL and IIHF books carry one. **So of the five books read for this document, CARHA sets the highest floor for a high stick: the major is where its rule starts, not a tier the referee reaches for.** That is CARHA-affiliated adult hockey and nothing else — it is not a rec-hockey standard and it is not evidence about your league.
- **The slap shot is banned outright** (Rule 79(a)) — a minor, or a major if an opponent is injured. The book defines it as bringing the stick back *"more than fifteen inches either on or off the ice."* ⚠️ **“Nothing like this in the other books” would be too strong, though — USA Hockey bans the slap shot too, in one discipline.** Its Disabled Hockey section provides that *“Slap Shots are prohibited in Blind Hockey”*, caps the stick during the act of shooting at *“parallel with their hip at maximum”*, and assesses a minor. **The NHL, IIHF and Hockey Canada books carry no slap-shot rule at all** — the phrase returns zero hits from each, counted on text flattened for hyphenated line breaks. So what is unusual about CARHA’s ban is that it reaches **all** of a league’s play, not that the rule exists. CARHA also writes a clause none of the other four has any counterpart to: **79(b)** makes a *fake* slap shot a minor where it is used *“for the purpose of intimidating an opposing player and/or goalkeeper”*.

The **ASHL** (Canlan's Adult Safe Hockey League) also publishes a rulebook, but it works as an overlay rather than a replacement: it states that *"the current rules issued by Hockey Canada (Canadian ASHL locations) and USA Hockey (USA-based ASHL locations) provides the foundation for game rules in the ASHL."*

So the column is verifiable in outline, and CARHA is a fair representative. What it is still not is authoritative for **your** league: neither of those books governs you unless your league says it does. Treat the column as a prompt to go and read your own league's rules sheet.

---

## Common Mistakes

These are rules misconceptions players genuinely hold, and what each one costs.

- **"I have to be behind the blue line."** No — **one skate** on or behind the plane keeps you onside, and the line is a foot wide. Coasting behind the line to be safe kills your entry. Drag a trail skate and enter at speed.
- **"High-sticking is a double minor."** That is the NHL's ceiling *for injury* (60.3). Everywhere else a high stick reaches a major and a game misconduct — IIHF 60.4, USA Hockey 621(b), Hockey Canada via 7.7(b) or 7.6(b), with the match tier routed the same way to 7.7(c) or 7.6(c) — and even the NHL has a match penalty at 60.4 where the injury was deliberate or attempted. ⚠️ **In one book read here the major is not a tier a high stick *reaches* — it is where the rule begins.** CARHA Hockey Rule 62(b) assesses a major for contact above the opponent’s shoulders *“whether or not injury results”*, accidental and intentional alike, and writes no minor rung above the shoulders; the game misconduct follows when it was intentional, or when an accidental one injures. **That binds CARHA-affiliated adult leagues and nobody else** — check your own book rather than carrying this across. Hockey Canada reads further still: its 9.5 preamble lets a penalty be assessed *"regardless of whether or not contact occurs"* — although 9.5(a), the clause that assesses one, requires contact, so the book does not settle whether the carry alone is punishable. Assume it is. ⚠️ **And "he was crouching, so it cannot be a high stick" is wrong in every book.** That waist floor is in the NHL book and the IIHF 2026/27 one and nowhere else; it is measured at **the struck player's** waist, not yours (IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13); and no book's 60.2 — the clause that actually imposes the minor — carries it at all.
- **"I never touched him, so it cannot be high-sticking."** ⚠️ **It can — and under USA Hockey the playing rule and the Casebook disagree about whether it can.** USA Hockey Rule 621's Note requires that a player carry the stick above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders *"and makes contact with the opponent"*, but its Casebook answers *"No."* to whether contact must occur at all, adds that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*, and requires the call *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions"* (Rule 621, Situation 2). The **IIHF 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** book — which the IHUK In-House Rules Introduction names, though their cover names the *current* IIHF book instead, so ask which edition your league is on — defines a high stick as one *"carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"*, with no contact element at all; the **2026/27** edition rewrote that clause into a *contact* test, so the answer there flips. And **Hockey Canada's 9.5** preamble says a high-sticking penalty *"may be assessed regardless of whether or not contact occurs"* — **though 9.5(a), the only clause that assesses one, requires contact, so that text does not settle it either.** **Keep the blade below your own shoulders in traffic and the question never arises.**
- **"My skate was in the air, so I was offside."** Under the **NHL**, no — if the **blade** has not broken the vertical plane of the leading edge, you are onside (NHL Rule 83.1, which defines a *"skate"* as *"the blade of the skate only"*). ⚠️ **The IIHF says that only in its 2026/27 book, which adopted the sentence.** The **2025/26 (Version 1.1)** book writes the same plane test but never defines *"skate"*, so a raised **boot** across the plane with the blade still behind it is unsettled there — a trail skate held wholly back over the neutral zone is onside under either edition, but do not reach a toe across under the older one. Under **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, yes** — Rule 630(a) requires **skate contact** with the neutral zone or the blue line, and Hockey Canada Rule 6.11 counts *"only the player's skate(s) that are in physical contact with the ice surface"*, so the airborne skate is offside in both. That is two books of four. Know which one you are in.
- **"During a delayed offside I just can't touch the puck."** Wrong, and this one costs possession every game. **Under the NHL and the IIHF** (Rule 83.3), forcing the defending puck carrier deeper into his own zone or being **about to make contact** with him also stops play. ⚠️ **Neither USA Hockey Rule 630(d) nor Hockey Canada Rule 6.11(b)(ii)** (the stop-play clause) **writes that contact trigger** — so under either of those books do not close on the carrier expecting a whistle to arrive before you do, because he is not braced for you. Retreat to the line and do not chase, whichever of the four you are under.
- **"We tagged up, so the goal counts."** Not if the puck was **shot in** and that same shot went in. **Clearing the zone afterwards has no bearing** (Rule 83.4) — and that is all four books. The NHL, the IIHF and Hockey Canada say so in their playing rules; **USA Hockey says it in its Casebook**, at Rule 630, Situation 41, which disallows the goal even where the attacking team has *"nullified the delayed offside by clearing the zone prior to the puck entering the goal."* Its *Playing Rules* alone do not settle it — Rule 630 writes no disallowed-goal provision and 617(b)(2) and 617(c)(8) point opposite ways — so it is the Casebook that closes it.
- **"Icing is a race to the dot."** It is not — Rule 81.1 says in terms that *"the determining factor is which player would first touch the puck, not which player would first reach the end zone face-off dots."* The race is to the **puck**; the dot is only where the linesperson's decision is locked in, and a race too close to call at that point is icing. Under **USA Hockey's automatic icing** there is no race at all.
- **"I got the puck over the red line with my skate, so no icing."** Under the **NHL and IIHF**, no — **"gaining the line" requires the puck to be on your stick** when it contacts the centre red line (Rule 81.1). **Under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada the question is put differently**, because neither book uses the phrase or draws the stick-versus-skate distinction: the test is the point of last contact with the puck relative to the red line (USA Hockey Rule 624 Note; Hockey Canada Rule 6.7, Interpretation 5). Do not argue either wording in the other book's game. Get the **puck** past the line and you are safe in all four.
- **"My blade snapped, so I can come off after our icing."** ⚠️ **Only maybe, and only under the IIHF book.** The NHL's substitution bar has three exceptions and a broken skate is not one of them (Rules 81.4, 82.1). The IIHF adds *"a Player with a broken skate"*, but **it adds it at Rule 81.4 only**: Rule 82.1 restates the same icing bar with the other three and without it, the rulebook does not say which governs, and no Situation Handbook answer names a broken skate. ⚠️ **One cuts against the exception without naming it, though:** Situation 81.7 answers that *"[o]nly time a line change is permitted is when a penalty is assessed which affects the on-ice strength of either team"* — over-broad, because that drops the injured player and the pulled goalkeeper 81.4 also permits, but not encouraging. **Under Hockey Canada it is not an exception either** — Rule 6.7(d)(i)'s three carve-outs are *"to replace a Goaltender that has been substituted for an extra attacker"*, *"to replace an injured player"*, and *"when a penalty or penalties have been assessed to either team"*, and that rule only applies in U18AAA, Junior and Senior at the Member's option in any case. So stay on, tell the official, and let them send you off if they will. **What you must not do is send a replacement over the boards on the assumption:** IIHF Rule 82.2(V) has the referee *"send the Players who have attempted to change back to their Players' bench"* and warn the coach that any subsequent violation *"shall result in a bench minor penalty for 'delaying the game'"*. **That warning is one per team per game and covers all three of the acts 82.2(V) names**, so if your bench has already spent it on something else the attempt is a bench minor immediately — in the last two minutes and in overtime included, because 82.2(VIII) suspends only points (vi) and (vii). **Under the NHL it may be a bench minor with no warning at all:** Rule 82.3's first paragraph gives one to the visiting team for *"any attempt… to make a change after the Referee's signal"*, which its own 82.2(v) contradicts.
- **"USA Hockey doesn't bar the change after an icing, so I can go over the boards whenever I like."** ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey the icing is not what stops you — the change window is, and it closes at every stoppage.** Rule 624 writes no substitution bar, so the icing itself costs you nothing; but **Rule 204(a)** gives the visiting team five seconds and the home team five and then shuts the window — *"Once the line change procedure has been completed, no additional player substitutions shall be permitted until the face-off has been completed legally and play has resumed"*, bar a penalty that changes on-ice strength. A player sent over after that meets two provisions, at two tiers, each of which warns first. **Rule 204(a)**'s own second paragraph: *"If there is any undue delay by either team in placing their lineup on the ice or changing players, the Referee shall warn the offending team. Any subsequent delay by the same team will result in a minor penalty for delay of game."* And **Rule 610(g)**: a **bench minor** *"after warning by the Referee"* to a team *"that causes any delay by making additional substitutions or attempts to delay the game in any manner."* USA Hockey's *Casebook* supplies the on-ice remedy, at Rule 613 Situation 15 — on a false face-off, either team attempting to change is ordered *"back to the players' bench"*, which is what **IIHF Rule 82.2(V)** does too. **So: you may change after your own icing under USA Hockey, and you may not change late. Late is the part that is penalised.**
- **"I'll knock the net off — we get a whistle and I get a change."** ⚠️ **You get the whistle and you do not get the change.** **NHL and IIHF Rule 63.8** put the draw in your own end, give the *other* team the choice of dot, and provide that *"[t]he offending team shall not be permitted to make any player substitutions prior to the face-off"* — on three exceptions, none of which is being tired: a goalkeeper who had come off for an extra attacker, an injured player, or a penalty that changes on-ice strength. If the referee reads the displacement as deliberate rather than accidental it is a **minor for delay of game** as well. **Hockey Canada writes the same bar at Rule 6.1(g), but only *"[i]n categories of U18 AAA, Junior and at the option of the Member in Senior"***, and **no counterpart was found in USA Hockey**, whose Rule 610(d) stops play and penalises a deliberate displacement without attaching any substitution consequence.
- **"I'll hop off the ice to kill the delayed offside."** That works — **into your own bench.** **NHL and IIHF Rule 83.3** clear you *"when both skates are off the ice and the Linesperson judges [you] to have left the playing surface"*, and both name *"his players' bench"*. ⚠️ **The wrong door is a bench minor in one book.** **USA Hockey Casebook Rule 630, Situation 12**: *"if the player jumped off the ice surface into any other part of the rink, and was substituted for, their team must be assessed a bench minor penalty for illegal substitution."* And whichever door you use, **your replacement is not free either** — if he comes on in the attacking zone while the arm is still up, he has to clear as well.
- **"A cross-check to the chest that rides up is a two-minute hockey play."** Not under Hockey Canada, whose sentence carries no division scope at all. **Rules 7.6(b) and 7.7(b) carry it word for word**: *"Any player who strikes an opponent above the normal height of their shoulders with a cross-check will be penalized with a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty, **whether or not injury results**"*, and its Interpretation 2 to 9.2(b) makes that *"must"*. It is not a catch-all for every stick that drifts high — Interpretation 2 to 7.6(b) says the rule is *"not intended to penalize players who make minimal, accidental contact with an opponent's head with their stick"* but those who *"make a deliberate cross-checking action and strike an opponent in the head or neck."* **But that takes away the major, not the penalty:** 7.6(a) still gives a **minor** for accidental head contact in minor and female hockey, and in those same divisions 4.8(b)(ii) ejects a player assessed *"three Minor or double Minor penalties"* for head contact in one game. Below the shoulders, Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to 9.2(a) makes the test the motion rather than the contact — *"If a pushing motion is used, a penalty may not be necessary. However, a striking motion must be penalized"* — and that test is Hockey Canada's alone. **Control their *stick* instead of their body — a stick lift taken below their bottom hand, lifted up rather than sideways.** And lift; do not chop: USA Hockey's Standard of Play singles out *"hard slashes to the upper portion of the stick (just below the hands) of an opponent, with no attempt to legally play the puck"* for strict enforcement.
- **"It was only stick-on-stick, so it cannot be hooking."** It can be, in two books of four — and the carve-out you are thinking of is narrower than you think even in the two that write it. **NHL 55.1 and IIHF 55.1 exempt stick-to-stick contact only *"when a player is checking another"***, so it is a carve-out for a check, not a general licence. **USA Hockey 623 and Hockey Canada 8.2 both name the opponent's *stick* as a hookable surface** — *"any part of an opponent's body or stick"* — and the NHL and IIHF both carry a further limb at Rule 56.1 under which a player without body position who uses the stick *"to impede or prevent his opponent from moving freely on the ice"* — the NHL's wording, the IIHF's the same but for its pronouns — gets a hooking penalty, with no tug at all. **"No tug, no hooking" is not safe in any of the four books. Stick on the puck.**
- **"I got the puck first, so it is not a trip."** **In the NHL and the IIHF that is false** — it saves you the penalty shot, not the two minutes. NHL 57.3 and IIHF 24.8 both say that *"if, in the opinion of the Referee"*, a player *"makes contact with the puck first and subsequently trips the opponent in so doing, no penalty shot will be awarded, **but a minor penalty for tripping shall be assessed**."* USA Hockey and Hockey Canada do give relief, on terms that are not the same claim as each other. **USA Hockey's turns on the action, not on the puck:** no penalty *"under this rule"* — Rule 639 only, so it shields nothing under the hooking or holding rules — where *"in the opinion of the Referee, the player was **clearly** hook-checking or poke-checking the puck **for the purpose of gaining possession**."* Reach the puck first by a skate, a body or a slide and that clause does nothing for you. **Hockey Canada's does turn on the puck:** its Interpretation 1 to 8.6(a) covers a player who *"gains possession of the puck and, with the same action, trips the puck carrier"*, and its Interpretation 2 — the falling or sliding player who reaches the puck first — carries **no referee-judgement qualifier at all**. ⚠️ **Neither book's relief reaches slew-footing** — and Hockey Canada's does not reach a clip either, where USA Hockey's can, because its Rule 639 covers clipping and tripping together. ⚠️ **And the relief that does run the NHL's and the IIHF's way is a different one, which those two books alone write:** 57.1 in each — *"Accidental trips which occur simultaneously with a completed play will not be penalized. Accidental trips occurring simultaneously with or after a stoppage of play will not be penalized"* — with **no equivalent in USA Hockey or Hockey Canada**. Neither pair of books, then, is simply the harsher one. **Beating the man to the puck is the defence; beating him to it and then taking his feet is not.**
- **"I can grab his stick as long as I let go."** Two books of four — and not the two North American amateur books. **How much North American rec and youth hockey runs on USA Hockey's book or Hockey Canada's is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it, so find out which book your league runs.** **NHL 54.2 and IIHF 54.2** do permit a player to defend against an opponent's stick and require them to *"immediately release"* it. **Hockey Canada 8.1 permits only *blocking*** — *"While players are permitted to block an opponent's stick with their hands, arms, or body, they are not permitted to hold an opponent's stick"* — and writes no release grace at all. **USA Hockey's Rule 622 lists *"grabbing the opponent's body, stick or jersey with one or both hands"*** with no impedance condition, and its Standard of Play tells officials these are *"to be called with very strict enforcement."* USA Hockey permits blocking an opponent's *body* — its Declaration of Player Safety: *"A skater may block an opponent so long as they are in front of the opponent and moving in the same direction"* — but the very next bullet limits it, *"so long as they do not use a hand or arm to hold or block the opponent"*, and the phrase *"fend off"*, which carries the permission in the NHL's and IIHF's Rule 56.1, appears nowhere in its **playing-rules volume**. ⚠️ **USA Hockey does write that permission — in its *Casebook*, not its playing rules.** Standard of Play, Situation 6 allows a player *"in control of the puck"* to use *"the free arm to fend off the opponent and maintain their body position"*, provided the position is one they *"legally established"*, and withdraws it the moment they *"grab the stick, sweater or arm of the opponent, or use the free arm to push off and create space."* ⚠️ **Read what it lets you fend off: the opponent, not their stick.** Rule 622's Note still names *"using a free hand to clutch, grab or hold the stick"* as
