holding, so the NHL's *"or his stick"* limb has no USA Hockey counterpart. **So: block it with your body and do not close your fingers on it — and under USA Hockey, do not reach for the stick at all.**
- **"Grabbing his cage is just two minutes."** Not in two books of four, and the two that do list it there leave the referee a route straight past it. **USA Hockey Rule 622(b)** — its *Holding an Opponent* rule, not its unsportsmanlike one — puts a **major plus a game misconduct** on a player who uses their hand *"to rub, grab or hold the facemask of an opponent"*, with a match penalty available at 622(c); and under Rule 404(b) a game misconduct also costs you *"the team's next game"*, automatically. **Hockey Canada** does not file it under unsportsmanlike conduct either — Rule 7.1, *Attempt to Injure*, starts at a **double minor** at minimal violence and reaches a match penalty. The NHL and IIHF do list it in Rule 75.2 among the minors, but neither caps it there: the NHL's escalation turns on *"if warranted"* and its Rule 21 reaches an attempt to injure with nobody hurt, and **the IIHF's own route — the British reader's — fires on the *degree of force* with no injury required at all**, into Rule 21 Dangerous Actions and a major plus an automatic game misconduct. **Never touch an opponent's cage, and assume you have no floor.**
- **"A punch with the glove on is just roughing — a mutual two."** In the NHL and the IIHF, roughing really is defined as *"a minor altercation that is not worthy of a major penalty to either participant"*. **But no book makes that your ceiling, and the IIHF book — the British one — does not.** IIHF Rule 46.5 gives *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"* to a player who punches *"an unsuspecting or 'unwilling combatant / opponent' (i.e., 'sucker punch') that could cause an injury"*, and IIHF 51.2 sends a player who *"persists in continuing to be involved in roughing"* to the fighting rule. **Under Hockey Canada a punch to the head, face or neck is not roughing at all**: Rule 7.9's preamble says *"if a player punches an opponent in the head, face or neck, then the appropriate penalty will be assessed under Rule 7.6 – Head Contact"* — mandatory major plus game misconduct if injury results, and a match penalty available under 7.6(c). Under USA Hockey, Rule 640(a) sends anything major-worthy to Rule 615 Fighting, whose Note counts *"a punch, or an attempted punch, thrown by any player in the direction of an opponent, regardless as to whether contact is made"*, and 615(a) is a **major plus a game misconduct**. Assume ejection, wherever you play. **And if you are the one who was hit, three of the four books write you a way out — USA Hockey's is the plainest wording of it, not the only one that reaches it:** Rule 615(a) says *"a player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule."* Retaliate and the same clause gives the referee a minor, a double minor or a major plus a game misconduct. **Hockey Canada's is discretionary, and sits in an Interpretation rather than in a lettered clause where you would look for it** — Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.10(a) and 7.10(b), under which a player who makes *"no attempt to retaliate, other than to protect or defend themselves"* leaves their opponent *"the only player to be assessed a Major penalty and Game Misconduct for Fighting"*, while they themselves *"may receive a Minor penalty for Roughing or perhaps no penalty at all."* **The IIHF's — yours in Britain — allows more, promises less, and moved between editions.** The **2025/26 (Version 1.1)** book gives it a rule of its own, **46.6 *Defender – Unwilling Combatant***, classing a player who *"defends"* themself with *"a few punches"* as an *"unwilling combatant"* — while assessing *"at least a minor penalty"* to one who retaliates *"if the criteria for 'fighting back' with a few punches is not fulfilled."* **The 2026/27 edition deletes that rule** — 46.6 there is *Helmets* — and repeats the relief instead inside **46.2, 46.8, 46.10 and 46.12** as a single sentence: a player who *"defends"* themselves against an aggressor or an instigator *"may be considered as an 'unwilling combatant' and the automatic game misconduct penalty may be waived at the discretion of the referee."* **That waives the ejection, not the five minutes.** **No such relief was found in the NHL book**, whose fighting rule uses *"unwilling combatant"* the other way round — at 46.2, to define the **aggressor** who keeps punching one — leaving 46.1's *"very wide latitude"* as the only route to it. *No NHL casebook was available to search here, so read that as not found rather than as absent.* So do not retaliate: turn away, get to an official or your bench, and let the referee sort it out — see [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md). **And if it landed on your head, come off and say so:** a suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, and a medical professional before you play again. [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md) carries the red flags that mean an ambulance instead, and the sources.
- **"Helmets come off in fights all the time — that is nothing."** ⚠️ **It is nothing in exactly one pair of books, and they are the pair you have watched.** NHL Rule 46.6 and IIHF Rule 46.12 — 46.6 in the 2026/27 edition — both close with *"[h]elmets that come off in the course of and resulting from the altercation will not result in a penalty to either player."* **USA Hockey writes the opposite outcome into Rule 615(c).** A **match penalty**, *"(for all age classifications)"*, for deliberately removing your own helmet/facemask *"prior to an altercation"* or an opponent's *"prior to or during"* one; a **game misconduct with an express two-game suspension** for a player *"whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"*; and a further game misconduct if yours comes off because you were not wearing it as designed — which its Casebook measures at Rule 615, Situation 19: *"If the space between the player's lower jaw and the chin strap allows for more than one finger to be inserted into that space, then the chin strap is not being properly worn."* **Hockey Canada ejects you too, through its equipment rule rather than its fighting one** — Rule 10.6(f), a **Gross Misconduct** for intentionally removing your own or an opponent's helmet before or during a fight, though its Interpretation 15 does excuse a genuinely incidental removal where nobody undid a chinstrap. ⚠️ **And under USA Hockey none of this needs a fight:** the Casebook's Note applying to Rule 615, Situations 14–20 applies the rule *"in any altercation where at least one player will receive a penalty."* So do your chin strap up properly, and keep your hands away from anybody's helmet — yours included. Full four-book comparison in [Body fouls](#body-fouls) and in [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md).
- **"A ten-minute misconduct puts us on the kill."** A misconduct **on its own** does not: the player is replaced immediately and the team plays full strength (**NHL Rule 22.3**, and **IIHF Rule 22.3** at the same number in both its 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions). The player pays; the team does not. ⚠️ **But that holds only while the misconduct stands alone, and the rule says so in the same sentence** — it applies *"unless he also receives a minor, major or match penalty in addition to the misconduct penalty"* (the IIHF wording is *"a minor or major"*, that book having no match penalty). A minor **plus** a misconduct is the common pairing, and then a substitute serves the minor and you are short-handed for those two minutes.
- **"The other team scored, so my major is over."** **Majors do not expire on a goal** (**NHL and IIHF Rule 20.2**, the same number and the same rule in both). Only minors and bench minors do, and only if the team is genuinely shorthanded and serving one.
- **"I only took two strides, so it cannot be charging."** ⚠️ **Wrong on the count in one book, and wrong on the reasoning in all four.** Hockey Canada 7.4(ii) makes charging *"two or more strides immediately prior to making contact"*, so two strides is already the offence there, where USA Hockey Rule 607 writes *"more than two strides"*. **But no book settles charging on the count.** The same USA Hockey sentence continues *"or travels an excessive distance to accelerate through a body check for the purpose of punishing the opponent"*; IIHF 42.1 says only *"multiple strides"*; and the NHL puts no number on it at all, judging a charge *"as a result of distance traveled"* (42.1). ⚠️ **And leaving your feet is a trigger on its own, with no approach needed** — NHL 42.1 reaches a player who *"skates, jumps into or charges an opponent in any manner"*, IIHF 42.1 one who *"jumps to check an opponent"*, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to 7.4(a) fixes the moment: feet off the ice *"prior to making body contact"*. ⚠️ **A minor is nobody's ceiling here.** USA Hockey 607(a) writes no bare minor at all — *"[a] minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed for charging an opponent"* — and under the IIHF book, which is the British reader's at every level, **42.4 reaches a major plus a game misconduct** where the referee judges that the Player *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'charging'"*, **with nobody hurt**. Count nothing; arrive under control or do not arrive.
- **"A hit to the head is two minutes."** In the NHL, yes — Rule 48.3 says *"There is no provision for a major penalty for this rule."* **Nowhere else.** IIHF Rule 48 covers the head **or neck**, states *"There is no clean check to the head or neck"*, names jumping to deliver the blow as a trigger, and gives **a major plus an automatic game misconduct** for reckless endangerment (48.3). USA Hockey Rule 620(a) and Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 are the same shape — and 620(b) makes the major compulsory not only for reckless endangerment but for merely careless head contact. **In Britain, and in North American youth and rec hockey, a hit to the head can end your game.** **And do not read that as protection you can skate on:** the same IIHF rule says a bodycheck on an opponent *"skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player"*, delivered without an upward motion, *"shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'."* The checker's obligation not to hit your head is unchanged, and the IIHF book puts no penalty on the player whose head was down. **Hockey Canada goes further, in junior and senior divisions only, and on a different trigger — ducking to avoid a check, not carrying the puck head down.** Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) provides that a player who *"ducks to avoid a check"* and is hit in the head area with *"an otherwise legal check"* draws no Head Contact penalty against the checker, and that the referee *"may assess a penalty to the player who ducks to avoid a check"* under Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 — Clipping — a Minor under Hockey Canada Rule 8.7(a), but **a mandatory major plus game misconduct under 8.7(b)** if the duck injures the checker — or at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence even if nobody is hurt — and **a mandatory match penalty under 8.7(c)** for an attempt to injure as well as a deliberate one. The prohibition itself is 8.7's preamble, *"Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked."* That Hockey Canada interpretation *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, and no equivalent exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules or in Hockey Canada's rules for minor and female hockey. ⚠️ **And the crouching prohibition is not only Hockey Canada's — the IIHF writes one too, in the British reader's own book.** IIHF Rule 44.1 says a player *"may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them"* — narrower than Hockey Canada's flat prohibition, because it wants the boards **and** the checker going over you, but real: IIHF 44.2 is a minor on the degree of violence and IIHF 44.3 reaches a major plus an **automatic game misconduct** for reckless endangerment. Neither the NHL nor USA Hockey writes any crouching clause at all. So keep your head up — **and your chin off your chest** — and note that under Hockey Canada, **in any division**, ducking out of the check is the version that can put *you* in the box as well, whatever penalty the checker takes, because Rule 8.7's prohibition on crouching carries no division scope; near the boards the IIHF book reaches it too. Head up — chin off your chest — is not standing tall into the hit. Take it on a wide base with the knees bent, and **near the wall get your skates parallel to it** and take the contact along your side — **your forearm and hip meet the wall, your shoulder and hip turn toward the checker** — never the point of your shoulder, never your back to the boards, and never ducking. Neither of those first two is penalised in any book; avoiding them is how you avoid a spinal injury. Ducking is both penalised and dangerous — penalised under Hockey Canada, whose Rule 8.7 prohibition on crouching to avoid a check **carries no division scope at all**, and under the IIHF near the boards. (What *is* confined to junior and senior is the **checker's** amnesty, Interpretation 3 to 7.7(a).) [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) covers how to take one.
- **"He turned at the last second, so the check from behind is on him."** It is not — but the reason is not that every book puts an onus on the checker, because **two of the four do not.** **USA Hockey Rule 608** states it in those words — *"The onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind"* — and **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a)** puts this exact question and gives the penalty to the checker, because *"The responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back"*; Hockey Canada's preamble to Rule 7.5 also names the player who *"turns and, as a result, creates contact with the back"* as the **first** situation referees are told to enforce strictly. **NHL Rule 43 and IIHF Rule 43 state no onus at all.** Where those two books do write one down it is in their boarding rules — **NHL Rule 41.1** and **IIHF Rule 41.1** — and there it is expressly balanced against *"whether the opponent put themself in a vulnerable position immediately prior to or simultaneously with the check"*, which is the very excuse being made here. (That is the IIHF's wording; the NHL's reads *"himself"*.) **Hockey Canada's boarding rule 7.2 carries the same balancing clause — but Hockey Canada sends this case back to the checking-from-behind rule anyway.** Its Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.5(a) says that *"if a player is cross-checked from behind into the boards or the goal, then Checking from Behind will be called"*, and Rule 7.5's own preamble is unqualified: *"In all circumstances the responsibility is on the player delivering the check to avoid contact to an opposing player's back."* The boarding balance does not rescue a hit from behind. **So the answer is not the onus; it is that the waiver is for a *deliberate* turn, which is a different thing from a late one.** **NHL Rule 43.1** waives the penalty only where a player *"intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back"*, and **IIHF Rule 43.1** only where a player *"intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position… to create a 'checking from behind' situation"* — adding, as the NHL does not, that *"other penalties may still be assessed."* Turning late because you did not see the hit is not turning deliberately to draw it, and **neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada writes even that waiver** — under Hockey Canada the responsibility is *"always"* the checker's. Hockey Canada does write **one** waiver, and it is not about turning: Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.5(a) says the rule is not meant to catch *"accidental contact as a result of playing the puck"* where it is *"marginal in nature and does not endanger the safety of the opponent"*, and that *"pinning a player to the boards at low speed and with minimal impact is permitted, even if contact is initiated from behind (except under Rule 7.3 – Body-checking, as applicable)."* **That parenthetical is the whole no-checking game:** Rule 7.3 covers U13 and below, all female hockey and any Member-approved division, and there 7.3(a) makes an intentional push or shove a minor — with 7.3(b) reaching a major plus a game misconduct on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt**, and making that same pair **mandatory** where the check injures. **Neither limb of Interpretation 3 is about turning** — a late turn is not accidental contact while playing the puck, and it is not a low-speed pin. And the cost is not two minutes: there is **no minor option** in the NHL or the IIHF (both at 43.2), Hockey Canada attaches a game misconduct to a minor *and* a major (Rule 7.5(e)), and only USA Hockey's minor-plus-misconduct (Rule 608(a)) leaves you in the game — **and its Casebook holds that tier to an open-ice push and a low-force pinch, requiring the major and game misconduct or the match penalty *"In every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"* and in every instance of reckless endangerment *"regardless of whether or not board contact is made"* (Rule 608, Situation 1).**
- **"Three majors before I'm thrown out."** That is the NHL's *residual* count (Rule 20.4) — **and both books can eject you on a first major long before you reach it — though not for the same fouls, and the NHL not unconditionally.** The genuine NHL/IIHF split is narrower than the count suggests: **IIHF 20.4 also ejects you for a second *misconduct*, which the NHL book does not**, and the IIHF's residual trigger is a second major where the NHL's is a third. **What both share is the limb that actually bites** — an automatic game misconduct *"whenever a [P]layer is assessed a major penalty for any of the infractions listed in the Reference Tables"* — where the IIHF's sentence stops, and the NHL's runs on to name *"Table 5 – Summary of Major Penalties"*. The IIHF's Table 6 — the 2025/26 book's number, merged into that edition's successor's single Table 5 — lists twenty, three of them fighting entries, so seventeen others — and applies to all of them unconditionally, among them checking from behind, clipping, illegal check to the head, high-sticking, cross-checking, slashing, hooking and slew-footing. **The NHL's Table 5 is not the same list and not unconditional** — it writes no major at all for high-sticking, slew-footing or an illegal check to the head — **all three reach a *match penalty* there instead (60.4, 52.2, 48.5), and 52.2's is mandatory and unconditional while 60.4 and 48.5 need an attempt to injure or a deliberate one** — and of its thirteen non-fighting rows six need an injury. So one major is enough for any of the IIHF's twenty, unconditionally — **and for five more under Table 7 — likewise a 2025/26 number, and likewise merged into that single Table 5 — each written as a single major-plus-game-misconduct act — boarding 41.4, charging 42.4, elbowing 45.4, interference 56.5 and tripping 57.4** — and for seven of the NHL's thirteen, with six more once an opponent is injured. **Assume the harsher reading.**
- **"I can't screen the goalie."** **A clean screen from outside the paint is legal**, and referees allow it constantly. What is illegal is impairing him **by contact**, **establishing a significant position inside the crease that both obstructs his vision and impairs his ability to defend the goal** (Rule 69.3 asks for all three of those, not any one of them) — and both of those grounds name the crease. **Rule 69.1's clause about positioning sits under *"Goals should be disallowed only if"***, a ceiling on when a goal may be taken off the board rather than a direction to take one off; the sentence in 69.1 that actually disallows one reaches an attacker who *"enters the goal crease"*, and 69.4, the rule for outside it, has no positioning limb at all. **Both books' own worked situations apply that:** NHL Reference Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Appendix IV Table 16 each answer *"Goal is allowed"* at situations 5C and 5E, where the screen is set outside the crease and does impair the goalie, and the two rows they disallow, 5B and 5D, both put the attacker inside it. ⚠️ **That is a reading of the rule's structure rather than something either book states in terms**, and both add that Rule 69 is *"enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s)."* Stand in his eyes; keep your feet out of the blue paint — **and off the red line around it, which is USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's requirement rather than the NHL's or the IIHF's.** The NHL and the IIHF allow the screen on the line at 5E above; what puts the line inside the crease is USA Hockey's Note to Rule 625(b), Hockey Canada's Glossary and IIHF Rule 1.7 — which leaves the IIHF book at odds with its own table. **Off the line holds under all four**, so play it that way. **That crease keying is NHL and IIHF only.** Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada reaches a screen set from **outside** the crease — USA Hockey writes no goaltender-vision provision at all, and Hockey Canada's two references to the goaltender's view both reach only a player standing in the crease. Inside it the two books answer differently: USA Hockey bars the goal on your position alone, whoever scored, while Hockey Canada lets a teammate's goal from outside the crease stand — but only if you stay completely passive in there, making no attempt to play the puck, interfere with the play or impede the goaltender's *"vision or movements"*.
- **"Any contact with the goalie is interference."** ⚠️ **Under the NHL and the IIHF only, and the permission is narrower than it is usually quoted.** In those two books, outside the crease **incidental contact is permitted only while the goalkeeper is in the act of playing the puck**, and then only if you made a reasonable effort to avoid it (Rule 69.4). A goaltender who has come out to challenge and is not playing the puck is not covered by that clause at all, and both books say a penalty *"should be assessed in every case"* of unnecessary contact. Contact **is** separately permitted, with no such condition, on **rebounds and loose pucks** either side of the crease line, where goalkeeper and attacker are going for the puck at the same time (Rule 69.7). ⚠️ **But 69.7 is a permission with limits, and they sit in the same rule.** It disallows the goal where a goalkeeper *"has been pushed into the net together with the puck by an attacking player after making a stop"* — all three of those conditions together — and adds that *"[i]f applicable, appropriate penalties will be assessed"*; it disallows the goal again where the puck is under a player in or around the crease and that player is pushed into the goal with it. **It runs your way once:** where a defending player pushed or fouled you into causing that push, *"the goal can be permitted."* ⚠️ **USA Hockey writes no rebound carve-out, and its version of the first permission is keyed to possession rather than to the crease line.** Rule 607(d) carries the "not fair game" protection for a goalkeeper outside the *privileged area*, and its Casebook at **Rule 607, Situation 5** allows that such a goalkeeper *"can be legally checked"* while permitting an attacker to *"physically engage with the goalkeeper in an effort to gain possession of the puck"* only *"When the goalkeeper has possession of the puck"* — adding that once they are *"no longer in control of the puck, any avoidable contact (meaning the attacking player has sufficient time to avoid the contact) must also be penalized under this rule."* ⚠️ **Hockey Canada writes something like the first and nothing like the second, so be exact about what its clause does.** Rule 8.5 says that *"While incidental contact with the goaltender may occur, attacking players must make an effort to avoid contact in all circumstances"*, and hangs the penalty on the effort: *"The onus is always on the attacking player and players who do not make an effort to avoid the goaltender must be penalized."* **That is the penalty question, not the goal question, and it is not a rebound carve-out.** On whether the goal counts, Hockey Canada runs the other way: its **Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.5(a)** reads *"Any goal scored on a play where an attacking player initiates contact with the goaltender will be disallowed, regardless of whether the contact occurs inside or outside of the goal crease."* — binding **the attacking player**, not the goaltender, with its only stated exception an attacker who is fouled by a defending player and as a result cannot avoid the contact. **Under USA Hockey the permission runs while the goalkeeper has the puck and stops when they lose it, and there is no rebound carve-out to fall back on. Under Hockey Canada, making an effort to avoid the goaltender is what keeps you out of the box; it does not keep the goal on the board.** ⚠️ **And under Hockey Canada the cost is not capped at two minutes:** Rule 8.5(b) reaches a major plus game misconduct three ways — at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of impact"*, **with nobody hurt**, then **mandatorily** for a player who *"charges the goaltender"* and **mandatorily** for any interference-with-goaltender foul that injures an opponent — and 8.5(c) reaches a match penalty for attempting to injure or deliberately injuring a goaltender by interference.
- **"Our goalie can go get it as long as a defenceman is coming."** There is **no teammate-based exception** to the trapezoid. The **only** exception is the goalie **maintaining skate contact with his crease** (NHL Rule 27.8; IIHF Rule 27.7).
- **"The puck was still on our side of the red line, so our goalie was allowed to play it."** ⚠️ **True in two books and a two-minute minor in the other two, and this one catches goalies out every season.** Under the **NHL and the IIHF** the rule says the position of the **puck** decides (NHL 27.7; IIHF 27.6), so it is legal. Under **USA Hockey** and **Hockey Canada** the position of the **goalkeeper's skates** decides — a goalie whose skates are over the line playing a puck on his own side is penalised (USA Hockey *Casebook* Rule 407 Situation 13; Hockey Canada Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.13(c)). **How much North American rec and youth hockey runs on USA Hockey's book or Hockey Canada's is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it, so find out which book your league runs.** **Keep the skates behind the line and you are legal everywhere.**
- **"Closing my hand on the puck at the draw is a faceoff violation."** Under the NHL and IIHF it is a **minor penalty** (Rule 67.2) — **and those two books escalate in the crease as well**, though they print it differently: the NHL hangs a Note off 67.2(ii) reading *"When this is done in his team's goal crease area, a penalty shot shall be assessed (67.4) or a goal awarded (67.5)"*, while the IIHF writes the escalation into the body of 67.2(II) itself — *"When this is done in their team's goal crease area, a 'Penalty Shot' shall be assessed"*, pointing from there at its own **67.4 – Penalty Shot** and **67.5 – Goal Awarded**. Both books reach both outcomes; only the typography differs. **Under USA Hockey it is neither, outside the crease** — Rule 618(a) makes a closed hand that is not immediately dropped a stoppage and a last-play faceoff, and attaches the minor to *picking the puck up off the ice* instead. **In the crease that same 618(a) makes it a penalty shot or optional minor**, and an awarded goal on an empty net — ⚠️ **though USA Hockey alone conditions that awarded goal**, 614(b) requiring that the act *"has prevented an obvious and imminent goal"* where the other three books ask only that the goalkeeper had been replaced. **Hockey Canada is a fourth answer again:** Rule 10.2(a)(iii) makes the closed hand a minor only where the player *"by doing so, gains an advantage"*, and expressly excludes *"a player who closes their hand on the puck to catch or stop the puck and immediately drops it to the ice"*. **It escalates in the crease too**, at 10.2(a)(v) — a penalty shot for that same closed hand *"when the infraction occurs inside the offending team's goal crease"*, and an awarded goal under 10.2(a)(vi) with the net empty. **All four books get harsher near your own net; only the ordinary-ice answer differs.** Either way it is not a faceoff violation. Batting the puck with the hand to win the draw is a **minor for delay of game — faceoff violation**. Neither is a simple "do it again". ⚠️ **And do not reach for the permission at the top of Rule 67.2 to get out of this:** you may normally catch a puck out of the air and knock it straight down, but under the NHL and IIHF the note to 67.2 — printed again at Rule 76.5 — takes that away from the two takers *"until such time as a third player (from either team) has at least touched the puck."* At the dot, your hand is simply out of it.
- **"Only the centre can cause a faceoff violation."** The opposite is the common case: **a winger encroaching gets the centre ejected from the draw** (Rule 76.6 — 76.7 defines the violation and the second-violation bench minor). A **second** violation by the same team on the same draw is a **bench minor** — **under USA Hockey Rule 613(d) it is instead a minor on the individual who committed it**, not a bench minor. ⚠️ **The IIHF used to be the exception to that and no longer is** — its **2025/26** book warned the team and ejected nobody, but its **2026/27** book, published June 2026, has the draw-taker *"ejected from the face-off circle"* in the NHL's own words (IIHF 2026/27 Rule 76.6). What is still IIHF-only is the skate win: legal in the NHL, a violation under both IIHF editions (Rule 76.3(VI)).
- **"Every puck over the glass is a penalty."** **Under USA Hockey it is not strict liability — but a deliberate clear still is a minor, from anywhere on the ice and expressly *"including after a stoppage of play"* (610(c)); only an accidental one escapes, with a last-play faceoff (631(d)).** ⚠️ **And after a stoppage the minor is not USA Hockey's ceiling** — Rule 601(c)(10) reaches a **misconduct** for the same act, 601(d)(7) and 601(d)(9) a **game misconduct**, and the Note to 601(d) a **match penalty**; see the next bullet. **Hockey Canada writes two limbs, and the first is not confined to skaters:** 10.1(ii) reaches *"[a] player who deliberately shoots, bats, or throws the puck outside the playing surface"*, from anywhere — and **"player" there textually includes the goaltender**, because Hockey Canada's Glossary defines *"PLAYERS"* as *"[t]he members of the team physically participating in the game. The goaltender is considered a player, except where explicitly stated because special rules or privileges apply to them."* Rule 10.1 knows how to name a goaltender expressly — it does so at 10.1(v), at 10.1(vi) and in its unnumbered Players' Bench paragraph — and **it does not at 10.1(ii)**. **The book then points at that limb from elsewhere in exactly those terms.** Its face-off-location rule, **6.3(e)(i) Note 1**, reads *"Notwithstanding the previous, Referees must penalize **any player of either team** who deliberately shoots, bats, or throws the puck outside the playing surface, as per Rule 10.1 (ii) – Delay of Game"* — no goaltender exclusion, one rule away from **6.4(a)**, which does carry one for a materially similar act. Hockey Canada writes an express *"excluding the goaltender"* five times — at 2.4(b) Note 2, at 6.2 (in the plural), at 6.4(a), in the hybrid-icing rule 6.7(e)(i) and at 10.2 — and at 10.1(ii) it does not. ⚠️ **That is still a reading of the rule's words rather than something the book rules on: none of the twelve Interpretations to 10.1(a) addresses whether 10.1(ii) reaches a goaltender.** **10.1(v) then names a goaltender who shoots or bats it *"directly out of the playing surface, with their stick"*, and writes no deliberateness word** where 10.1(ii) does. **The gap between the two limbs is the glove:** 10.1(v) is confined to the stick, and **10.1(ii) is the only limb in Rule 10.1 reaching a puck *thrown out of the playing surface*** — 10.1(vi) also says *"throws"*, but of a goaltender who puts the puck *"onto the top or back of the goal netting"*, which is a different act. So a goaltender who **deliberately** throws the puck over the glass is reached by 10.1(ii) rather than by 10.1(v), while an accidental glove clearance, or a deflection, is reached by neither. ⚠️ **But whether the missing word makes (v) strict liability is a reading of the rule's structure, not something the book states:** Rule 10.1's preamble reaches only a player or team who *"deliberately causes a stoppage of play or delays the restart of play"*, 10.1(a) hangs the minor on *"deliberately delaying the game"*, and none of the twelve Interpretations to Rule 10.1(a) settles it. **Interpretation 5 is the one written to this limb, and it does carve out two cases in the goaltender's favour** — no penalty where there is no glass, *"or if the puck touches the glass or deflects off a player or official"*, and none at all unless *"both ends of the rink"* are similar. **Neither escape is about intent**, so assume the harsher reading and ask your league. Under the NHL and IIHF the strict-liability version applies only from **your own defending zone**, only if **direct and non-deflected**, and not **into the bench**, not **directly off a faceoff**, and not where there is no glass (Rule 63.2(iii)). ⚠️ **The bench one is much narrower than it sounds, though.** Under the IIHF Situation Handbook only a puck *"shot **directly** into the Players' Bench (where there is no glass)"* escapes: a clear that goes **over** the glass and merely *lands* in the bench is still a minor, because what is judged is *"where the puck leaves the playing surface"* (Situation 63.24, 2025/26 and 2026/27 alike). Aiming at the bench under pressure is aiming at the penalty. ⚠️ **Under the IIHF, do not count a skate as outside that limb** — Rule 63.2(III) names only *"their hand or stick"*, but the IIHF Situation Handbook answers *"Yes."* at Situation 63.19 to a defender who kicks at the puck to block a shot and puts it over the glass — in the 2025/26 book's Version 1.1 (August 2025), reversing the *"No"* of its own Version 1.0, and again in the 2026/27 book (Version 1.0, June 2026). The rule book outranks the Handbook by the Handbook's own Foreword, so the words stand — but the officiating document has moved against you. But note Rule 63.2(ii): a player who **deliberately** shoots or bats the puck out of the playing area *"from anywhere on the ice surface"* takes the same minor. Outside your own end you are safe only if it was an accident.
- **"The whistle has gone, so I can just fire it into the corner."** ⚠️ **Two minutes in the NHL and the IIHF, and the bottom rung of a ladder in the two amateur books.** NHL and IIHF Rule 63.2(ii) reach a player who deliberately shoots or bats the puck out of the playing area *"during the play or after a stoppage of play"*, and NHL Rule 63.9 scopes delay of game to *"a penalty (minor, bench minor, penalty shot or awarded goal)"* — no misconduct rung in either book. **USA Hockey has one.** A **misconduct** for a player who *"[s]hoots or bats the puck outside of the playing area during a stoppage of play"* (601(c)(10)); a **game misconduct** if it was *"done in protest of an official's decision"* (601(d)(7)) or if it went *"in the general direction of an on-ice official, or in the direction of a spectator"* (601(d)(9)); and a **match penalty** in the Note to 601(d) for a puck shot *"directly at an official or spectator in a manner that recklessly endangers"* them — with 610(c)'s plain minor also on the page and **the book never saying which of the two governs**, and no Casebook situation found that decides it. **Hockey Canada writes the act out in full**, not in its delay-of-game rule but at **Rule 11.1, preamble item (v)** — *"Shooting the puck out of the playing area, after the whistle"* — a Minor or Bench Minor under 11.1(a), a **Misconduct** under 11.1(d)(i) for persisting, because 10.1(d) forbids a misconduct for delay of game. ⚠️ **And 11.1(d)'s own Note 1 lets a referee go straight to that Misconduct** — *"not required to assess a Minor penalty under this rule before assessing a Misconduct penalty"* — so there is no free one first. **Where the puck ends up is only half of it. What it goes near is the other half, and that half is where the ejections are.**
- **"The referee blows the whistle as soon as the offending team touches the puck on a delayed penalty."** The condition is **control**, not touch (**NHL and IIHF Rule 15.1**, which both say the whistle comes *"when the team to be penalized gains control of the puck"*). A deflection off their skate does not end it.
- **"We're on a delayed penalty, so let's shoot."** A missed shot they recover ends your **six-on-five** and downgrades you to a **five-on-four**. Keep the puck. Regroup if you must.
- **"We're killing, we can't ice it."** You can, whenever you are **below** their numerical strength (Rule 81.6) — **but not unconditionally, because two of the books carve the exemption back.** Under **USA Hockey** the nullification is prefixed *"(For all Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult classifications only)"* (Rule 624(b)(1)), so below those levels a shorthanded team cannot ice the puck at all — the clear comes straight back for a defensive-zone draw. Under the **NHL**, Rule 81.6's third paragraph withdraws it from a team shorthanded by a **major** that has *"neglected to ensure there is a player on the penalty bench to exit upon the expiry of the penalty"*: they *"are not permitted to ice the puck. Icing will be called."* **The IIHF book carries neither carve-out**, so for a British reader the exemption reads as unconditional. Not using it where you have it is the most common penalty-kill error at rec level.
- **"My stick broke, so I'll just play it out."** A minor. **Rule 10.3** lets you keep playing *without* a stick but not *with* a broken one — *"a player whose stick is broken may participate in the game provided he drops the broken stick."* Open your hand the moment it goes. 🇬🇧 **And in Britain do not carry it to the bench instead** — the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 add a Rule 10.3, new this season, under which *"[s]kating with a broken stick anywhere on the ice is not permitted"* and a player whose stick breaks *"must drop their stick immediately"*, on pain of *"a 2min penalty for illegal equipment"*, with no allowance for being near your bench or out of the play. **Hockey Canada Rule 3.2(a) is the same flat drop.** USA Hockey is the one book here that lets you carry it, and only if you are in the immediate vicinity of the bench, completely out of the play, and go at once. And do not take a teammate's by having it thrown or slid to you on the ice; it must be handed over, or picked up off the ice (NHL and IIHF only — **USA Hockey Rule 605(b)** allows a replacement only from your bench or from a teammate on the ice). **Your goalie is the exception**: under NHL Rule 10.4 — and IIHF Rule 10.4, which carries both clauses in the same words — they may carry on with a broken stick until a stoppage, and must be handed a replacement on the ice rather than fetching one from the bench. **That permission is the NHL's and the IIHF's alone.** It is not in the USA Hockey book — Rule 605(c) covers only where the stick comes from, and 605(a) is written to cover *"a player or goalkeeper"* alike — and **Hockey Canada bars it in terms**, at Rule 3.2(c): *"A goaltender may not continue to play with a broken stick of any kind."* Under either book that is a minor, so do not assume a goalie may keep playing with a broken one.
- **"I'll just tell the ref what I think."** Disputing a call **from the penalty box after play has resumed is an automatic misconduct** (**NHL Rule 75.4(iv)**; **IIHF 75.4(IV)** is the same sentence). Only the captain and alternates have standing at all.
- **"The rules are the rules."** They are not. Trapezoid, icing type, body checking, tagging up, overtime, penalty length, **the penalty for a high stick** and **the penalty for a hit to the head** all vary between the NHL, the IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and your league. Read your league's sheet before your first game.

## Check yourself

*Answer each question before you go on — a real attempt, not a guess. Then go
and check, because the checking is the part that does the work. Producing an
answer and getting it wrong is worth more than recognising a right one, but only
when the right answer follows: the section named with each question, and the Key
Takeaways after them, are where you get it. Where the answer lives in another
document, that is named too.*

1. A hit catches an opponent's head. What is the ceiling on that penalty in the
   NHL, what does the IIHF book reach instead and on what trigger — and which of
   the two should a British player learn it from? *(§Body fouls, and §10)*

2. You have already taken one major tonight. Under which book might that first one
   have ended your game already, under which does the next one end it, and what does
   the NHL book wait for? And what do two ten-minute
   misconducts do under each of them? *(§Penalties — Major and Misconduct, and
   §10)*

3. You are carrying through the neutral zone and want to fire it in. Under the NHL
   and IIHF books, what exactly has to reach the centre red line, and on what?
   What do the USA Hockey and Hockey Canada books ask instead? *(§"Gaining the
   line")*

4. Your trail skate is in the air over the neutral zone at the instant the puck
   crosses the blue line. In how many of the four books are you onside, and what
   do the others require you to do differently? *(§2. Offside — Rule-set
   differences)*

5. The linesperson's arm is up for a delayed offside and you are still in the
   zone. Under the **NHL and IIHF** books, name two things you can do **without
   touching the puck** that bring the whistle anyway — and say which of them neither
   the USA Hockey nor the Hockey Canada book writes.
   *(§Delayed offside and tagging up)*

6. Your winger leans over the hash marks before the drop. What does that cost your
   team under the NHL book, and what does it cost instead under the IIHF book?
   And which routine North American way of winning a draw is itself a violation
   there? *(§8. Faceoffs — the two things you must not leave without)*

7. Your goalie wants to go for a puck outside the trapezoid because a defenceman
   is on the way. What is the one exception the rule actually carries?
   *(§The trapezoid, and §Common Mistakes)*

8. Your stick snaps in the corner with the puck at your feet. What must you do in
   that same instant, what may you legally keep doing for the rest of the shift,
   and how may a replacement reach you? And if you play in Britain, which book
   forbids you to carry the broken one to the bench even when you are out of the
   play? *(§Broken sticks)*

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **One skate keeps you onside, and the blue line is a foot wide** — so drag a trail skate and enter at speed. Keep the blade **in contact** under **USA Hockey Rule 630(a)** and **Hockey Canada Rule 6.11**, where a raised skate is offside; it is onside only under the NHL and IIHF. That is two books of four. On a *delayed* offside every attacker clears with a skate genuinely touching the line, and the zone must be empty before anyone re-enters — all four books require that, three of them wording it *"at the same instant"* and Hockey Canada (6.11(b)(ii), the tag-up clause) as *"completely clear of attacking players"* — and **touching the puck or going for a loose puck ends the play in all four books** (Hockey Canada 6.11(b)(ii), the stop-play clause, adding that the puck must still be in the attacking zone). Forcing the defenceman deeper is **NHL and IIHF**, with **USA Hockey Rule 630(d)** writing a wider *pressure* limb in its place and **Hockey Canada Rule 6.11(b)(ii)** (the stop-play clause) writing neither; being **about to hit him** is **NHL and IIHF only**. ⚠️ **Neither North American book writes that contact trigger, so do not close on the carrier expecting a whistle to arrive before you do** — he is still playing the puck and he is not braced for you. ⚠️ **And if you are the defending player, keep your head up and expect to be hit in all four books:** the two NHL and IIHF triggers both name the defending **puck carrier**, as does USA Hockey's pressure limb, so every one of them lapses the instant the puck leaves your stick. **Tag-up is not universal:** **USA Hockey Rule 630(d)** grants it only at Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult — below those classifications the whistle is immediate. **Hockey Canada writes no such limit — 6.11 is unscoped**, where the same book scopes its icing rule (6.7(e)) and its body-checking rule (7.3) by category and by division in terms, so a delayed offside tags up the same way in every Hockey Canada division.

2. **Under hybrid icing the race is to the puck, not the dot** — the dot is only the deadline for the linesperson's decision, and a race too close to call at that instant is icing. "Gaining the line" — the puck **on your stick** touching the centre red line — is **NHL and IIHF only** (Rule 81.1); USA Hockey (Rule 624 Note) and Hockey Canada (Rule 6.7, Interpretation 5) judge the point of last contact with the puck instead. Getting the **puck itself** past the line works in all four. **And hybrid is the minority answer among the books:** USA Hockey is automatic in every standing classification (Rule 624), and Hockey Canada is automatic outside U18AAA, Junior and Senior-at-the-Member's-option (Rule 6.7(e)). Check yours.

3. **An icing costs you three things: a defensive-zone draw, no line change and no timeout.** That is the **NHL and the IIHF alike** (IIHF Rules 81.2, 81.4, 87.1 in the NHL's own words, bar a single added exception at 81.4 for *"a Player with a broken skate"* — which IIHF Rule 82.1's restatement of the same bar does not carry, so do not count on leaving the ice on it — and a replacement sent over on it is sent back with a warning to your coach under Rule 82.2(V), or a bench minor if that once-a-game warning is already spent), so it is the version a British player should assume. **USA Hockey imposes neither of the last two** — Rule 624 restricts no substitution, Rule 204 permits a change at every stoppage, Rule 636(f) puts no icing condition on time-outs. ⚠️ **That is not the same as a free change, and the difference is worth knowing before you send someone over.** USA Hockey closes the change window at every stoppage under Rule 204(a) and penalises a team that ignores it at two tiers, each after a warning from the referee: a **minor** for delay of game under Rule 204(a)'s own second paragraph, and a **bench minor** under Rule 610(g) against a team that *"causes any delay by making additional substitutions."* Its *Casebook*, at Rule 613 Situation 15, orders players attempting to change after a false face-off *"back to the players' bench"* — the same remedy as IIHF Rule 82.2(V). **Hockey Canada splits them, and only in three categories:** Rule 6.7(d) bars the line change in **U18AAA, Junior and Senior, at the Member's option** — not below U18AAA — with carve-outs for replacing a goaltender who was pulled for an extra attacker, replacing an injured player, and any stoppage where a penalty was assessed. **Senior is at the Member's option, so if you play adult Hockey Canada hockey, assume you may not change until somebody tells you otherwise.** In those same three categories 6.7(d)(iii) expressly *permits* the timeout: *"Either team is permitted to take their time-out … following an icing, but during that time out, the team in violation of icing will not be permitted to make player substitutions."* Hockey Canada's timeout is **30 seconds**, not USA Hockey's 60 — though Rule 6.17's preamble lets Members and leagues set their own time-of-game rules — and below those categories it is not automatic: Rule 6.17(e) Note 3 gives it *"in minor and female hockey stop-time games wherever the local Member gives approval"*, and makes it mandatory at Hockey Canada minor and female regional and national championships. The one relief is that a shorthanded team may ice freely — **but not in every book:** USA Hockey grants it only at Youth 15-Only and above, Girls 16U and above, High School and Adult (Rule 624(b)(1)), and the NHL withdraws it from a team shorthanded by a major that has left nobody on the penalty bench (Rule 81.6). ⚠️ **And an icing is not the only way to lose the change.** Under **NHL and IIHF Rule 63.8**, a **defending skater or goalkeeper** who **accidentally knocks his own net off** hands his team the identical package — the draw in his own end, the other team choosing the dot, **no timeout** (Rule 87.1), and no substitution before the face-off on three exceptions only — a pulled goalkeeper, an injured player, a strength-changing penalty. Being tired is not one of them. **Hockey Canada writes that bar at Rule 6.1(g) in the same three categories as its icing bar** and nowhere else, and **no counterpart was found in USA Hockey**, whose Rule 610(d) stops play and penalises a deliberate displacement without restricting substitution. So the whistle you bought is a whistle and nothing more.

4. **Minors end early when the other team scores; majors never do** (Rule 20.2), a misconduct on its
   own never leaves your team shorthanded (Rule 22.3), **and in the NHL's book and the IIHF's alike, a first
   major can end your game.** The IIHF's Reference Table 6 lists twenty infractions — three of them fighting entries, so
   seventeen others, against the NHL's nineteen rows rather than its thirteen non-fighting ones —
   and requires **no injury** for any of the seventeen — checking from behind, cross-checking, slashing, hooking, high-sticking,
   slew-footing and an illegal check to the head among them — though calling the major at all
   remains the referee's judgement. Table 7 adds five more, each written as a single
   major-plus-game-misconduct act; its heading rests that on the referee's judgement, so it is not
   beyond doubt. **Play as though the ejection follows the major.** ⚠️ **Those two IIHF numbers are
   the 2025/26 book's** — the 2026/27 edition merges its Tables 5, 6 and 7 into a single **Table 5**
   that still carries every entry named here, so do not confuse it with the NHL's Table 5 next.
   The NHL's Table 5 is neither the
   same list nor unconditional — **and the book disagrees with itself about it**: 20.4's second
   sentence attaches no condition to a Table 5 major, while the individual rules make seven of the
   thirteen non-fighting rows automatic and six injury-conditional. **Assume the harsher reading.**
   The NHL also writes no major at all for high-sticking, slew-footing or an illegal check to
   the head — **all three are a match penalty there instead (60.4, 52.2, 48.5).** But only
   **52.2's is mandatory and unconditional**: 60.4 requires that a player *"attempts to or
   deliberately injures"* and is mandatory once met; 48.5 has the same trigger but is **discretionary** —
   *"may assess"* against 60.4's *"shall assess"* — and reads it in the past tense, *"the player attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent"*, so for a merely reckless illegal check to the head the NHL offers a minor
   and nothing above it **on the ice** — 48.5 itself adds that *"supplementary discipline can be
   applied by the Commissioner at his discretion."* The NHL's residual trigger is a **third** major (Rule 20.4) where the IIHF's is a **second** — or a
   second misconduct (IIHF 20.4 and 22.1), which the NHL book does not have.

5. **A hit to the head is a minor in the NHL and can end your game in every other book — learn it
   from the harshest one, not the most watched.** NHL 48.3 says *"There is no provision for a major
   penalty for this rule."* IIHF Rule 48 covers the head **or neck**, opens *"There is no clean
   check to the head or neck"* and reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless
   endangerment; USA Hockey 620(a) allows a minor plus misconduct *or* a major plus game misconduct, and 620(b) makes the harsher one compulsory for reckless endangerment **or for contact that is merely careless**, with 620(c) reaching a match penalty; Hockey Canada 7.6
   opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck."* If you play in Britain,
   the lenient version is not yours. ⚠️ **And none of it gives you unconditional protection.**
   IIHF 48.1 declines to penalise a checker for *an illegal check to the head* — **that one
   infraction only** — where the opponent was *"skating with the puck with their head down in the
   direction of the Player"* and the checker used no upward motion. **The checker is still obliged
   not to hit your head** — Rule 48.1 also states that *"The Player delivering the hit must avoid hitting the
   opponent's head or neck"* — and how that carve-out sits against the rule's own triggers is not
   resolved in the published text. **So keep your head up and your chin off your chest whenever you
   carry the puck** — that is the whole of what this half of Rule 48 asks of you. Carrying the puck
   head down is **not** the same as ducking a check, which is the next takeaway.

6. **Never duck or crouch to avoid a check — in two books the player who ducks can be penalised too.** Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) — **Junior and Senior divisions only** — gives the checker no head-contact
   penalty when a player *"ducks to avoid a check"* and is hit in the head area *"with an otherwise
   legal check"*, and lets the referee penalise the ducker under
   Rule 8.7 — Clipping — where 8.7(a) is a minor, 8.7(b) is a major plus game misconduct at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence and a **mandatory** one if the duck
   injures the checker, and 8.7(c) makes a match penalty mandatory for an attempt to injure or a deliberate one. ⚠️ **Only the *checker's* amnesty is junior and senior: Rule 8.7's
   ban on crouching to avoid a bodycheck carries no division scope at all, and reaches minor and
   female hockey too.** The interpretation also *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable
   position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass,
   etc.)"* — so a head that drops while you shoot or take a pass is not a duck, and the checker
   keeps no amnesty for hitting it. ⚠️ **The prohibition is in the British reader's own book too** — IIHF
   Rule 44.1 writes its own, and it is narrower — a player *"may not crouch down near the boards to
   avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them"* — 44.2 is a minor on the degree of violence and 44.3 a major plus an automatic game misconduct, at the referee's discretion, for reckless endangerment. **Never duck. The reason is anatomy rather than a rule, so it holds whether or not your book penalises the duck.** USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* programme guide sets out the mechanism: when the head is flexed, chin toward the chest, the upper spine's natural curve is removed and *"the cervical spine becomes straight"*, so a head-on collision with the boards or a goal post stops the head while the body keeps moving, *"compressing the spine"* hard enough to fracture a vertebra. **And head up — chin off your chest — does not mean standing tall into the hit.** Wide base,
   knees bent; near the wall get your skates parallel to it and take the contact along your side, with
   **your forearm and hip meeting the wall**, never the point of your shoulder and **never your back to the boards**. See [Body Contact and Battles](../technique/body_contact_and_battles.md) for the technique itself.

7. **Checking from behind has no small version. Three of the four books eject you at every tier they write, and the fourth ejects you too for a forceful check along the boards or a reckless one anywhere.** That is a check on a player who is unaware and unable to protect himself, with contact on the back of the body. **NHL Rule 43.2** — *"There is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"* — and **IIHF 43.2** — *"there is no option to award a minor penalty"* — leave only a major plus an automatic game misconduct. **Hockey Canada Rule 7.5(a)** starts at *"a Minor penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty"*, and 7.5(e) makes the game misconduct compulsory for a minor and a major alike. **USA Hockey's 608(a) is the only tier of the four that stops short of an ejection** — a minor plus a misconduct, and that misconduct is not a game misconduct — but 608(b) makes the major and game misconduct compulsory where the check *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame, as a result of checking from behind"*, and its *Casebook* (Rule 608, Situation 1) holds the lighter tier to an open-ice push and a low-force *"'pinch' against the boards"* while requiring the harsher one *"In every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"* and wherever the check recklessly endangers, *"regardless of whether or not board contact is made."* **So the books differ in the tier they start at, not in what happens when you finish a check into somebody's back.** **Two of the four books put the onus on the checker in terms** — USA Hockey Rule 608 (*"the onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind"*) and Hockey Canada Rule 7.5's *"in all circumstances"* — while **NHL and IIHF Rule 43 state no onus at all.** It makes no difference to the excuse: "he turned at the last second" is not a defence in any of them, because the only turn the NHL and IIHF waive is a deliberate one. **If you play in Britain the lenient version is not yours:** the In-House Rules carry no amendment to Rule 43, so the IIHF version binds and there is no minor to hope for.

8. **Almost every rec-league penalty is a stick foul, and almost every stick foul is a conditioning failure** — a reading of the rulebooks against how adult recreational hockey is officiated, not a published count of calls. **But two actions are not two-minute fouls under every book, and under Hockey Canada neither of them is a two-minute foul at all.** A **deliberate cross-check above the shoulders** is a mandatory major plus a game misconduct there — Rules 7.6(b) and 7.7(b) carry the same sentence word for word, *"whether or not injury results"*, and between them cover the divisions Hockey Canada distinguishes — so under that book there is no minor floor to hope for. The NHL and IIHF do still allow a minor (59.2, at the referee's discretion). Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 spares *minimal, accidental* stick contact with the head from **that tier**, not from a penalty — 7.6(a) still gives a minor in minor and female hockey — **but that interpretation is written to 7.6(b) and Hockey Canada writes no equivalent for Junior or Senior**, so an adult reader should not count on it. And **slew-footing**, the leg through the skates from what began as a legal battle position, is a **match penalty** in the NHL where it causes a player *"to fall violently to the ice"* (Rule 52.2, with no lesser tier written); a major plus an automatic game misconduct under the IIHF, on a *"shall, at their discretion, assess"* frame with no lesser tier written beneath it (52.2); a major plus a game misconduct as the *minimum* under USA Hockey 639(b); and a double minor rising to a match penalty under Hockey Canada (8.8(a), 8.8(c)). **And do not assume a stick foul needs contact:** the **IIHF 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** book defines a high stick as one *"carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"*, and USA Hockey's Casebook answers *"No."* to whether contact must occur (Rule 621, Situation 2), against its own Rule 621 Note, which requires it. Stick on the puck, never on the player — and your feet out of theirs.

9. **Screening the goalie is legal, and under the NHL and IIHF the goal crease is what decides it — the region, not the painted line around it.** Get in their eyes and keep your skates out of the blue paint — **and off the red line around it, which is USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's requirement rather than the NHL's or the IIHF's.** The NHL and the IIHF allow the screen planted *"on the crease line"* outright at Situation 5E below; what puts the line inside the crease is USA Hockey's Note to Rule 625(b), Hockey Canada's Glossary and IIHF Rule 1.7 — which leaves the IIHF book at odds with its own table. **Off the line is the instruction that holds under all four books**, so it is the one to play. **Every clause of Rule 69 that voids a goal for where you stood names the goal crease:** 69.1's disallowing sentence reaches an attacker who *"enters the goal crease"*, 69.3's no-contact screen ground asks for a significant position *within* it, and 69.4, the rule for outside it, reaches contact only. Clause (1) of 69.1 — *"either by his positioning or by contact"* in the NHL's words, which the IIHF writes as *"either by their positioning or by a 'relevant contact'"* — sits under *"Goals should be disallowed **only if**"*, a ceiling rather than a direction. **Both books' worked situations apply that:** NHL Reference Table 14 and IIHF 2025/26 Appendix IV Table 16 each answer *"Goal is allowed"* at situations 5C and 5E, where the screen is set outside the crease and does impair the goalie, and disallow only 5B and 5D, which both put the attacker inside it. ⚠️ **That is a reading of the rule's structure rather than something either book states in terms**, and both add that Rule 69 is *"enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s)."* Outside the crease, incidental contact is permitted **only while the goalkeeper is in the act of playing the puck**, and then only if you made a reasonable effort to avoid it (Rule 69.4) — outside that condition both books have a penalty *"assessed in every case"* of unnecessary contact, and the other two books answer it differently again: USA Hockey keys it to possession (Casebook, Rule 607, Situation 5) and Hockey Canada 8.5 writes no permission at all. ⚠️ **The other two books answer differently in both directions, so do not carry either half across.** **USA Hockey 625(b)** and **Hockey Canada 8.5** do not run 69.1's premise, and they do not agree with each other. **USA Hockey's is a flat position bar** — no goal with an attacking player in the crease, whoever scored, unless the puck preceded you in, the goalkeeper is out of the crease area, or a defender's interference is what put you there — and its clause costs you the zone on a neutral-zone draw before that, on three conditions of its own. ⚠️ **The goalkeeper-out carve-out is half a rule, and the other half is a penalty:** its Casebook, at Rule 625, Situation 9, allows the goal where the goalkeeper is *"completely out of their crease"* but disallows it, and adds an interference minor, where the goalkeeper *"is attempting to return to the goal crease"* and the attacker prevents him from making a play. **Hockey Canada's is not flat:** it allows the goal where a teammate outside the crease scores and the crease player stays completely passive, so where a teammate scores from outside the crease while you stand in it, the two books give opposite answers. Hockey Canada does keep an impairment ground, and it names vision — Interpretation 3 to 8.5(a) disallows the goal where an attacker in the crease *"attempts to play the puck, interfere with the play, or impede the goaltender's vision or movements"* — which makes it 69.3's counterpart, not 69.1's. **Neither book reaches a screen set from outside the crease**, though: USA Hockey writes no goaltender-vision provision at all, and Hockey Canada's two references to the goaltender's view both stop at the crease line — so 69.1's positioning-alone ground has no counterpart in either, and outside the paint they are the *more* permissive books. **The push-in relief inverts again:** USA Hockey's is broadest (*"any legal goal scored shall be allowed"*, no effort proviso) but reaches your position only, while **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to 8.5(a) is the one rule of the four that addresses the fouled-in attacker in terms and makes a penalty on him mandatory** for failing to try to avoid the goaltender — and directs the referee to penalise the defender who fouled him as well. ⚠️ **And Hockey Canada does not cap this rule at a minor:** 8.5(b) reaches a major plus game misconduct — an ejection — three ways: at the referee's discretion on the degree of violence of impact **with nobody hurt**, then **mandatorily** for a player who charges the goaltender, and **mandatorily** for any interference-with-goaltender foul that injures an opponent. 8.5(c) reaches a match penalty, and 8.5(d) bars a misconduct, so there is no ten-minute step between the two minutes and the ejection. That is Hockey Canada only; the other three books' tiers are not stated here. **And on the trapezoid: the only exception is the goalie keeping skate contact with the crease** (NHL Rules 1.8 for the lines, 27.8 for the penalty) — there is no teammate exception. Neither North American amateur book writes a trapezoid and CARHA Hockey's does not either, so a league running any of those three has none; **how much rec, beer-league and youth hockey that adds up to is a question about leagues rather than about books, and this document has no count for it.** In England and Wales its status is unsettled. Either way it changes your whole breakout. **But "no trapezoid" is not "no restriction", and USA Hockey is the case that proves it:** it has no trapezoid — nothing restricts where its goalkeeper may *play* the puck — yet **Rule 614(c)** makes it the same two-minute delay-of-game minor to *freeze* it, on **four independent triggers**. Two of them bite behind the net: **(3)** covering the puck outside the *"goalkeeper's privileged"* area — the Note defines it as the end-zone faceoff spots joined by an imaginary line and lines from each spot perpendicular to the end boards, so it includes the ice directly behind the net but **excludes the corners** — and **(1)**, which is broader still and bounded by no area at all: *"The puck is behind the goal line and their body is entirely outside of the goal crease."* So under USA Hockey your goalie may **fetch** a corner puck but may not **smother** it anywhere behind their own goal line. **CARHA Hockey writes the same trade, so do not read "no trapezoid" as "no limit" there either:** it has no restricted area at all, and its Rule 58(b) still makes it a minor for a goalkeeper who **deliberately** falls on or gathers the puck into their body, or holds it against the goal or the boards, *"when the puck is behind the goal line and the goalkeeper's body is entirely outside the boundaries of the goal crease area."* Never state "no trapezoid" without it — a goalie told only the first half will freeze the puck and take the penalty anyway.

10. **Find out which rule set you are playing under before your first shift.** Trapezoid, icing type, checking, tagging up, penalty length, **what a high stick costs you** — a minor at the floor in four of the five books read here, and a **major from the first contact above the shoulders** under CARHA Hockey Rule 62(b), with a **game misconduct** on top where it was intentional or where an accidental one injures, all of which binds CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — **what a hit to the head costs you**, **what a cross-check that rides up costs you**, **what touching an opponent's cage costs you**, **what taking your own helmet off in a scrum costs you** — a minor in the NHL and IIHF books, a match penalty at every age under USA Hockey, a Gross Misconduct under Hockey Canada — and **how many strides you may build up before a check** all differ between the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and your own league. ⚠️ **Under the IIHF, ask which edition as well** — the 2025/26 and 2026/27 books differ on the face-off violation and on high-sticking, and the British In-House Rules name both of them.

---

*Sources — retrieved 27 July 2026, except the In-House Rules: the 2025-26 edition 29 July 2026, the 2026-27 edition 29 August 2026:*

*Primary rulebooks: [NHL Official Rules 2025-2026 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/slwjuaqwmuvj5bkplixo.pdf) — Rules 1, 5, 10, 15–29, 31–32, 34, 38, 41–64, **74.1 and 74.3** (read 31 August 2026: 74.1's *"within five feet (5') of his players' bench and out of the play before the change is made"*, its two triggers, and its bar on leaving by *"any other exit leading from the rink"*; 74.3's same five feet for a player leaving the penalty bench) (including **48.1's avoidability factors**, which the IIHF carries as 48.1(V)–(VII) in the plural: whether the opponent *"materially changed the position of his body or head immediately prior to or simultaneously with the hit"* (iii) and whether he *"put himself in a vulnerable position by assuming a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable"* (ii) — circumstances weighed in deciding whether the head contact was avoidable, not exemptions, and neither book penalises the player who ducked), 67, 69 — including **69.4's second paragraph**, which carries both the "not fair game" restriction and the permission conditioned on the goalkeeper being *"in the act of playing the puck outside his goal crease"*, and **69.4's third paragraph**, the returning-goalkeeper protection (*"When a goalkeeper has played the puck outside of his crease and is then prevented from returning to his crease area due to the deliberate actions of an attacking player, such player may be penalized for goalkeeper interference"*) and its reciprocal, in the same paragraph, which penalises the goalkeeper who deliberately interferes from outside the crease, and **69.7 read in full for all four of its limbs** — the rebound and loose-puck permission; the disallowed goal where a goalkeeper *"has been pushed into the net together with the puck by an attacking player after making a stop"*, with *"[i]f applicable, appropriate penalties will be assessed"*; the sentence after it permitting the goal where the attacking player *"was pushed or otherwise fouled by a defending player"* causing that push; and the fourth, under which *"a goal cannot be scored by pushing this player together with the puck into the goal"* where the puck is under a player in or around the crease area, up to a penalty shot if deemed covered deliberately — 74–87, including **78.5(ix)**, cited only as a cross-reference: it states the push-in in a shorter form, dropping *"by an attacking player"* and writing *"after making a save"*, and refers back to 69.7 for the rule; and Reference Table 14 (Interference on the Goalkeeper Situations) · [IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26 (PDF)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/contentimages/3_the_iihf/2025-26_iihf_rulebook_22122025-v1.pdf) — Rules **5** (5.1, whose interpretive sentence — *"any reference to 'Player' shall refer to both skaters and goalkeepers"* — is word for word the NHL's at its own 5.1, and identical in the 2026/27 edition), 9, 10, 20, 22, 23, 24, 27, 37, 38, 40, 41, 42, 43, 44, 46, 48, 51, 52, 54, 55, **56** (56.1's Body Position, Free Hand and Stick paragraphs, read 31 August 2026 against the NHL's — the same sentences in the IIHF's pronouns and house quotation marks), 57, 58, 59, 60, 61, 62, 63 (63.2(II), the deliberate limb reaching a *"hand, leg, foot, arm, stick etc."* from anywhere on the ice and after a stoppage; 63.2(III), the strict-liability limb, which reaches only a player who shoots or bats the puck *"(using their hand or stick)"* directly out of their defending zone, carves out *"except where there is no protective glass"* where the NHL writes *"no glass"*, and assesses the penalty *"when the puck is shot over the protective glass surrounding the Players' bench"* where the NHL writes *"'behind'"*), **67** (67.2, read 31 August 2026 — the crease escalation the NHL prints as a NOTE under 67.2(ii) is written into the body of the IIHF's 67.2(II) instead), 69 — whose **69.4** carries the same returning-goalkeeper paragraph and its reciprocal in the IIHF's own wording, unchanged across the 2025/26 v1.0 and v1.1 books and the 2026/27 book, and whose **69.7** carries all four of the NHL's limbs at the same number and in the same order, differing only in its capitalised *Player*, its *"goal crease"* for the NHL's *"crease"*, quotation marks of its own around *"incidental contact"* and *"Penalty Shot"*, and its opening *"In the event"* for the NHL's *"In the event that"*, read 31 August 2026 — **78** (78.5(IX), the disallowed-goal entry for a goalkeeper pushed into the net with the puck *"after making a save"*, which refers back to 69.7), 76, 77, 81, 82 (81.4 and 82.1 read against each other and against their NHL counterparts: 81.4's exception list has four items and 82.1's, restating the same bar, three), 83, 84, 87, 101, and Appendix IV Table 16 (Interference on the Goalkeeper situations) — **that is the 2025/26 book's number; the same table is Table 14 in the 2026/27 book** · [IIHF Situation Handbook 2025/26 (Version 1.1, August 2025) (PDF)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/officiating%20files/situation%20handbook/2025_iihf_situationhandbook_17082025-v1_1.pdf) — its Foreword, Situations 57.1 to 57.4, the whole of what it publishes on tripping — all four are penalty-shot questions, and none of them reaches the "trip or fall" against "trip and fall" conflict in the rule itself — and Situations 69.1 to 69.6, the whole of what it publishes on Interference on the Goalkeeper, together with **Situation 83.34**, the airborne clearing case for a delayed off-side — all of those read in what was then the current edition, which gives its own version as **1.0, June 2025**. **Version 1.1, August 2025 is the current version of that season's book**, and it was read here for its Foreword — *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis"* — and for **Situation 63.19**, the puck kicked over the glass while blocking a shot, which v1.1 answers *"Yes."* with no rule cited where v1.0 answered *"No. Rule 63.2 (III) states that the player must have shot or batted the puck using the hand or the stick."* Also read in v1.1 for the bench: **Situation 63.24**, which still assesses the minor where a clear goes over the glass and lands in the Players' Bench, exempting only a puck *"shot directly into the Players' Bench (where there is no glass)"*; **Situation 63.28**, the glove scoop over the glass behind the players' bench, *"considered shooting or batting the puck with the hand resulting in the puck leaving the playing surface"*; and **Situation 63.3**, exempting a puck that *"clears the glass at a point in the Penalty Bench where the glass is lower than the normal height around the rink"*. Read in both v1.0 and v1.1 for two further answers on the same rule, identical in each: **Situation 63.34**, the puck kicked over the glass from the defending zone, answered *"Yes"* with no rule cited, and **Situation 63.29**, the goalkeeper's stick save that goes over the glass, answered *"No … Rule 63.2. talks about deliberate"*. The two editions were compared in full and differ substantively in four answers; **the other three are not cited by this document** — Situation 10.7 (nudging a teammate's lost stick along the ice, legal in both editions, v1.1 adding the conditions that the stick be intact and that the nudge not interfere with an opponent), Situation 20.4 (a goal now disallowed where v1.0 allowed it after video review reduced the infraction to a minor) and Situation 46.1 (the on-ice strength after a third-man-in fight). Situations 57.1 to 57.4, 69.1 to 69.6 and 83.34 are unchanged between the editions · [IHUK In-House Rules 2025-26, Revision 1 (published 27/10/25) (PDF)](https://englandicehockey.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/IHUK-In-House-Rules-25-26-R1.pdf) — Introduction and Rules 1, 37, 38, 84, 100.1, 200, Section 6 and Appendix 1; the source for the England Ice Hockey amendments above, except the trapezoid suspension, which is in the Rules & Regulations below, and except the face-off and embellishment amendments, which are in the 2026-27 edition beside it · **[IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, Revision 1 (published 25 July 2026)](https://englandicehockey.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/08/IHUK-In-House-Rules-2026-2027.pdf) — the current British document.** Read for its cover page and Introduction, for the new **Section 10 – Game Flow** amendment to Rule 76 (the immediate replacement of the centre for all violations bar those on post-icing draws, and the free choice of which centre places their stick first), for the new **Section 9** amendment to Rule 64 (*"Adding a misconduct to a minor. The required call is now 2 plus 10"*), for the new **Section 3 – Equipment** amendment to Rule 10.3 (*"Skating with a broken stick anywhere on the ice is not permitted under IHUK In-House Rules. A player whose stick is broken must drop their stick immediately.
